Montana Cannabis & Hemp Research — Audited Report, Parts 0–12

Last audited: 1 November 2026. Cut-off: 1 November 2026. No 3 November 2026 election result is included — that election had not occurred at the cut-off. This file is a research dossier, not legal advice.

How this audit was done, and its limits. This is an independent audit of a report the auditor did not write. Four independent checkers audited Cover + Parts 0–2, Parts 3–6, Parts 7–9, and Parts 10–12 + Combined Notes respectively, against primary sources only: the Montana Code Annotated and bill pages on leg.mt.gov, the Administrative Rules of Montana, the Department of Revenue Cannabis Control Division (CCD), DPHHS historic medical records, the Department of Agriculture, the Secretary of State and county election offices, Montana courts, official tribal government sources and state–tribal agreements, and official county/city codes. News, law-firm blogs, trade press, AI summaries, search excerpts and mirror sites (Cornell/Justia/LawServer) were not treated as confirmation. Where a checker did not personally open a primary source, the claim is marked Unverified even if the original report cited an official page — that is a limit of this round, not proof the report is wrong. Where checkers disagreed, the stronger primary opening wins and the reconciliation is stated below. Claims repeated identically across sections are grouped in the tables below; verdict counts are the sum of the four checkers' row counts.

173 + 25 + 1 + 187 = 386. Every Corrected and Removed claim is listed in full below, with the correction applied in place in the rewritten report that follows. Confirmed claims are listed by section with counts and key primary sources; still-unverified items follow with an exact follow-up route for each.

Federal baseline — applied as given (not re-audited)

  • Scheduling is split. Under a final rule at 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026), FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana are in Schedule III. All other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is NOT final.
  • IRS 280E: Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming). Adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E.
  • Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized. The full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026.
  • USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026.
  • No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted.

Parts 7–9 and Parts 3–6 state this baseline correctly; no contradiction was found in those chunks. Schedule III and any 280E relief are applied to medical activity only, dual-market businesses must split medical vs adult-use activity, and all adult-use activity is treated as Schedule I. Whether a tribal medical programme counts as “state-licensed” is Verify — it is not decided by the federal rule text as quoted in the report, and no conclusion is drawn on it in the rewritten report. For a combined-use licensee, any Schedule III / 280E treatment could in any event apply only to the state-licensed medical portion; the adult-use portion remains Schedule I and fully subject to §280E.

Internal contradictions resolved by this audit

  • Original green/red count: resolved to 28 green / 28 red in 2020 (November 2021 Legislative Services / EAIC paper: “Of the 28 red counties”), not 29. 28 − Granite + Dawson + Custer = 29 green today. The MTPR “29 counties that voted in favor” figure must not be used as the original count. Current split GREEN 29 / RED 27 is confirmed against the DOR September 2026 county sales table (Parts 3–6 track opened it; Parts 7–9 track had not, hence its Unverified flag is superseded for the current split, not for individual election percentages).
  • September 2026 sales: unified to adult $25,577,747 / medical $2,477,682 / total $28,055,429 everywhere, and Jan–Sept 2026 to adult $225,104,336 / medical $24,137,248 / total $249,241,584, with a $7 residual flag against the annual-sum derivation — the DOR monthly table must be re-read live before final publication. Disclosed counties sum to $27,751,064; the $304,365 gap is the five confidentiality-suppressed counties, not an error.
  • Combined-use 150-mile / tier-1 / same-premises: current MCA §16-12-225(2) text retrieved contains no tier-1, same-premises or 150-mile wording, but the CCD FAQ still states tier-1 + same premises + 150 miles and HB 903 (2023) is in that lineage, while HB 128 (2023) had said facilities do not have to be at the same premises. The rewritten report therefore states the statutory text and the FAQ position, flagged for a live MCA §16-12-225(2)/(4) check before siting — neither version is presented alone as settled law.
  • MAR 2026-155.2: resolved in favour of the MAR Notice 2026-155.2 opened in the Parts 4–5 track: effective 26 September 2026 (ARM 42.39.102/105). The Parts 0–1 track's Verify flag reflected that it had not opened the notice; final register text should still be cited.
  • SB 375 signature date: resolved to “enacted 2025; chapter assigned 8 May 2025; signature date Verify on bills.legmt.gov” — the enrolled PDF opened in the Parts 7–9 track shows blank signature-date lines, so 5 May 2025 (a passage date in secondary summaries) must not be stated as the verified signature date.
  • Missoula Ordinance 3786: ordinance text governs over council-coverage framing: finally adopted 6 July 2026, effective 30 days after passage/adoption, MMC §5.08.121(D) sunsets two years after its effective date, City page prohibition through 5 August 2028. The 1 July 2027 date in some coverage is the state moratorium end, not the ordinance sunset.
  • HB 932 / SB 307 / SB 537 / HB 952: HB 932 is Ch. 758, L. 2025, effective 1 July 2025 (signing announced 16 May 2025; the “11 June 2025” sign-date attribution is deleted). SB 307 died in process 23 May 2025 — NOT law. SB 537 and HB 952 veto/override dates and letter quotes remain Verify on leg.mt.gov / governor veto letters (secondary trackers agree on SB 537 veto 6 June 2025 / override failed 11 July 2025 and HB 952 veto 19 June 2025, but that is not primary confirmation).

Montana Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete: Parts 0–12

Last researched: 1 November 2026. All sources accessed 1 November 2026 unless a different date is stated in the source itself. This is the complete dossier: Parts 0–6 from Run 1, Parts 7–12 from Run 2, and combined research notes covering both runs. This file is a research dossier, not legal advice.

Montana has both medical and adult-use marijuana, regulated by the Department of Revenue, Cannabis Control Division (CCD), within the Cannabis and Alcohol Regulation Division (CARD). Hemp is regulated by the Montana Department of Agriculture (MDA) under MCA Title 80, Chapter 18. Montana meets biennially in odd-numbered years; the 69th Legislature (2025) is the latest completed regular session and the next regular session is in 2027. There is therefore no bill "pending" in a sitting regular session in November 2026 in the ordinary sense.

Federal baseline (given, not re-researched). State-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use remains Schedule I. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026; after 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law. Montana note: Montana has both medical and adult-use, many licensees serve both; Schedule III and 280E relief apply to medical activity only, dual businesses must split activity, and adult-use remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E. Whether any tribe operating a medical programme counts as "state-licensed" is Verify (Part 10 §10.4).

How to read flags. A fact is treated as confirmed only when the primary source itself was opened and read. Verify means the source was form-gated, rendered only through a search index, blocked by a script, obtained only via a mirror (Cornell, Justia, FindLaw, LawServer, Esper), or otherwise not opened directly — keep the flag until a direct opening resolves it. Unavailable means no primary source could be found by the route tried, which is stated so the gap can be retried rather than silently dropped. A search excerpt, dashboard summary or review-site percentage is never upgraded to a confirmed figure. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced; only published agency contacts appear.

Read before Parts 0–6 — corrections applied in this complete edition. The Parts 0–6 text below is Run 1, preserved verbatim. The following Run 1 wordings are superseded and must be read as corrected here and in the combined notes: (a) any statement presenting 29 counties as the 2020 I-190 result — the 2020 result was 28 Yes / 28 No, and 29 green / 27 red is the current count after Dawson (in, December 2021), Granite (out, June 2022) and Custer (in, November 2024); (b) any combined-use description using tier-1, same-premises or within-150-air-miles wording — current §16-12-225 (Part 10 §10.2) contains none of those words, and the CCD FAQ wording to that effect is stale; (c) any hemp citation to Title 80 Chapter 27 — the hemp chapter is Title 80 Chapter 18; (d) any statement that MAR 2026-155 was only proposed — MAR 2026-155.2 is effective 26 September 2026 (with the adoption-notice caveat stated in the notes); (e) any delivery statement beyond cardholder-only transporter delivery and the April 2022 newsletter hand-delivery exception — dispensary-own delivery remains Verify; (f) any DUI, vehicle-storage or under-21 statement that does not reflect Part 12 — Montana has a per se THC limit (§61-8-1002(1)(d), 5 ng/mL), vehicle storage is §61-8-1027 (Title 61), and the under-21 marijuana offence is §45-5-639; (g) any edible serving figure of 10 mg as current — the current serving limit is 5 mg (HB 636, from 1 July 2026); (h) any "5 oz possession" phrasing — medical possession is 1 oz, with purchase up to 1 oz per day and 5 oz per month (§16-12-515). Where Parts 0–6 and Parts 7–12 differ on any other point, the combined notes record the conflict rather than silently picking one figure.

PART 0: HEADLINES

0.1 Licence moratorium — in force, “Freeze the Footprint”

From 1 July 2025 to 30 June 2027 the Department may accept applications for and issue licences only if the application is related to an existing licensed premises; it may allow a licensee to move an existing licensed premises; it may not allow new licensed premises. On or after 1 July 2027 it shall begin accepting applications for and issuing licences to applicants who were not previously licensed and for premises not previously licensed. Source: DOR CCD FAQ, revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/faqs, accessed 1 Nov 2026; SB 27, 69th Legislature (2025), enrolled text, SB 27 enrolled PDF, which strikes “January 1, 2022, and June 30, 2025” and substitutes “July 1, 2025, and June 30, 2027”. The original HB 701 (2021) moratorium limited new licences to former medical licensees licensed or pending with DPHHS on 27 April 2021. SB 27 removes the applicant moratorium in form but replaces it with a premises moratorium to 30 June 2027. Growth in 2025–2027 is therefore by transfer or relocation of existing premises, not by new sites. Combined-use (tribal) licences are not subject to the moratorium on other licence types, per DOR/CCD (see Part 4.8). [AUDIT: this exemption is stated by DOR, not in the SB 27 enrolled text in those words.]

0.2 Green / red counties

Adult-use sales began in January 2022 only in “green” counties, where a majority voted for I-190 on 3 November 2020. In “red” counties adult-use sales are prohibited; medical sales may continue through grandfathered licensees. A red county may opt in by election; a green county may opt out by election for a category of business. Cities may vote separately from their county. Official map: CCD Local Option Map (PNG), via the CCD FAQ. The map is an image, not a text table — see Part 3.3. Dated flips verified in this run: Dawson County red → green (December 2021 special election, 55% yes); Granite County green → red (June 2022 primary, sales ended 4 September 2022; re-legalisation failed November 2022, 52–48); Custer County red → green (November 2024, 58% yes). Current split on the reconstructed table in Part 3.2: 29 green, 27 red, of 56 counties.

0.3 Tax — headline rates unchanged; distribution changed in 2025

Rates are unchanged: 20% adult-use, 4% medical, plus up to 3% county local-option tax. Source: DOR, Legal is Legit, accessed 1 Nov 2026 (“Currently, there is a 20% tax levied upon adult-use marijuana, and a 4% tax on medical marijuana”). HB 932 (2025), signed May 2025 and effective 1 July 2025, changed distribution, not rates: HEART moves from a $6 million cap to 11%, and the habitat share goes to a new Habitat Legacy Account (see Part 2.3). SB 537 (2025), the “cops and conservation” reallocation, was vetoed 6 June 2025 and the veto override failed 11 July 2025 — it is NOT law. Do not present its accounts as current law. The DOR Legal-is-Legit page still printed the old $6 million formula on 1 November 2026 and is treated here as a stale education page, not current law.

0.4 Market size and saturation — flat dollars, falling prices, adult-use about 90%

Calendar 2025 total sales were $326.9 million ($289.4 million adult-use + $37.6 million medical), up only 0.8% on 2024 ($324.3 million). Cumulative January 2022–September 2026, per DOR’s own running totals, was $1,523,162,119 (adult-use $1,260,192,343; medical $262,969,775), with estimated state taxes of $262,557,260. Source: DOR Cannabis Sales Reports, revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/sales-reports/, accessed 1 Nov 2026; annual sums cross-checked to CannBus Montana Cannabis Market Intelligence Report Q3 2026, cannbus.org/cannmir/mt_cannmir.html. DOR Retail Price Study, July 2024–June 2025 average buds $5.34 per gram (via MJBizDaily, 15 January 2026, MJBizDaily); July 2025–June 2026 average $5.14 per gram, directly read from DOR, Retail Price Studies. Secondary coverage describes the market as saturated/plateauing, with falling prices offsetting volume. Conflict flagged: MPP’s table lists Montana 2025 adult-use tax as $74,570,927, which cannot be reconciled with 20% × $289.4 million = $57.9 million; the DOR-derived figure (≈$59.4 million total for 2025) is used here and the MPP figure is flagged Unverified. Conflict flagged: secondary dispensary counts (465 locations in August 2024 per the DOR Biennial Report via CannBus; “roughly 557” and “418” in other CannBus passages; 550/558 in other secondary analyses) do not reconcile — no single current official count is adopted here; see Part 5.9.

0.5 Medical market decline — steep and continuing

Monthly medical sales fell from $10,143,750 (January 2022) to $2,609,660 (June 2026), down 74%. Annual medical sales: about $93.6 million in 2022 → $61.0 million in 2023 → $46.7 million in 2024 → $37.6 million in 2025. Cardholders: 40,522 on 1 January 2022 → 22,325 on 1 January 2023 → 14,930 in August 2024 → 13,987 on 10 February 2025 (MPP compilation, MPP patient numbers), a 65% fall from January 2022. Latest official registry count as of late 2026: Unavailable in this research — Verify against CCD registry statistics. As cardholders lapse and buy adult-use, the Schedule III / §280E-relieved share shrinks: a former patient buying adult-use pays 20% tax and the sale remains Schedule I / §280E-subject, even if the buyer has a medical condition.

0.6 Hemp-derived THC — among the strictest regimes; intoxicating hemp channel effectively closed outside dispensaries

HB 948 (2023), signed 22 May 2023, made synthetic cannabinoid products illegal to manufacture, process or offer for sale. Source: DOR, Synthetic marijuana product changes. HB 49 (2025), signed 7 April 2025, added the hemp cap in §16-12-117(3): 0.5 mg total delta-9 THC per serving, 2 mg per package for hemp/hemp products sold by any person, with unadulterated hemp flower not further processed into extracts, infused products or concentrates exempted by §16-12-117(4). SB 375 (2025), enacted 2025 (chapter assigned 8 May 2025; signature date Verify on bills.legmt.gov), provides that, unless authorised as a food or drug by the FDA, a hemp product containing total delta-9 THC may not be sold to a consumer in Montana. DOR summarises SB 375 as prohibiting the sale of any product made from hemp that contains any level of total THC.

Verify — tension to resolve: SB 375 read literally bans any total-delta-9 hemp product sale to consumers unless FDA-authorised; §16-12-117(3) read literally permits up to 0.5 mg/serving / 2 mg/package. How DOR/DPHHS reconcile the two, and which agency enforces SB 375 day to day, was not fully resolved in the sources opened. Do not present either bill alone as “the” hemp rule.
Montana response to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 (12 November / 11 December 2026): Unavailable / Verify. No Montana Department of Agriculture or DOR statement responding specifically to those dates was located as of 1 November 2026, which is expected because the dates fall after the research date and the legislature is not in regular session in 2026. Montana’s own caps are in a different unit from the federal 0.4 mg per container cap, so no direct stricter/looser comparison should be drawn without a Montana conforming amendment.

0.7 Tribal operations — combined-use licences exist in law; compact bill vetoed

Up to 8 combined-use licences, one per federally recognised tribe in Montana or majority-tribal-owned entity: one cultivator licence + one dispensary licence, in a green county and (per CCD FAQ) within 150 air miles of the reservation exterior boundary (Little Shell: tribal service area) [AUDIT NOTE: current MCA §16-12-225(2) text retrieved contains no tier-1, same-premises or 150-mile wording; the FAQ/HB 903 position must be checked against live MCA §16-12-225(2)/(4) before siting]. Source: §16-12-225, MCA; DOR CCD FAQ. HB 952 (2025), which would have removed the single-location/size/150-mile constraints and authorised tribe-by-tribe compacts, was vetoed 19 June 2025 — NOT law. Whether a veto-override poll was held or succeeded for HB 952 is Verify on leg.mt.gov. Which tribes actually hold or operate combined-use licences, and any tribal ordinances or state-tribal agreements, are Unavailable in Run 1 — reserved for Part 10 in Run 2, from official tribal and government sources only. August 2024 biennial-report figures reported secondarily put combined-use licences at 0.
