PART 1: LAWS

1.1 Legal status — what is legal for adults

Both medical and adult-use are legal in Montana, with different age, purchase and tax rules. Medical: since I-148 (2004), as recodified by HB 701 (2021) into Title 16, Chapter 12, Part 5. Adult-use: possession and use legal from 1 January 2021 (I-190); licensed adult-use sales from 1 January 2022 (HB 701). DOR CCD FAQ, accessed 1 Nov 2026: “As of January 1, 2021, adults 21 and over may possess and use up to one ounce of marijuana with no criminal penalties.” The FAQ also states “As of January 1, 2022, medical and adult use marijuana is legal to purchase in the state of Montana by individuals 21 and over” — that second sentence conflates medical purchase age; medical cardholders may be under 21 (see §1.4). Constitutional age: CI-118 (2020) set 21 as the adult-use age in the Montana Constitution — Verify CI-118 article/section citation in Part 9 (Run 2).

Adults 21+ may: possess, purchase, obtain, use, ingest, inhale or transport ≤1 oz usable marijuana (sub-limits below); transfer ≤1 oz without consideration to another 21+ adult or registered cardholder; cultivate at a private residence within the plant limits; possess paraphernalia (sale/transfer to 18+ for paraphernalia under §16-12-106(1)(e)). What is not legal: public consumption (civil fine ≤$50 for smoking in public, §16-12-106(4)); consumption in a public place except as allowed by the Department, on public transport, on school / health-care / correctional grounds, where tobacco smoking is prohibited, or in a non-smoking hotel/motel room (§16-12-108(1)(h)–(k)); operating a vehicle while under the influence (§16-12-108(1)(a)); synthetic cannabinoid products (§16-12-108(1)(d), §16-12-117(1)); sale to under-21s except registered cardholders; possession above the limits; unlicensed sale; taking marijuana across state lines or onto federal land (federal law — Part 10, Run 2).

1.2 Every statute and regulation — citation, summary, verbatim operative text, last-amended date, official link

How to read the history lines: “En. Sec. __, I.M. No. 190” = enacted by Initiative Measure 190 (2020). “Ch. 576, L. 2021” = HB 701 (2021). Where a chapter number for a 2025 bill is not shown in the MCA history line retrieved, it is given as bill number + session only and flagged Verify for chapter. Official MCA index pattern: leg.mt.gov — MCA Title 16, Chapter 12. All MCA 2025 pages cited below were accessed 1 Nov 2026. Where a section was not opened in full in this run, that is stated — do not treat a summary below as a substitute for the official text for a filing.

Montana Marijuana Regulation and Taxation Act — MCA Title 16, Chapter 12

§16-12-101 — Short title; purpose. Names the Act; purposes include legal possession for 21+, licensure/regulation, eliminating the illicit market, preventing synthetic cannabinoid manufacture/distribution, preventing distribution to under-21s, local-government authority, and taxation. Subsection (3): marijuana is not an agricultural product and its cultivation/processing/manufacture/sale is not agriculture subject to Department of Agriculture regulation unless expressly provided. Verbatim: “(1) This chapter may be cited as the ‘Montana Marijuana Regulation and Taxation Act’.” / “(3) Marijuana and marijuana products are not agricultural products, and the cultivation, processing, manufacturing or selling of marijuana or marijuana products is not considered agriculture subject to regulation by the department of agriculture unless expressly provided.” Last amended: En. Sec. 1, I.M. No. 190, approved Nov. 3, 2020; amd. Sec. 37, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 4, Ch. 746, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 1, Ch. 100, L. 2025. Link: §16-12-101.

§16-12-102 — Definitions (key definitions only; full section has 42 definitions). Decisive definitions: “marijuana” (excludes hemp per §80-18-101, excludes synthetic cannabinoid products, excludes FDA-approved drugs); “debilitating medical condition” (closed list, §1.4); “synthetic cannabinoid” (artificial production, chemical synthesis or biosynthesis with recombinant agents incl. yeast/algae); “registered cardholder” (Montana resident with a debilitating condition + valid card); “resident” (meets §1-1-215, and not a person claiming residence elsewhere or an absentee property owner paying Montana property tax); “canopy”; “financial interest” (≥5% of net profits/net worth, including holders of private loans or convertible securities — SB 74, 2025 — and excluding bank/licensed-lender interests and security interests/liens); “controlling beneficial owner” (≥5% owner’s interest, affiliate control, or position to control; qualified institutional investor >15%). Verbatim excerpts: (10) “‘Debilitating medical condition’ means: (a) cancer, glaucoma, positive status for human immunodeficiency virus, or acquired immune deficiency syndrome when the condition or disease results in symptoms that seriously and adversely affect the patient’s health status; (b) cachexia or wasting syndrome; (c) severe chronic pain that is a persistent pain of severe intensity that significantly interferes with daily activities as documented by the patient’s treating physician; (d) intractable nausea or vomiting; (e) epilepsy or an intractable seizure disorder; (f) multiple sclerosis; (g) Crohn’s disease; (h) painful peripheral neuropathy; (i) a central nervous system disorder resulting in chronic, painful spasticity or muscle spasms; (j) admittance into hospice care in accordance with rules adopted by the department; or (k) posttraumatic stress disorder.” (20) “‘Marijuana’ means all plant material from the genus Cannabis containing tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) or seeds of the genus capable of germination. (b) The term does not include hemp as provided in 80-18-101. (c) The term does not include synthetic cannabinoid products. (d) The term does not include any drug approved by the United States food and drug administration pursuant to section 505 of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, 21 U.S.C. 301, et seq.” (39) “‘Synthetic cannabinoid’ has the meaning described in 50-32-222 and includes any cannabinoid produced artificially, whether from chemical synthesis or biosynthesis using recombinant biological agents, including but not limited to yeast and algae.” (35) “‘Registered cardholder’ or ‘cardholder’ means a Montana resident with a debilitating medical condition who has received and maintains a valid registry identification card.” Last amended: En. Sec. 2, I.M. No. 190 (2020); amd. Sec. 38, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 1, Ch. 743, L. 2023, Sec. 12, Ch. 743, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 746, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 2, Ch. 100, L. 2025; amd. Sec. 2, Ch. 550, L. 2025 (SB 74). Link: §16-12-102.

§§16-12-103 / 16-12-104 — Department responsibilities; licensure and moratorium. DOR (through CARD/CCD) licenses and regulates cultivation, manufacture, transport and sale, administers/enforces the Act, and maintains registries of licensees and cardholders. Licence types: cultivator, manufacturer, adult-use dispensary, medical marijuana dispensary, testing laboratory, marijuana transporter, combined-use marijuana licence, plus other types/subtypes/endorsements the Department considers necessary. A licensee may not cultivate hemp or engage in hemp manufacturing at a licensed premises. Moratorium (as amended by SB 27, 2025): during 1 July 2025–30 June 2027 the Department “(i) may accept applications from and issue licenses to persons qualified for licensure under this chapter only if the application is related to an existing licensed premises; (ii) may allow a licensee to move an existing licensed premises; and (iii) may not allow for new licensed premises”; and “On or after July 1, 2027, the department shall begin accepting applications for and issuing licenses to cultivate, manufacture, or sell marijuana or marijuana products to applicants who were not previously licensed and for premises not previously licensed” (DOR FAQ formulation, same substance). Full current §16-12-104 MCA 2025 text: Verify — the MCA 2025 page for §16-12-104 was not opened in full in this run; the moratorium dates are confirmed by both SB 27 enrolled text and DOR FAQ.

§16-12-105 — Seed-to-sale tracking (see §1.7). Department must implement a tracking system; licensees must use it as primary inventory/record system (ARM 42.39.203). Full §16-12-105 verbatim text: Verify — not opened in full.

§16-12-106 — Personal use and cultivation; penalties (full verbatim in §1.3). Last amended: En. Sec. 8, I.M. No. 190 (2020); amd. Sec. 1, Ch. 312, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 41, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 712, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 6, Ch. 155, L. 2025. Link: §16-12-106.

§16-12-108 — Limitations of act (full verbatim in §1.3). Last amended: En. Sec. 16, I.M. No. 190 (2020); amd. Sec. 43, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 6, Ch. 712, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 6, Ch. 746, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 3, Ch. 100, L. 2025 (HB 49).

§16-12-111 — Marijuana state special revenue account; distribution. Dedicated account; after a 3-month operating reserve, at fiscal year-end: 11% to HEART (§16-12-122); then net balance: 20% habitat legacy account (§87-5-811), 4% state park account (§23-1-105(1)), 4% trails/recreational facilities (§23-2-108), 4% nongame wildlife (§87-5-121), 3% or $200,000 whichever is less to veterans/surviving spouses (§10-2-108), $150,000 to Board of Crime Control for crisis intervention team training (§44-7-110) — remainder to general fund. [AUDIT: the $150,000 item DOES survive as §16-12-111(4)(b)(vi).] Verbatim: “(4) At the end of each fiscal year, the department shall transfer funds in excess of a 3-month operating reserve necessary to fund operating costs at the beginning of the next fiscal year in the following order: (a) 11% must be transferred to the healing and ending addiction through recovery and treatment (HEART) account established in 16-12-122; (b) the net balance remaining after distribution to the HEART account must be distributed as follows: (i) 20% to the habitat legacy account provided for in 87-5-811; (ii) 4% to the state park account established in 23-1-105(1); … (vii) the remainder to the general fund.” Last amended: En. Sec. 35, I.M. No. 190 (2020); amd. Sec. 46, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 758, L. 2025. Note: whether Ch. 758 is HB 932 or another 2025 bill is Verify before attributing it in Part 2. Prefer the official leg.mt.gov MCA 2025 page for §16-12-111 over any mirror copy.

§16-12-112 — Rulemaking authority; fees. Department may adopt rules including licensing, testing, tracking, labelling/packaging standards (pharmacologically active ingredients incl. THC/CBD, mg per serving, servings per package, quantity limits per sale), THC potency limits if necessary, fees for testing labs, background checks, employee certification, transporter licences, worker permits, and other fees. Amended by HB 792 (2025) to add labelling/education rulemaking. Full verbatim: Verify — §16-12-112 was not opened in full.

§16-12-117 — Synthetic cannabinoid products prohibited; restriction on sale; hemp caps (complete, MCA 2025). Verbatim: “(1) A person may not manufacture, process, store, or offer for sale a synthetic cannabinoid or a synthetic cannabinoid product. (2) Marijuana and marijuana products may only be sold by a manufacturer licensed under 16-12-222 [AUDIT NOTE: §16-12-117(2) as retrieved cites “manufacturer licensed under 16-12-222”, an uncorrected cross-reference — manufacturer is §16-12-221 and transporter is §16-12-222 per the Part 2 index; do not cite §16-12-117(2) as the manufacturer-licensing authority without this note], a cultivator licensed under 16-12-223, or a dispensary licensed under 16-12-224 unless the products are authorized as a drug by the United States food and drug administration. Products under this section may not exceed the potency levels established in 16-12-224. (3) Hemp and hemp products sold by any person may not exceed 0.5 milligrams of total delta-9 THC for each serving and may not exceed 2 milligrams of total delta-9 THC per package. (4) This section does not apply to unadulterated hemp flower that is not further processed into extracts, infused products, or concentrates.” Last amended: En. Sec. 1, Ch. 746, L. 2023 (HB 948); amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 100, L. 2025 (HB 49). Link: §16-12-117.

Part 2 — Licensing (general). §16-12-201 (licensing generally); §16-12-203 (application / residency / background); §16-12-207 (licensing as privilege, criteria and 500-foot rule); §16-12-208 (manufacturer / product rules); §16-12-210 (licence categories/renewal, as amended by SB 27); §16-12-211 (limitations on advertising / rulemaking); §16-12-215 (labelling / education posters, as amended by HB 792); §16-12-221 (manufacturer fees, as amended by SB 74); §16-12-222 (manufacturer licensing — note: in the current MCA numbering the transporter is §16-12-222 in some retrieved texts and the manufacturer is §16-12-221; the Parts 4–5 track cites manufacturer as §16-12-221 and transporter as §16-12-222 — use the MCA Part 2 index to confirm numbering before a filing); §16-12-223 (cultivator licensing, canopy tiers); §16-12-224 (dispensary licensing); §16-12-225 (combined-use licensing); §16-12-226 (worker permits). §16-12-207 verbatim (operative, 500-foot rule): “(3)(a) The department shall deny a cultivator license, manufacturer license, adult-use dispensary license, medical marijuana license, or testing laboratory license if the applicant’s proposed licensed premises: (i) is situated within a zone of a locality where an activity related to the use of marijuana conflicts with an ordinance, a certified copy of which has been filed with the department; (ii) is not approved by local building, health, or fire officials as provided for in this chapter; or (iii) is within 500 feet of and on the same street as a building used exclusively as a church, synagogue, or other place of worship, as a school or postsecondary school other than a commercially operated school, or as a child-care facility licensed or registered by the department of public health and human services, unless the locality requires a greater distance. This distance must be measured in a straight line from the center of the nearest entrance of the place of worship or school to the nearest entrance of the licensee’s premises. This subsection (3)(a)(iii) does not apply if the application is for license renewal and the licensed premises was established before the church, synagogue, or other place of worship or school or postsecondary school or child-care facility existed on the same street.” “(b) For the purposes of this subsection (3), ‘school’ includes public and private preschools.” “(c) The provisions of subsection (3)(a)(iii) apply to new license applications submitted on or after January 1, 2024.” Verify against official MCA 2025 §16-12-207 page in Part 4 — the text above was retrieved via secondary mirrors. ARM 42.39.124 implements the three-part test (same street + same-street address + ≤500 ft straight-line entrance-to-entrance). Note: the 500-foot rule is a licensed-premises rule. It is not a home-grow rule. §16-12-224 fee verbatim: “(6)(b) The dispensary license fee is $5,000 for the first location that a licensee operates as an adult-use dispensary or a medical marijuana dispensary. The dispensary license fee increases cumulatively by $5,000 for each additional location under the same license.” §16-12-225 combined-use: department may issue a total of eight combined-use licences to a federally recognised tribe located in the state or a majority-owned business entity; each consists of one cultivator licence and one dispensary licence; initial and annual fee $7,500; operate in a county satisfying §16-12-301 and (per CCD FAQ) within 150 air miles of the reservation exterior boundary (Little Shell: tribal service area) [AUDIT NOTE: current MCA §16-12-225(2) text retrieved contains no tier-1, same-premises or 150-mile wording; the FAQ/HB 903 position must be checked against live MCA §16-12-225(2)/(4) before siting] — Verify full current §16-12-225 text; the 1,000 sq ft figure cited in secondary HB 952 coverage is NOT in the §16-12-225 text retrieved and must not be adopted without checking current §16-12-225.

Part 3 — Local government. §16-12-301 (local government authority to regulate; opt-in requirement; exemption for existing licensees). Operative structure: (1)(a) a marijuana business may not operate in a county where a majority voted against I-190 until (i) the category/categories it seeks have been approved by the local jurisdiction under (3)/(4) and (ii) it is licensed; (1)(b) former medical licensee not applying as adult-use dispensary may operate in existing premises notwithstanding local inaction; (1)(e) businesses in green counties are not subject to the local approval process in (3)–(6); (2)(a) local government may by ordinance regulate a marijuana business to protect public health/safety/welfare, including inspections; (3) election on approval of categories may be requested by petition (Title 7, Ch. 5, Pt. 13) or governing-body resolution. Full current §16-12-301 MCA 2025 verbatim: Verify — not opened in full in this run. §§16-12-309 to 16-12-312, 16-12-317 — Local-option tax. §16-12-309 verbatim: “As required by 7-1-112, 16-12-309 through 16-12-312, and 16-12-317 specifically delegate to the qualified electors of a county the power to authorize their county to impose a local-option marijuana excise tax within the corporate boundary of the county.” History: En. Sec. 94, Ch. 576, L. 2021. §16-12-311 (2021 text): tax question must state rate (≤3% of retail value), effective date (≥90 days after election), and purposes; election by petition or governing-body resolution; question may not be placed before electors more than once per fiscal year. Current §16-12-311 text: Verify.

Part 5 — Medical marijuana. §§16-12-501 to 16-12-515 (registry, written certification, card issuance, protections, allowable amounts). Formerly Title 50, Chapter 46 (Montana Medical Marijuana Act, I-148 2004 / SB 423 2011 / I-182 2016), recodified into Title 16, Chapter 12, Part 5 by HB 701 (2021). Full Part 5 verbatim texts: Verify — only §16-12-515(1) was retrieved in full (see §1.4); §§16-12-501–514 must be opened from MCA 2025.

Title 15, Chapter 64, Part 1 — Marijuana taxes. §15-64-101 (definitions, incl. “retail price” as amended by SB 74 to be price after discounts/promotions — secondary summary, Verify text); §15-64-102 (tax on marijuana sales — 20% adult-use / 4% medical, per DOR); §§15-64-103, 15-64-104 (collection / penalties; violation can lead to licence revocation under §16-12-108(7)). Full texts: Verify — not opened in full in this run; rates are confirmed by DOR FAQ, county sales tables, and multiple secondary sources, but the task requires the official §15-64-102 verbatim in Part 2.

Dangerous drugs — Title 45, Chapter 9; Title 50, Chapter 32. §45-9-101 et seq. (criminal distribution / possession of dangerous drugs): amounts above Chapter 12 limits remain criminal under Title 45, Chapter 9 — §16-12-108(8): “Unless specifically exempted by this chapter, the provisions of Title 45, chapter 9, apply to the conduct of consumers, licensees, and registered cardholders.” HB 701 amended Title 45 provisions to reflect that Chapter 12 amounts are no longer criminal (DOJ 2021 Legislative Update, DOJ update: “Amounts in excess of the permissible amounts are charged under Title 45, Chapter 9, as modified”). §50-32-222 — Schedule I dangerous drugs (tetrahydrocannabinols): lists tetrahydrocannabinols, including synthetic equivalents, derivatives and isomers, expressly including delta-1 (delta-9) and delta-8 (delta-6) THC and optical isomers (secondary summaries). Full current §50-32-222 verbatim: Verify — not opened from leg.mt.gov in this run. The Schedule I listing is the legal basis, alongside §16-12-117, for treating converted delta-8 as prohibited even when hemp-derived, subject to the hemp exclusion / Chapter 12 authorisation — the interaction between §50-32-222 and the hemp definition is a known tension and should be flagged as such, not smoothed over. §45-5-624 / §45-5-639 (under-21 / under-18 penalties): §16-12-106(5) cross-refers under-21 non-cardholder possession/use/delivery to §45-5-639 (MCA 2025 text as opened); older summaries cite §45-5-624 — Verify which section is current.

I-182 (2016), I-190 (2020), CI-118 (2020), HB 701 (2021) — lineage. I-148 (2004): original medical marijuana initiative (former Title 50, Ch. 46). SB 423 (2011): restrictive amendments and litigation — Part 9 (Run 2); not current law in its 2011 form. I-182 (2016): voter-approved loosening of SB 423 restrictions (removed the 3-patient provider limit, allowed dispensaries); its provisions were carried into former Title 50, Ch. 46 and then recodified by HB 701. I-182 is not a freestanding current code chapter — do not cite I-182 as current law without tracing it to its current MCA section. Verify I-182 section-by-section mapping in Part 9. I-190 (2020) + CI-118 (2020): I-190 created the Act (approved 3 Nov 2020); CI-118 set the constitutional age at 21. Possession/use legal 1 Jan 2021; sales 1 Jan 2022 under HB 701. HB 701 (2021), Ch. 576, L. 2021, signed 18 May 2021: implementation act — transferred medical programme from DPHHS to DOR; created separate cultivator / manufacturer / dispensary / transporter / testing-lab licences + combined-use licences + worker permits; created the local-option tax and the §16-12-301 county approval system; set the original moratorium (former medical licensees only to 30 June 2023, later extended); reduced home-grow from I-190’s 4+4 to 2+2 for adult-use (medical 4+4 retained); set flower 35%, edibles originally 100 mg/package / 10 mg/serving; set taxes 20% adult-use / 4% medical (medical rate history: I-190/HB 701 debates cite 5% at one stage — Verify: current medical rate is 4% per DOR; the 5% figure in some 2021 summaries is superseded); created the expungement/resentencing process (Part 12, Run 2). Later amendments (2023): HB 948 (Ch. 746) — synthetic ban, §16-12-117 created; HB 903 (2023) — combined-use tier increases, preschool distance, dispensary fee structure; Ch. 712, 714, 743 — various 2023 amendments. Each 2023 chapter’s subject: Verify on leg.mt.gov bill pages before attributing in Part 9. Later amendments (2025): SB 27, HB 49 (Ch. 100), SB 74 (Ch. 550 in §16-12-102 history), SB 375 (Title 50, Ch. 31, Pt. 2), HB 636 (Ch. 500 in §16-12-224 history), HB 792 (labelling/education), HB 932 (distribution — Verify chapter), plus Ch. 350 and Ch. 155 amendments to §§16-12-224 and 16-12-106 respectively (subjects Verify).

Administrative Rules of Montana — Title 42, Chapter 39 (DOR marijuana rules). Official ARM gateway: rules.mt.gov. Key rules (each Verify current text on rules.mt.gov before relying for compliance, as ARM pages retrieved were via Cornell / DOR notices, not the live rules.mt.gov text for every rule): ARM 42.39.102 (definitions, incl. “green county” / “red county”; “day-to-day operational control” proposed 2026, MAR 2026-155.1); ARM 42.39.105 (licensure / day-to-day control residency proof, proposed 2026); ARM 42.39.122 (licensed premises; mandatory signage, as amended eff. 22 Aug 2026, MAR 2026-93.2); ARM 42.39.123 (advertising); ARM 42.39.124 (determination of proximity to place of worship or school); ARM 42.39.203 (seed-to-sale tracking and reconciliation, as amended eff. 22 Aug 2026, MAR 2026-445.2); ARM 42.39.314 (general labelling); ARM 42.39.315–42.39.320 (packaging/labelling sub-rules, as amended eff. 22 Aug 2026); ARM 42.39.319 (exit packaging / QR code, as amended 2026); ARM 42.39.312 (recalls, as amended eff. 11 July 2026); ARM 42.39.413 (delivery — medical delivery allowed per secondary summaries citing this rule — Verify whether adult-use delivery is allowed); ARM 42.39.415 (combined-use licences). Former ARM Title 37, Chapter 107 (DPHHS medical rules): historic medical registry rules under DPHHS before the HB 701 transfer. Current status: superseded / recodified — Verify whether any ARM 37.107 rules remain in force or were repealed on transfer. Do not cite ARM 37.107 as current CCD rules without that check. The live CCD rules are ARM 42.39, not 37.107.

Hemp statutes and rules — MCA Title 80, Chapter 18; ARM Title 4, Chapter 19. §80-18-101 (definitions); §80-18-102 (hemp authorised as agricultural crop); §80-18-103 (hemp licensing); §§80-18-104 to 80-18-111 (testing, enforcement). Full chapter text: MDA booklet (2019, superseded in part) and MCA Title 80, Chapter 18 index on leg.mt.gov (Verify current §§80-18-101–111 MCA 2025 pages in Part 7, Run 2). §80-12-101 is not the hemp definition — the hemp definition is §80-18-101(1)(a), verbatim via mirror: “‘Hemp’ means the plant species Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry weight basis. (b) The term does not include synthetic cannabinoids.” MDA official hemp pages: MDA Hemp Questions — “Hemp is defined in federal and Montana statute as Cannabis sativa L. that contains no greater than 0.3% delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC). Hemp is authorized as an alternative agricultural crop by the Montana Legislature, Section 80-18-101 through 80-18-111 of Montana Code Annotated.” ARM Title 4, Chapter 19 — Hemp (MDA): definitions, licensing categories, testing. Current ARM 4.19 text and USDA-approved state plan status: Verify in Part 7 — the 2019 booklet is stale and must not be quoted as current rules without checking rules.mt.gov. SB 375 codification: SB 375 §2 states it is intended to be codified as an integral part of Title 50, chapter 31, part 2, and the provisions of Title 50, chapter 31, part 2, apply to it. So the hemp-THC sale ban sits in Title 50 (Health), enforced alongside DPHHS/FDA food/drug law — not in Title 16 or Title 80. Which agency enforces SB 375 day to day (DPHHS vs DOR vs MDA): Verify — secondary summaries split on this.

1.3 Possession, home grow, public consumption, vehicles, product types and THC limits

Possession — adults 21+ (§16-12-106(1)(a)–(b), verbatim)

“(1) Subject to the limitations in 16-12-108, the following acts are lawful and may not be an offense under state law or the laws of any local government within the state, be a basis to impose a civil fine, penalty, or sanction, or be a basis to detain, search, or arrest, or otherwise deny any right or privilege, or to seize or forfeit assets under state law or the laws of any local government for a person who is 21 years of age or older or a registered cardholder: (a) possessing, purchasing, obtaining, using, ingesting, inhaling, or transporting 1 ounce or less of usable marijuana, except that not more than 8 grams may be in a concentrated form and not more than 800 milligrams of THC may be in edible marijuana products meant to be eaten or swallowed in solid form; (b) transferring, delivering, or distributing without consideration, to a person who is 21 years of age or older or a registered cardholder, 1 ounce or less of usable marijuana, except that not more than 8 grams may be in a concentrated form and not more than 800 milligrams of THC may be in edible marijuana products meant to be eaten or swallowed in solid form;”

Plain English: The 1 oz is a total possession cap, not 1 oz flower plus 8 g concentrate plus 800 mg edibles. Within that 1 oz, concentrate is sub-capped at 8 g and solid edibles at 800 mg THC. “Usable marijuana” = dried leaves and flowers appropriate for use; excludes seeds, stalks, roots (§16-12-102(42)). Note on edibles sub-limit vs package limit: the 800 mg figure in §16-12-106 is a possession sub-limit for solid edibles. The package limit for adult-use edibles sold at dispensaries is 100 mg (§16-12-224, below). A consumer could lawfully possess up to 800 mg THC in solid edibles (for example eight 100 mg packages) within the 1 oz overall cap — Verify how DOR/CCD counts edible weight toward the 1 oz cap in practice (by THC mg vs by product weight), as §16-12-224(8)(a) says dispensary sales are regulated by THC concentration, not weight.

Home grow (§16-12-106(1)(c), verbatim)

“(c) in or on the grounds of a private residence, possessing, planting, or cultivating up to two mature marijuana plants and two seedlings, or four mature marijuana plants and four seedlings for a registered cardholder, and possessing, harvesting, drying, processing, or manufacturing the marijuana, provided that: (i) marijuana plants and any marijuana produced by the plants in excess of 1 ounce must be kept in a locked space in or on the grounds of one private residence and may not be visible by normal, unaided vision from a public place; (ii) not more than twice the number of marijuana plants permitted under this subsection (1)(c) may be cultivated in or on the grounds of a single private residence simultaneously; (iii) a person growing or storing marijuana plants under this subsection (1)(c) must own the private residence where the plants are cultivated and stored or obtain written permission to cultivate and store marijuana from the owner of the private residence; and (iv) no portion of a private residence used for cultivation of marijuana and manufacture of marijuana products for personal use may be shared with, rented, or leased to a marijuana business;”

Plain English / residence caps: Adult: 2 mature + 2 seedlings per person; 4 mature + 4 seedlings per residence maximum, however many adults live there. Cardholder: 4 + 4 per person; 8 + 8 per residence maximum on the same “twice the number” rule (for a mixed adult + cardholder household, the exact combined cap is Verify; do not assume 8+8 applies if only one resident is a cardholder). Definitions: “mature marijuana plant” = harvestable plant (§16-12-102(26)); “seedling” = no flowers, <12 inches height and <12 inches diameter (§16-12-102(38)). Harvest above 1 oz may be kept at home only in a locked space, not visible from a public place. Grower must own or have the owner’s written permission. No part of a home-grow residence may be shared/rented/leased to a marijuana business. Distance-from-dispensary rule for home grow: NONE FOUND. No provision in §16-12-106 links home grow to distance from a dispensary. The only distance rule located is the 500-foot same-street rule for licensed premises (§16-12-207(3)(a)(iii)). Any claim of a home-grow-to-dispensary buffer should be treated as Verify / likely false unless a local ordinance is produced. Timing note (secondary): adult-use home cultivation was delayed by HB 701 until 1 July 2023 in secondary summaries — Verify effective date in HB 701 / §16-12-106 history; the MCA 2025 text itself does not state a 2023 start date in the excerpt opened. Penalties for home-grow violations (§16-12-106(2)–(4)): visible from public place: civil fine ≤$250 + forfeiture. Outside a locked space: civil fine ≤$250 + forfeiture. Smoking in public (other than a Department-licensed area): civil fine ≤$50. 1–2 oz tier (§16-12-106(7)): possession / production / delivery without consideration / possession with intent to deliver more than 1 oz but less than 2 oz (or >8 g but <16 g concentrate), unless permitted under Part 5 (medical): forfeiture + (a) first violation: choice of civil fine ≤$200 or ≤4 hours community service; (b) second: ≤$300 or ≤6 hours; (c) third+: ≤$500 or ≤8 hours. Other protections (§16-12-106(8)–(9)): may not be denied adoption/custody/visitation solely for Chapter 12-permitted conduct; may not be denied organ transplant / health care solely for permitted conduct. Under-21 / under-18: §16-12-106(5): under-21 non-cardholder possession/use/delivery without consideration punishable per §45-5-639. §16-12-106(6): under-18 non-cardholder paraphernalia possession/use/transport/delivery: forfeiture + 8 hours drug education/counselling.

Public consumption, vehicles, other limitations (§16-12-108, verbatim operative list)

“(1) This chapter does not permit: (a) any individual to operate, navigate, or be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, train, aircraft, motorboat, or other motorized form of transport while under the influence of marijuana or marijuana products; (b) consumption of marijuana or marijuana products while operating or being in physical control of a motor vehicle, train, aircraft, motorboat, or other motorized form of transport while it is being operated; (c) smoking or consuming marijuana while riding in the passenger seat within an enclosed compartment of a motor vehicle, train, aircraft, motorboat, or other motorized form of transport while it is being operated; (d) production, delivery, distribution, purchase, or consumption of synthetic cannabinoid products; (e) delivery or distribution of marijuana or marijuana products, with or without consideration, to a person under 21 years of age, unless the person is a registered cardholder; (f) purchase, consumption, or use of marijuana or marijuana products by a person under 21 years of age, unless the person is a registered cardholder; (g) possession or transport of marijuana or marijuana products by a person under 21 years of age unless the underage person is a registered cardholder or is at least 18 years of age and is an employee of a marijuana business licensed under this chapter and engaged in work activities; (h) possession or consumption of marijuana or marijuana products or possession of marijuana paraphernalia: (i) on the grounds of any property owned or leased by a school district, a public or private preschool, school, or postsecondary school as defined in 20-5-402; (ii) in a school bus; (iii) in a health care facility as defined in 50-5-101; or (iv) on the grounds of any correctional facility; (i) using marijuana or marijuana products in a location where smoking tobacco is prohibited; (j) smoking marijuana in a hotel or motel room, except for a hotel or motel room that is designated as a smoking room and rented to a guest; (k) consumption of marijuana or marijuana products: (i) in a public place, except as allowed by the department; or (ii) on trains, buses, or other forms of public transportation. (l) conduct that endangers others; (m) undertaking any task while under the influence of marijuana or marijuana products if doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice; or (n) performing solvent-based extractions on marijuana using solvents other than water, glycerin, propylene glycol, vegetable oil, or food-grade ethanol unless licensed for this activity by the department.” “(3) A person may not cultivate marijuana in a manner that is visible from the street or other public area.”

Vehicle storage / open-container analogue (audit correction): DOR Know Before You Go states purchases must remain in unopened, original packaging; unlawful possession in a motor vehicle is fined up to $100, is not a criminal offence, and is not recorded on the driver’s record. The MCA section number for the vehicle-storage provision remains Verify until MCA Title 61 is opened (Cover cites §61-8-1027; Part 12 confirms §61-8-1027 in full). What is established: consumption in/while operating a vehicle is prohibited, and driving under the influence is prohibited (see §1.8 / Part 12 context: 5 ng/mL whole-blood delta-9 THC per se standard, secondary sources — Verify current §61-8-1002 / §61-8-401 text). No on-site consumption at dispensaries as a general rule: ARM 42.39.122 signage (2026) includes “No on-site consumption of marijuana or marijuana products” (MAR 2026-93.2). The §16-12-108(1)(k)(i) exception (“except as allowed by the department”) and §16-12-106(4) exception (“other than in an area licensed for that activity by the department”) imply a Department-licensed consumption area is legally possible, but no general licensed-consumption programme was located — Verify whether any such area licences exist.

Product types and THC limits — dispensary sales (§16-12-224(8), verbatim, MCA 2025)

“(8)(a) Marijuana and marijuana products sold at a dispensary are regulated and sold on the basis of the concentration of THC in the products and not by weight. (b) Except as provided in subsection (8)(d), for purposes of this chapter, a single package is limited to: (i) for marijuana sold as flower, 1 ounce of usable marijuana. The total potential psychoactive THC of marijuana flower may not exceed 35%. (ii) for a marijuana product sold as a capsule, no more than 100 milligrams of THC per capsule and no more than 800 milligrams of THC per package. (iii) for a marijuana product sold as a tincture, no more than 800 milligrams of THC; (iv) for a marijuana product sold as an edible or a food product, no more than 100 milligrams of THC a package. A single serving of an edible marijuana product may not exceed 5 milligrams of THC. (v) for a marijuana product sold as a topical product, a concentration of no more than 6% THC and no more than 800 milligrams of THC per package; (vi) for a marijuana product sold as a suppository or transdermal patch, no more than 100 milligrams of THC per suppository or transdermal patch and no more than 800 milligrams of THC per package; and (vii) for any other marijuana product, no more than 800 milligrams of THC. (c) There may be a deviation of 10% above or below the allowed amount under subsections (8)(b)(ii) through (8)(b)(vii). (d) A dispensary may sell marijuana or marijuana products having higher THC potency levels than described in subsection (8) to registered cardholders.”

History / effective dates: En. Sec. 5, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 2, Ch. 714, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 743, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 350, L. 2025; amd. Sec. 1, Ch. 500, L. 2025 (HB 636 — edible serving 10 mg → 5 mg); amd. Sec. 7, Ch. 550, L. 2025 (SB 74 — 10% deviation for (ii)–(vii)). The 5 mg serving limit therefore applies from 1 July 2026 (HB 636 delayed effective date, confirmed by DOR FAQ: “When licensees are able to operate, edible adult-use marijuana products may contain up to 5 mg of THC per serving, and up to 100 mg of THC in an entire package” and “The total psychoactive THC of marijuana flower may not exceed 35%”). Do not quote the old 10 mg serving limit as current law — it is superseded for sales on/after 1 July 2026; whether pre-1 July 2026 stock could still be sold after that date is Verify (sell-through / grace period, if any, in ARM or DOR guidance). Allowed product types (from §16-12-102(24) + §16-12-224): flower, edibles/food products (including beverages — DOR FAQ expressly includes beverages in the edible/food category: “For a marijuana product sold as an edible or a food product (including beverages), no more than 100 milligrams of THC” / 5 mg per serving), vapes (concentrates intended for smoking/vaping are within “marijuana product” / “marijuana concentrate”), concentrates/extracts (800 mg/package under (vii), subject to the 8 g possession sub-limit for consumers), capsules, tinctures, topicals (6% / 800 mg), suppositories/transdermal patches, live plants (dispensaries may sell live plants — §16-12-224(4)–(5)). Beverage-specific THC limits beyond the edible 100 mg/5 mg rule: none located — Verify whether DOR has a beverage-specific rule. Medical exception: §16-12-224(8)(d) — higher potency may be sold to registered cardholders. §16-12-224(9): “A licensee or employee is prohibited from conducting a transaction that would result in a consumer or registered cardholder exceeding the personal possession amounts set forth in 16-12-106 and 16-12-515.” Purchase limits at dispensary (adult-use): per transaction, a consumer may not be sold more than the §16-12-106 possession amount (1 oz / 8 g / 800 mg solid edibles) — secondary summaries also cite an 8 g / 8 mL concentrate per-purchase figure from §16-12-224 context; Verify the exact per-transaction (vs per-day) adult-use purchase limit in current §16-12-224 / ARM, as the MCA 2025 §16-12-224 text opened states the transaction-possession rule in (9) but does not state a separate daily adult-use purchase cap.

1.4 Medical programme

Regulator transfer: DPHHS → DOR. HB 701 (2021) transferred authority over licensing, cultivation and sale of medical marijuana to registered cardholders to DOR (CARD/CCD). DOR CCD FAQ and programme pages are now the official source; DPHHS historic registry data is historic only. Exact transfer effective date: Verify in HB 701 session law (multiple effective dates in HB 701: 18 May 2021, 1 July 2021, 1 Jan 2022 per DOJ update) — do not state a single transfer date without that check.

Qualifying conditions — closed list (§16-12-102(10), verbatim in §1.2): (1) cancer, glaucoma, HIV-positive status, or AIDS when symptoms seriously/adversely affect health; (2) cachexia/wasting syndrome; (3) severe chronic pain (persistent, severe intensity, significantly interferes with daily activities, as documented by treating physician); (4) intractable nausea/vomiting; (5) epilepsy / intractable seizure disorder; (6) multiple sclerosis; (7) Crohn’s disease; (8) painful peripheral neuropathy; (9) CNS disorder resulting in chronic painful spasticity/muscle spasms; (10) hospice admittance per Department rules; (11) PTSD. No catch-all / physician-discretion condition. Anxiety alone does not qualify (secondary, Leafwell 2026 guide, accessed 1 Nov 2026 — secondary only, but consistent with the closed statutory list).

Provider certification: written certification by a Montana-licensed physician (MD/DO, treating or referral physician with bona fide relationship — secondary detail, Verify in §§16-12-503/509 and ARM). Physician Statement dated within 60 days of application (secondary, multiple 2026 guides — Verify on DOR cardholder page). Telemedicine: secondary guides state video telemedicine permitted for new and renewal patients, audio-only only if previously seen in person — Verify on DOR physician-information page. Minors: custodial parent/legal guardian + second physician certification unless physician is oncologist/neurologist/epileptologist (secondary — Verify in statute/ARM).

Registry cards and fees: apply via DOR TransAction Portal (TAP); $20 state fee (new or renewal, non-refundable per secondary guides); $10 replacement (secondary). Temporary printable card valid 60 days while permanent card is processed (secondary). Card validity up to 1 year or shorter if physician specifies (secondary). Official DOR cardholder page URL cited in secondary sources: revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/cardholder-information — Verify by opening; the $20 fee itself is consistently reported across secondary guides and is adopted here as secondary-sourced, not official-text-verified. Department duty (secondary): approve/deny complete application within 30 days; issue permanent card within 5 days after approval — Verify in §§16-12-503/509.

Purchase limits — medical (§16-12-515(1), verbatim):

“(1)(a) A registered cardholder who has elected to obtain marijuana and marijuana products through the system of licensed cultivators, manufacturers, or dispensaries may: (i) possess up to 1 ounce of usable marijuana; and (ii) purchase a maximum of 5 ounces of usable marijuana a month and no more than 1 ounce of usable marijuana a day. (b)(i) A registered cardholder may petition the department for an exception to the monthly limit on purchases. [AUDIT: DOR cardholder guidance states petitions increase the monthly limit from 5 ounces to 8 ounces, physician-signed — not to an unstated amount.] The request must be accompanied by a confirmation from the physician who signed the cardholder’s written certification that the cardholder’s debilitating medical condition warrants purchase of an amount exceeding the monthly limit. (ii) If the department approves an exception to the limit, the approval must establish the monthly amount of usable marijuana that the cardholder may purchase and the limit must be entered into the seed-to-sale tracking system.”

Note: possession at any one time is 1 oz (same as adult-use), but purchase is 1 oz/day, 5 oz/month — the “5 oz possession allowance” phrasing in some secondary guides is wrong and must not be repeated. A 2025 draft (LC 3814 / HB 748 draft) proposed raising possession to 5 oz and daily purchase to 5 oz — that draft was NOT enacted as §16-12-515(1) in MCA 2025 as retrieved; do not present it as law. Its final status (died / tabled) is Verify on leg.mt.gov. Protections (§16-12-515(2)): cardholder may not be arrested/prosecuted/penalised or denied any right/privilege (including professional licensing board / DLI disciplinary action) solely for cultivating/manufacturing/possessing/transporting within Part 5 amounts — subject to §16-12-108 and §16-12-515(7). Full §16-12-515 text beyond (1): Verify.

Patient home grow: 4 mature + 4 seedlings per cardholder (see §1.3); residence cap 2× (see caveat for mixed households). Out-of-state patients: No reciprocity located. “Registered cardholder” means a Montana resident (§16-12-102(35)). An out-of-state visitor 21+ may purchase adult-use in a green county as a consumer, but may not purchase as a medical patient (4% tax, higher potency, higher purchase limits) without a Montana card. Verify: whether any reciprocity / visiting-patient rule exists in ARM 42.39 — none was found in this research. Medical-only dispensaries: a medical dispensary licensed on/before 3 Nov 2020 may sell adult-use at the same location (DOR FAQ). Conversely, a dispensary that elects medical-only must post at entry, visible from outside: “This dispensary is licensed for medical marijuana sales only. No sales will be permitted without a valid registry identification card.” (ARM 42.39.122, MAR 2026-93.1.)

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and THC measurement (current Montana law, before any 2026 federal conforming amendment)

Hemp (§80-18-101(1)(a)): Cannabis sativa L. and any part, including seeds and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total delta-9 THC concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry weight basis. Does not include synthetic cannabinoids (§80-18-101(1)(b)). Measurement: “Total delta-9 THC” — how total delta-9 is calculated (delta-9 + THCA conversion factor, dry-weight basis, measurement uncertainty / DEA lab method) is Verify in ARM Title 4, Chapter 19 and MDA testing guidance. The statute says “total delta-9,” not “total THC” (all isomers). That distinction is the crux of the THCA question below. Hemp vs marijuana boundary: above 0.3% total delta-9 = marijuana under §16-12-102(20) (which excludes hemp as provided in §80-18-101), regulated by CCD, not MDA. Below/equal = hemp, regulated by MDA as an agricultural crop (§80-18-102), but sale to consumers is separately restricted by §16-12-117 and SB 375 — meeting the hemp definition does not by itself make a finished THC product freely saleable.

Delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC — status

Delta-8: PROHIBITED as a synthetic cannabinoid product in practice. (1) §50-32-222 lists delta-8 (delta-6) THC as a Schedule I tetrahydrocannabinol (secondary, Verify official text). (2) §16-12-102(39) synthetic definition covers cannabinoids produced artificially by chemical synthesis or biosynthesis (yeast/algae) — commercial delta-8 is overwhelmingly made by converting hemp CBD isolate, which DOR describes as: “Hemp biomass containing high levels of naturally occurring CBD is extracted and concentrated into legal, non-intoxicating CBD isolate. This CBD isolate is then mixed with hazardous chemicals in an unregulated setting to convert the CBD into nearly any synthetic cannabinoid” (DOR synthetic page). DOR lists delta-8-THC, delta-10-THC, THC-O-acetates, THCV, THCP, HHC, HHC-O-acetate, HHCP and CBN as examples to look for. (3) §16-12-117(1) prohibits manufacture/process/store/offer for sale of any synthetic cannabinoid product. Naturally occurring, non-converted delta-8 present in trace amounts in the plant: DOR’s page distinguishes “Naturally Occurring Cannabinoids — Legal in Montana” (regulated testing/labelling/age) from synthetic products — but no de minimis / naturally-occurring delta-8 threshold was located. Verify.

Delta-9 (hemp-derived): SALE TO CONSUMERS PROHIBITED unless FDA-authorised, subject to the §16-12-117 mg caps. SB 375 verbatim (enrolled, §1): “Unless authorized as a food or drug by the United States food and drug administration, a hemp product containing total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) may not be sold to a consumer in the state.” SB 375 defines “hemp product” (in the enrolled text summary) as a product containing post-harvest hemp intended for consumption, smoking, vaping or other administration. DOR summarises: “SB 375 prohibits the sale of any product made from hemp that contains any level of total THC.” No FDA-authorised hemp-derived delta-9 food/drug product was identified — Verify FDA authorisation list; in practice this is a ban on retail hemp delta-9, with the §16-12-117 0.5/2 mg caps as the only trace-amount tolerance located. The reconciliation between SB 375 and §16-12-117(3) is Verify (see Part 0.6).

THCA: NO THCA-SPECIFIC MONTANA STATUTE LOCATED — Verify. THCA is not named in §§16-12-102, 16-12-117, 80-18-101 or SB 375 as retrieved. Legal analysis must therefore proceed by category, not by name: (a) if THCA is in unadulterated hemp flower meeting ≤0.3% total delta-9 dry weight and not processed into extracts/infused products/concentrates, §16-12-117(4) exempts it from the mg caps; (b) if it is processed, converted, or sold as an intoxicating product, it falls under §16-12-117(3) / SB 375 (total delta-9) and, if synthetically/chemically converted, §16-12-117(1); (c) if the product’s total delta-9 (including any THCA-to-THC conversion on heating, if Montana’s testing method counts it) exceeds 0.3%, it is marijuana, not hemp. Whether Montana’s “total delta-9” testing method decarboxylates / counts THCA: Verify in MDA/DEA lab rules — this single testing question decides most THCA-flower cases and must not be guessed.

Other isomers / converted cannabinoids (delta-10, THC-O, HHC, THCP, etc.): treated as synthetic cannabinoid products when produced by conversion/synthesis — prohibited under §16-12-117(1) and listed by DOR. Naturally occurring trace amounts: Verify, same caveat as delta-8. CBD (non-intoxicating, naturally occurring): legal as a hemp product, subject to hemp definition and to DPHHS/FDA food/drug rules — secondary summaries consistently state CBD may not be added to conventional food or dietary supplements in Montana (DPHHS position) — Verify in DPHHS rules / Title 50, Chapter 31. Hemp seed foods are distinguished in secondary sources — Verify.

Minimum age, testing, labelling — hemp products

Age: DOR synthetic page states synthetic products have “Lack of age restrictions” as a problem (i.e. unregulated), and naturally occurring cannabinoids have “Regulated age restrictions.” A specific minimum age for hemp product purchase (for example 21) in statute/ARM was NOT located — Verify. Do not state “21+ for hemp” as Montana law without a citation. (For CCD marijuana products, the age is 21 for adult-use, cardholder age for medical.) Testing / labelling (hemp): MDA hemp programme requires DEA-registered lab testing under the USDA-approved plan framework (federal baseline: enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 — per task, not re-researched). Montana-specific hemp testing/labelling rules for finished consumer products: Verify in ARM Title 4, Chapter 19 and Title 50, Chapter 31, Part 2. For CCD marijuana products, testing/labelling are in ARM 42.39.314 et seq. (see §1.6) — those rules do not apply to hemp products sold outside dispensaries. THC beverages: two different regimes: (1) Marijuana beverages sold at dispensaries = edible/food products, 100 mg/package, 5 mg/serving (from 1 July 2026), §16-12-224(8)(b)(iv). (2) Hemp beverages sold outside dispensaries = hemp products, 0.5 mg total delta-9/serving, 2 mg/package (§16-12-117(3)), and SB 375 FDA-authorisation bar. In practice, an intoxicating hemp beverage (>2 mg/package) cannot lawfully be sold outside a dispensary in Montana after 2025. Hemp beverage-specific age / labelling / serving rules beyond the mg caps: Verify.

Montana response to the new federal hemp definition (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026)

No Montana conforming bill, rule, or agency statement responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 was located as of 1 Nov 2026 — Unavailable / Verify. Searches of DOR, MDA and leg.mt.gov sources opened for this task returned no Montana implementation guidance for those dates. This is expected: the federal dates fall after the research date (12 Nov and 11 Dec 2026), and Montana’s legislature is not in regular session in 2026. What would need to change if Montana conforms: Title 80, Chapter 18 definition (total delta-9 → total THC, including THCA and other isomers), plus the finished-product cap (federal 0.4 mg total THC per container vs Montana’s 0.5 mg/serving / 2 mg/package + SB 375 ban). Whether Montana will conform, or rely on its already-stricter sale bans, is Verify — do not predict. Tribal hemp: whether any Montana tribe operates a hemp programme under a USDA-approved tribal plan vs the Montana state plan: Verify in Part 10 (USDA hemp records).

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffers

Packaging / labelling — CCD marijuana products

Statutory basis: §16-12-112(1)(h) (labelling/packaging standards: pharmacologically active ingredients incl. THC/CBD/other cannabinoids, mg per serving, servings per package, quantity limits per sale); §16-12-215 (labelling / education, as amended by HB 792); ARM 42.39.314 (general labelling), 42.39.315–320, 42.39.319 (exit packaging). 2026 labelling changes (MAR 2026-93.2, eff. 22 Aug 2026): exit packaging must be child-resistant and opaque, contain all state-mandated warnings, and include a state-provided QR code linking to substance-use resources. Warnings address anxiety, agitation, paranoia, psychosis, excessive vomiting, pregnancy/nursing risks, and driving under the influence. Dispensaries must display a state-issued consumer education poster at every point of sale, clearly visible. Sources: DOR September 2026 Newsletter; Marijuana Herald, 1 Sept 2026 (secondary summary); MAR adoption notice: MAR 2026-93-2. HB 792 (2025) purpose: “AN ACT GENERALLY REVISING LAWS RELATED TO MARIJUANA LABELING AND EDUCATION; REQUIRING WARNING POSTERS AT POINTS OF MARIJUANA SALE; PROVIDING RULEMAKING AUTHORITY; AMENDING SECTIONS 16-12-112 AND 16-12-215, MCA; AND PROVIDING A DELAYED EFFECTIVE DATE” (1 July 2026). Four warning statements required (secondary summary: anxiety/psychosis risks, pregnancy/nursing harm, driving under influence — Verify full four warnings in current §16-12-215). Historic labelling content (ARM, via rules.mt.gov gateway excerpt): container label must show licensee business/trade name + registration number; harvest/manufacture date; strain/common name; net weight/volume (US customary + metric); concentration by weight/volume of THC, THCA, CBD, CBDA; unique identification number (seed-to-sale); consumer warnings (“For use by Montana Medical Marijuana Program registered cardholders only. Keep out of reach of children.” / “It is illegal to drive a motor vehicle while under the influence of marijuana.” / “This product is not approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to treat, cure, or prevent any disease.”); if passed testing: “This Product Has Been Tested and Meets the Quality Assurance Requirements of the State of Montana.” Labels ≥8 pt Times New Roman/Helvetica/Arial, in English, unobstructed/conspicuous; no untruthful/misleading statements; not attractive to minors (no cartoons, no resemblance to non-cannabis products marketed to minors, no symbols/celebrities marketed to minors, no images of minors, no words associated with minors). Verify which of these historic (medical-era) labelling rules survive in current ARM 42.39.314 as amended 2026 — the excerpt may be a superseded version. Edible shape restrictions (HB 636, 2025, amending §16-12-208): edible candy may not be sold in shapes/packages attractive to children or easily confused with commercially sold non-marijuana candy; desserts may not be in shapes/packages attractive to children or easily confused with common desserts (brownies, cakes, cookies, cupcakes). Verify final §16-12-208 text as enacted.

Advertising

Statute: §16-12-211 — Limitations on advertising; rulemaking. Full text: Verify. Rule — ARM 42.39.123 (verbatim excerpts, via Cornell): “(1) A licensee may promote its business and market its brand but may not advertise marijuana or marijuana products except in electronic advertising.” “(2) ‘Advertise or advertising’ means the publication, dissemination, solicitation, or circulation of visual, oral, or written communication to directly induce any person to purchase or consume marijuana or marijuana products. Advertising includes the promotion of special pricing, sales, or discounts on marijuana and marijuana products. Advertising does not include branding, marketing, or packaging and labeling of marijuana and marijuana products or information regarding special pricing, sales, or discounts on display within a licensed premises.” “(6) A licensee may use the phrase ‘marijuana’ or ‘cannabis’ in its signage or in its electronic advertising.” “(7) A licensee’s outdoor signage may not use colloquial terms for marijuana or marijuana products (e.g., pot, reefer, weed) and may not use an image or visual representation of useable marijuana, marijuana-infused products, marijuana concentrates, marijuana paraphernalia, or an image that indicates the presence of a product such as smoke, edibles, etc.” “(8) A licensee’s outdoor signage must comply with any applicable local jurisdiction sign ordinances and regulations.” “(11) A marijuana business may not: (a) engage in advertising via marketing directed towards location-based devices, including, but not limited to cellular phones, unless users affirmatively opt in to receiving push notifications…; (d) engage in advertising or utilize signage that asserts its products are safe; (e) utilize a billboard; …” (plus youth-appeal / toy / cartoon / mascot prohibitions in secondary summaries — Verify full (11) text). Plain English: brand promotion is allowed; product advertising is allowed only electronically. No billboards, no “safe” claims, no youth-appealing signage, no colloquial terms or product imagery outdoors. Failed broader ban (2023): a bill to broaden the advertising ban (billboards, outdoor signage, brand use in print/TV/radio/sponsorships) was tabled in committee and did not become law. Bill number: Verify — do not cite a number without checking leg.mt.gov.

Delivery

Medical delivery: secondary summaries cite ARM 42.39.413 as allowing medical marijuana delivery. Verify current ARM 42.39.413 text and whether adult-use delivery is permitted, to whom, in which counties, and with what vehicle/security rules. No delivery provision was located in §§16-12-106/108/224 as opened. Adult-use delivery: Unavailable / Verify — do not state it is allowed or prohibited without ARM 42.39.413. Note also Part 4.6: a transporter may deliver only to licensed premises or registered cardholders and may not deliver to adult-use consumers.

Zoning and buffers (licensed premises)

State buffer: 500 feet, same street, from church/synagogue/place of worship, school/postsecondary school (other than commercially operated school), or DPHHS-licensed/registered child-care facility, unless locality requires greater distance; straight-line entrance-to-entrance; renewal exception if premises pre-dated the protected use; applies to new applications on/after 1 Jan 2024; “school” includes public/private preschools (HB 903, 2023). ARM 42.39.124 three-part test (same street + same-street address + ≤500 ft). Local ordinance supplanting §16-12-207 displaces the state rule (ARM 42.39.124). Local zoning: local government may by ordinance regulate a marijuana business for public health/safety/welfare, including inspections (§16-12-301(2)(a)). Licensed premises must not be in a zone where marijuana activity conflicts with a local ordinance filed with the Department (§16-12-207(3)(a)(i)), and must be approved by local building/health/fire officials (§16-12-207(3)(a)(ii)). County approval (green/red) — §16-12-301 structure: green counties: no local approval process (3)–(6) required. Red counties: category-by-category approval by local jurisdiction election (petition or governing-body resolution) required before a business in that category may operate, plus Department licence. Former medical licensees not seeking adult-use may continue in existing premises notwithstanding local inaction (see §1.2 caveat — Verify full §16-12-301 text). City treatment: ARM 42.39.102 “green county” / “red county” definitions expressly state: “This definition does not include a municipality that has approved or disapproved a category or categories of marijuana businesses, as authorized in 16-12-301, MCA.” That is, a city can approve/disapprove categories separately from its county — the city-by-city map is Verify in Part 3; do not assume a city follows its county. Dispensary premises design (secondary, §16-12-207 context): adult-use/medical dispensary must have a single secured entrance for patrons and strict security measures — Verify in current §16-12-207 / ARM.

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking system

System: Metrc. Confirmed by multiple sources: DOR seed-to-sale rule amendments refer to scheduling harvests “in Metrc” and affixing “METRC tags” (MAR 2026-445.2; Marijuana Herald Sept 2026 summary); Flowhub Montana guide (secondary, accessed 1 Nov 2026): “dispensaries are required to track inventory, transfers, and sales in Metrc — Montana’s official seed-to-sale tracking system.” Statutory basis: §16-12-105 (tracking system) + §16-12-112(1)(g) (rulemaking for tracking). Full §16-12-105 verbatim: Verify. Rule — ARM 42.39.203 as amended eff. 22 Aug 2026 (MAR 2026-445.2, MAR 2026-445-2): “A licensee must use the department’s seed-to-sale tracking system as the primary inventory and record keeping system. Licensees must use a unique identification number tag purchased from the seed-to-sale tracking system vendor for all seed-to-sale tracking purposes.” “All samples taken for quality assurance testing must be recorded in the seed-to-sale tracking system.” “Licensees must monitor all compliance notifications from the seed-to-sale tracking system and must resolve any compliance notification issues within 30 days.” Packages under administrative action must be quarantined, not sold/altered/manufactured (except destruction per ARM 42.39.310). Secondary summary of the same rule (Marijuana Herald, 1 Sept 2026): individual plants tagged once 12 inches in height, tag physically attached by 18 inches; harvest lots / process lots / test batches package-tagged; flower/trim/usable packages generally ≤5 lbs (exceptions: concentrate/extract-only material not undergoing standard testing, whole wet plants packaged directly after harvest); on-site + in-transit inventory reconciled by 11:59 p.m. daily; wet weight of each harvested plant recorded on harvest day; harvests scheduled in Metrc ≥72 hours in advance. Verify each of these operational details in the adopted ARM 42.39.203 text — they are adopted here only as secondary summary of the rule. Medical exception limits in Metrc: §16-12-515(1)(b)(ii) — Department-approved monthly exception amount “must be entered into the seed-to-sale tracking system.” Hemp tracking: hemp is not in Metrc. Hemp licensees report to MDA / USDA under the hemp plan (Part 7, Run 2). A marijuana licensee “may not cultivate hemp or engage in hemp manufacturing at a licensed premises” (§16-12-104) — the two tracking/reporting systems are kept separate by law.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections

This is a two-statute tension — both must be stated together, not one alone.

§16-12-108(5) — No accommodation / no cause of action (verbatim):

“(5) Nothing in this chapter may be construed to: (a) require an employer to permit or accommodate conduct otherwise allowed by this chapter in any workplace or on the employer’s property; (b) prohibit an employer from disciplining an employee for violation of a workplace drug policy or for working while intoxicated by marijuana or marijuana products; (c) prevent an employer from declining to hire, discharging, disciplining, or otherwise taking an adverse employment action against an individual with respect to hire, tenure, terms, conditions, or privileges of employment because of the individual’s violation of a workplace drug policy or intoxication by marijuana or marijuana products while working; (d) prohibit an employer from including in any contract a provision prohibiting the use of marijuana for a debilitating medical condition; or (e) permit a cause of action against an employer for wrongful discharge pursuant to 39-2-904 or discrimination pursuant to 49-1-102.”

§39-2-313 — Lawful products / off-duty use (HB 655, 2021, as amended; secondary summaries). Marijuana is a “lawful product” for §39-2-313 purposes. Employers may not refuse to hire, or discriminate in compensation/promotion/terms, because an individual lawfully uses marijuana (including medical marijuana) off the employer’s premises during non-working hours, subject to exceptions including safety-sensitive positions, bona fide occupational qualifications (for example federal DOT), impairment at work, violation of a workplace drug policy, and good-faith belief of policy violation / impairment. Sources: Baker Donelson Montana Guide (updated Sept 2025); National Drug Screening Montana page; First Advantage update (20 Jan 2026). Full current §39-2-313 verbatim: Verify on leg.mt.gov — the secondary summaries agree on the structure but differ on the safety-sensitive / DOT carve-out wording. Workforce Drug and Alcohol Testing Act (WDATA), §§39-2-205 to 39-2-211: employers with a qualified testing programme must comply with §§39-2-207 criteria; pre-employment testing as a condition of hire permitted under §39-2-208(1). Testing must follow WDATA procedures; a positive test alone vs impairment at work is the disputed line — §16-12-108(5)(c) protects action for policy violation or intoxication while working, while §39-2-313 protects off-duty lawful use. How Montana courts / DLI reconcile a positive test with no evidence of workplace impairment: Verify — case law (including pre-2021 medical-accommodation case law cited in secondary sources) is not current-law authority for the post-HB 655 regime and must not be cited as such without checking. Other employment protections in Chapter 12: §16-12-106(8)–(9) (adoption/custody/visitation, organ transplant/health care); §16-12-515(2) (cardholder may not be penalised / denied right or privilege, including professional licensing board / DLI disciplinary action, solely for Part 5 conduct — subject to §16-12-108). Professional licensing boards: Verify whether any board has issued marijuana-specific guidance. Marijuana industry workers: a worker permit is required for any employee participating in any part of a marijuana business (DOR FAQ; §16-12-226). SB 74 (2025) extends the worker-permit requirement to third-party individuals performing work for a marijuana business. Worker permit fee, renewal, background-check and disqualifying-offence details: Verify in §16-12-226 / ARM — not established in this research. Driving (for completeness, Part 12 to expand): 5 ng/mL whole-blood delta-9 THC per se standard is reported in secondary sources (NORML Montana Drugged Driving; MPP DUID page — Montana/Washington 5 ng/mL, no patient exception). Verify current §61-8-1002 (formerly §61-8-401, recodified 2021) text — the per se figure is secondary-sourced here.

1.9 Pending legislation — bill numbers, summaries, status confirmed on leg.mt.gov

Session context (critical): Montana’s Legislature meets biennially in odd-numbered years. The 69th Legislature (2025) adjourned in 2025. There is no regular session in 2026. Therefore, as of 1 Nov 2026, there are no bills “pending” in a sitting regular session in the ordinary sense. What exists is: (a) 2025 bills enacted or vetoed (final status below); (b) veto-override polls (closed — see SB 537); (c) interim committee work and pre-filed / draft (LC) measures for the 70th Legislature (2027); (d) any ballot-issue filings for 2026. Any list that presents a 2025 bill as “pending” in Nov 2026 without noting adjournment is wrong.

Moratorium / taxes / licensing / local / hemp — pending summary

  • Taxes: no pending rate change located (20%/4%/3% remain). SB 537 reallocation is dead; HB 932 distribution is the signed 2025 change (Verify chapter).
  • Licensing: no new-licence opening before 1 July 2027 except existing-premises / relocation / transfer and (Verify) combined-use. SB 74 third-party disclosure rulemaking is the live licensing change.
  • Local options: county opt-in/opt-out elections can occur at any election under §16-12-301 / Title 13, Ch. 1, Pt. 5 — there is no single “pending” local bill; each county election is a Part 3 item.
