PART 2: TAXES

2.1 State excise taxes: 20% adult-use, 4% medical — unchanged in rate

Current rates, confirmed on DOR pages accessed 1 Nov 2026: “Currently, there is a 20% tax levied upon adult-use marijuana, and a 4% tax on medical marijuana.” — DOR, Legal is Legit. “20% tax on adult use marijuana and marijuana products sales; 4% tax on medical marijuana and marijuana products sales.” — DOR Cannabis Tax page, as summarised in secondary cross-checks; the DOR Sales Reports page confirms the same structure in its tax totals (see §2.6). Statute: MCA Title 15, Chapter 64, Part 1 — cited in secondary and DOR material as §15-64-102, MCA (tax on marijuana sales). The DOR Sales Reports page itself directs readers: “For more information on Montana’s general provisions for cannabis taxation, please visit 15-64-10, MCA” [sic — the DOR link text truncates the section number]. Source: DOR Sales Reports. No rate change has been found. No enacted 2025 bill changing the 20%/4% rates was located. The 2025 bills that did change cannabis money were about distribution (HB 932, §2.3) and licensing footprint (SB 27), not the headline rates. Any claim of a rate change to, for example, 14% or a medical exemption is wrong for Montana — one secondary tax guide found in searching incorrectly described medical cannabis as “exempt from excise tax” in one passage while correctly giving 4% in another; the DOR 4% figure is the one used here. Montana has no general state sales tax. The cannabis excise is therefore the whole state retail-level tax burden on cannabis, not a layer on top of a general sales tax. Combined-rate arithmetic: adult-use 20% + local 3% = 23% maximum; medical 4% + local 3% = 7% maximum.

Tax base — CONFLICT flagged. Two different formulations appear: (1) DOR Sales Reports page, “Regarding Estimated Sales Data”: DOR “assesses the Adult Use and Medical Cannabis Tax on the ‘Retail Price’” defined as “the established price paid by the consumer,” and adds (pre-SB 74 wording): “This established price would not include any discounts or reductions.” [AUDIT: current law (SB 74, Ch. 550) is §15-64-101(10): “Retail price” means the established price AFTER any discount or reduction. Resolve the conflict in favour of §15-64-101(10); the DOR page wording is stale for the tax base.] It also warns monthly sales tables “may include discounted or reduced purchase prices” that “may not reflect the actual ‘Retail Price.’” (2) Secondary summaries (for example Herring Bank, citing SB 74) state state marijuana tax is now calculated on the retail price after discounts and promotions. Status: Verify. Do not rely on either formulation for a filing decision without checking the current DOR Cannabis Tax page and MCA §15-64-102 / §15-64-103 text in full.

Filing cadence. Dispensaries file and remit quarterly — returns due 15 April, 15 July, 15 October and 15 January (15 days after quarter-end). A return must be filed even with zero sales; the minimum penalty for not filing / filing late was reported as $50 in DOR’s September 2022 CCD newsletter (secondary republication, Bloomberg Tax, 6 Sept 2022). That quarterly cadence is why DOR labels all monthly sales figures “Estimated Sales” and revises them after quarters close.

2.2 Local-option tax: up to 3%, county by county, election required

Statute: MCA §16-12-311, “Local government excise tax — election required — procedure — notice.” History: En. Sec. 96, Ch. 576, L. 2021 (HB 701); amd. Sec. 26, Ch. 712, L. 2023. Text as shown on the Justia mirror of the 2025 MCA (official MCA text to be re-checked at mca.legmt.gov before legal reliance): “(1) A county that has permitted an adult-use dispensary or medical marijuana dispensary to operate within its borders pursuant to 16-12-301 or a county in which the majority of voters voted to approve Initiative Measure No. 190 in the November 3, 2020, general election, may not impose or, except as provided in this section, amend or repeal a local-option marijuana excise tax unless the local-option marijuana excise tax question has been approved by a majority of the qualified electors voting on the question.” “(3) The petition or resolution referring the taxing question must state: (a) the rate of the tax, which may not exceed 3% of the retail value of all marijuana and marijuana products sold at an adult-use dispensary or medical marijuana dispensary; (b) the date when the tax becomes effective, which may not be earlier than 90 days after the election…” “(6) The question of the imposition of a local-option marijuana excise tax may not be placed before the qualified electors more than once in any fiscal year.” Mirror: Justia — §16-12-311. DOR summary, Sales Reports page: “The Local Options Marijuana Excise tax is a tax on the retail value of all marijuana and marijuana products sold at an adult use dispensary or medical marijuana dispensary within a county. The rate of the Local Options Tax must be established by the election petition or resolution. The rate may not exceed 3%.”

Key points: the tax may be on adult-use only, medical only, or both — the ballot question states which. This is why the CCD/DOR table has separate Medical and Adult-Use columns, and why “blue” has three meanings in CCD usage (see Part 3.1). The question may be put by elector petition (MCA Title 7, Chapter 5, Part 1 procedure) or by resolution of the county governing body. Effective date ≥90 days after the election. For example Gallatin County’s 3% tax is reported as effective 1 March 2023 following its 2022 votes. A county that has not permitted a dispensary and did not vote for I-190 cannot impose the tax under §16-12-311(1) until it has permitted operation under §16-12-301 — in practice, red counties vote on the sales question and the tax question together or in sequence (Beaverhead and Granite are the clearest examples).

Distribution of local-option revenue — official DOR formula (unchanged by HB 932): 50% retained by the county; 45% apportioned to municipalities in the county on the basis of the ratio of the city/town population to total county population; 5% retained by DOR for administration. Sources: DOR Sales Reports and Legal is Legit (“50% retained is by the County — 45% is apportioned to Municipalities based on the ratio of the population of the city or town to the total county population — 5% is retained by Department of Revenue for administration”). Distribution timing (DOR FAQ): local-option distributions are made 60 days after the quarter-ending due date: Q1 distribution 15 June (collection 1 Feb–30 Apr); Q2 15 Sept (1 May–31 July); Q3 15 Dec (1 Aug–31 Oct); Q4 15 Mar (1 Nov–31 Jan). Source: DOR FAQ. Note: “Local Option Tax is not derived from estimated sales reports. Local option distributions are connected to the sales taxes collected by licensees and submitted to [DOR] prior to the end of the applicable collection period.” — DOR Sales Reports page.

Which counties have a local-option tax TODAY — DOR county table, September 2026 (the latest county table opened). Source: September 2026 county sales — columns “Local Option Tax – Medical” / “Local Option Tax – Adult Use.” Every county in this table levying 3% levies 3%; no county levies 1% or 2% in this table. 30 of 56 counties levy a local-option tax; 26 levy none.

Local-option scopeCounties (Sept 2026 DOR table)
Both adult-use and medical, 3% + 3% (24)Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Cascade, Custer, Dawson, Deer Lodge, Gallatin, Hill, Jefferson, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Lincoln, Madison, Mineral, Park, Powell, Richland, Roosevelt, Rosebud, Sheridan, Silver Bow, Valley, Yellowstone
Adult-use only, 3% (4)Flathead, Missoula, Ravalli, Sanders (medical sales in these counties carry only the 4% state tax)
Medical only, 3% (2)Beaverhead, Granite (both RED for adult-use — see Part 3; the tax is collected on medical sales; there are no lawful adult-use sales to tax; Granite’s adult-use tax ballot history is flagged in §2.4)
No local-option tax (26)Broadwater, Carter, Chouteau, Daniels, Fallon, Fergus, Garfield, Glacier, Golden Valley, Judith Basin, Liberty, McCone, Meagher, Musselshell, Petroleum, Phillips, Pondera, Powder River, Prairie, Stillwater, Sweet Grass, Teton, Toole, Treasure, Wheatland, Wibaux

Combined rates that follow: in a both-tax county, adult-use = 23% (20+3), medical = 7% (4+3). In an adult-only county, adult-use = 23%, medical = 4%. In a medical-only county, medical = 7%, adult-use sales are prohibited. In a no-tax county, adult-use = 20% (if green), medical = 4%. The December 2025 county table shows the identical tax columns for every county (checked: December 2025 county sales). No county changed its local-option status between Dec 2025 and Sept 2026 in the DOR tables.

2.3 Distribution of STATE revenue: the HEART / habitat / veterans / general-fund split — and the 2025 change (HB 932)

This is the section where an official DOR page and the current statute disagree on the page as retrieved, and both are shown.

(a) The formula still printed on DOR’s education page (accessed 1 Nov 2026)

DOR, Legal is Legit states state-level distribution as: “$6 million to the Healing and Ending Addiction through Recovery and Treatment (HEART) account established in 16-12-122, MCA.” “After the HEART distribution, the remainder goes to: 20% to Department of Fish, Wildlife, and Parks to be used solely as funding for wildlife habitat; 4% to the State Park account; 4% to the Trails and Recreational Facilities account [and] 4% to the Nongame Wildlife account; 3% or $200,000, whichever is less, to the Veterans and Surviving Spouses State Special Revenue account; $150,000 to the Board of Crime Control to fund crisis intervention team training; Remaining funds go to the state’s General Fund.” That matches the pre-HB 932 text of MCA §16-12-111(4): at the end of each fiscal year, after retaining a 3-month operating reserve, DOR transfers (a) “an amount not to exceed $6 million” to HEART, then (b) distributes the net balance 20% / 4% / 4% / 4% / 3%-or-$200,000 / $150,000 / remainder to the general fund. History: En. Sec. 35, I.M. No. 190, approved Nov. 3, 2020; amd. Sec. 46, Ch. 576, L. 2021 (HB 701). The $300,000 Department of Justice drug-detection-canine item in the old §16-12-111(4)(b)(vi) terminated 30 June 2025 (Sec. 117(2), Ch. 576, L. 2021).

(b) The formula as amended by HB 932 (2025, signed, effective 1 July 2025) — this is the current statute

HB 932 (69th Legislature, 2025; sponsor Rep. Ken Walsh; “revising laws related to funding for conservation; revising the allocation of the marijuana state special revenue account; establishing a habitat legacy account…; amending sections 16-12-111…” etc.) was signed by Gov. Greg Gianforte in May 2025 and took effect 1 July 2025. Sources: Montana Public Radio, 22 May 2025 (“Gov. Greg Gianforte signed House Bill 932 into law late last week… It will go into effect on July 1.”); Marijuana Herald, 16 May 2025 (“takes effect July 1”). The HB 932 amendment text strikes “an amount not to exceed $6 million” and substitutes “11%” for HEART, and strikes the direct FWP wildlife-habitat line and substitutes the Habitat Legacy Account: “(4) At the end of each fiscal year, the department shall transfer funds in excess of a 3-month operating reserve necessary to fund operating costs at the beginning of the next fiscal year in the following order: (a) [an amount not to exceed $6 million] 11% must be transferred to the healing and ending addiction through recovery and treatment (HEART) account established in 16-12-122; (b) the net balance remaining after distribution to the HEART account must be distributed as follows: (i) 20% to the [department of fish, wildlife, and parks…] habitat legacy account provided for in [HB 932 §1]; (ii) 4% to the state park account…; (iii) 4% to the trails and recreational facilities account…; (iv) 4% to the nongame wildlife account…; (v) 3% or $200,000, whichever is less, to the veterans and surviving spouses state special revenue account…” Summaries of the enacted allocation (secondary, consistent across MTPR, Wild Montana, Ducks Unlimited, May 2025) give the post-HB 932 shares as approximately: HEART 11%; Habitat Legacy Account 20%; State Parks 4%; Trails 4%; Nongame Wildlife 4%; Veterans 3% (≈$3.5m on a ~$117m biennium forecast); remainder to the General Fund (54% is a biennium-forecast approximation only, not a statutory share). Wild Montana’s summary: “54%… will go to the state’s General Fund… 11% will go to the HEART fund… 3%… will go to a program that helps veterans… The rest (32%…) will be invested in public lands, access, habitat, working lands, and land stewardship” (20+4+4+4 = 32%). Source: Wild Montana, 21 May 2025. Inside the Habitat Legacy Account (HB 932 §1): at each fiscal year-end the State Treasurer transfers from the account: 75% to the Land and Wildlife Stewardship Account (to be used solely as funding to secure wildlife habitat under §87-1-209, including Habitat Montana and water-related projects); 20% to the Wildlife Habitat Improvement Program (WHIP) account; 5% to the Wildlife Crossings account (to reduce wildlife-vehicle collisions). MTPR (22 May 2025): “roughly $12 million will be used for a ‘habitat legacy account’”; Ducks Unlimited (27 May 2025): HB 932 “allocates $32 million through recreational cannabis tax revenue every two years to support outdoor recreation and habitat” and “After July 1, 2025… the more recent share of cannabis tax revenue will go into [the Habitat Legacy Account].” Sources: MTPR; Ducks Unlimited.

What to use going forward: the HB 932 formula (11% HEART + Habitat Legacy) is the current statute for FY2026 onward. The DOR Legal-is-Legit page still printing “$6 million” on 1 Nov 2026 is treated here as a stale DOR education page, not as current law. The $150,000 Board of Crime Control crisis-intervention item: HB 932 draft text retains a flat-dollar item after the veterans line, but the post-enactment percentage summaries (54% general fund) do not separately break it out. Verify whether the $150,000 item survived in the enrolled HB 932 text before quoting a complete FY2026 distribution table. The enrolled chapter number for HB 932 was not verified in this search and is marked Verify — do not cite a chapter number from memory. HEART Fund in plain English: Healing and Ending Addiction through Recovery and Treatment, §16-12-122, MCA, administered for substance-use / behavioural-health treatment and recovery. Under the old formula it was capped at $6m/year; under HB 932 it is 11% of the excess over the operating reserve, i.e. roughly $6–6.5m on ~$55–60m annual state tax revenue — similar in dollars, different in mechanism. Worked illustration (for scale only, not an official distribution table): on FY2025-budget-projection figures reported by Reilly Neill (Substack, 2025, citing the state’s own fiscal-year table — secondary): cannabis tax revenue ≈$54.1m; HEART $6.0m (old cap); wildlife habitat ≈$9.6m; state parks, trails and nongame wildlife ≈$1.9m each; veterans $200,000; Board of Crime Control $450,000 including a temporary appropriation; general fund ≈$32.1m. That illustration is pre-HB 932 and must not be presented as the FY2026 formula. Source: Reilly Neill, Substack, 2025.

2.4 Local-option election history — dated results that could be verified

Montana’s local-option tax elections came in waves. Individual-county vote percentages for most counties were not located in official Secretary of State canvass form in this search and are marked Verify below where only the DOR current-status table proves the outcome. The dated waves that are sourced:

2 November 2021 (first wave, MTPR, unofficial results at the time): Missoula County approved 3% local tax on recreational (adult-use) only; narrowly rejected a medical increase. Park County approved 3% on recreational and approved a medical increase. Yellowstone County approved 3% on recreational and approved a medical increase. Source: MTPR, 3 Nov 2021 (“Missoula, Park and Yellowstone counties each approved a 3% local tax on recreational marijuana… Park and Yellowstone counties also appear to have approved a tax increase on medical marijuana, and Missoula narrowly rejected that increase.”) This matches the Sept 2026 DOR table exactly: Missoula = adult 3% / medical –, Park = 3%/3%, Yellowstone = 3%/3%.

7 June 2022 primary: secondary reporting (Nov 2022 preview) states: “During the June primary, 13 counties, mostly rural, approved the local option taxes by a 3-to-1 margin.” The full list of those 13 was not reproduced in the snippet retrieved. Verify the June 2022 county list from the Secretary of State canvass / county canvasses before naming all 13. Source: Sensijuana, Nov 2022. Gallatin County is a special case in this round: Gallatin voters passed adult-use and medical tax measures in the June primary, but “an administrative snafu necessitated a do-over” in November 2022, when they passed again (Valley Press/Mineral Independent, 14 Nov 2022, Valley Press).

8 November 2022 general election (MTPR early results, 9 Nov 2022; Valley Press final summary, 14 Nov 2022): adult-use tax measures on the ballot in up to 10 counties: Granite, Sheridan, Madison, Gallatin, Mineral, Sanders, Hill, Valley, Cascade and Flathead. “Most were on track to do so” (MTPR, MTPR, 9 Nov 2022). Final summary: “Every adult-use tax measure passed, and nearly all of the medical marijuana tax votes passed as well.” Flathead and Sanders were the only counties where the medical-tax vote was ahead for “no” (Sanders by just two votes at the early count, raising a possible recount). This matches the Sept 2026 DOR table: Flathead = adult 3% / medical –, Sanders = adult 3% / medical –; Cascade, Gallatin, Hill, Madison, Mineral, Sheridan, Valley = 3%/3%. Granite County in Nov 2022: Valley Press reports Granite voters “did pass separate measures to tax both medical marijuana and adult-use sales, should the county reinstate the latter at a future date,” even as they rejected re-legalising adult-use sales (52–48, §3.2). CONFLICT: the DOR Sept 2026 table shows Granite as medical 3% / adult “–”. Either the adult-use tax is shown as “–” because it is inoperative while sales are prohibited, or it was never put into effect. Verify Granite’s adult-use tax status from the county canvass / DOR local-option distribution list; do not cite Granite as currently collecting an adult-use tax. Beaverhead County in Nov 2022: preview reporting says Beaverhead would vote to tax medical and recreational sales although recreational sales were prohibited in the county. The Sept 2026 DOR table shows Beaverhead as medical 3% / adult “–” — the same inoperative-tax question as Granite. Verify on the same basis. MJBizDaily’s Nov 2022 roundup also lists tax measures approved in Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Gallatin, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Powell, Ravalli, Richland, Roosevelt, Rosebud and Silver Bow counties (all 3%). Whether each of those was a June-primary or November-general approval is Verify county by county; the DOR Sept 2026 table confirms all twelve now levy tax as follows: Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Powell, Richland, Rosebud, Silver Bow = 3%/3%; Ravalli = adult-only 3%; Roosevelt = 3%/3%.

June 2023 primary — Roosevelt County: Northern Plains Independent (23 July 2026 article, reporting DOR June figures) states: “Roosevelt County residents voted in favor of establishing 3 percent local option excise taxes for both medical and non-medical marijuana during the primary election in June 2023. Funds from the taxes are split with 50 percent going to the county, 5 percent to the state and the rest divided among the cities in the county. Funds for the county go to law enforcement efforts.” Source: Northern Plains Independent, 23 July 2026. CONFLICT / Verify: this June 2023 date sits uneasily with the Nov 2022 roundup listing Roosevelt among counties approving tax measures in 2022. Possibilities include a 2022 approval followed by a 2023 re-vote, or a reporting error in one source. Roosevelt’s current 3%/3% status is not in doubt (DOR Sept 2026 table); its first effective date is Verify.

November 2024 general election — Custer County: ballot preview (Custer County Politics, 2024): two Custer County questions — (1) “LOCAL-OPTION EXCISE TAX… Shall Custer County be authorized to impose a local-option excise tax of 3% of the retail value of all marijuana and marijuana products sold at any adult-use dispensary and at any medical marijuana dispensary within Custer County? This tax will be effective 90 days after passage. The tax revenue will be used for any authorized governmental purpose.” (2) “ALLOW ADULT-USE SALES AT MARIJUANA DISPENSARIES IN CUSTER COUNTY.” Source: Custer County Politics, 2024. Both passed in substance: Custer voted 58% in favour of opening the county to the recreational market (Miles City Star, Jan/Feb 2025 reporting), adult-use sales began at the start of 2025, and the 3% tax was implemented with revenue shared between Custer County, Miles City and Ismay plus DOR’s share; Miles City’s Q1 2025 (Jan–Mar) share was $12,900 (Miles City Star finance-committee reporting). DOR tables confirm the sequence: Feb 2024 Custer = None/None, adult $0, medical $131,891; Sept 2026 Custer = 3%/3%, adult $277,402, medical $67,684.

Counties with no dated election found in this search — status from DOR table only, election date Verify: Lincoln, Jefferson, Carbon (beyond the Nov 2022 roundup mention), Blaine, Big Horn, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Powell, Richland, Rosebud, Silver Bow (beyond the Nov 2022 roundup mention), Dawson (tax status; Dawson’s sales opt-in is dated in §3.2), Deer Lodge, Mineral, Valley, Sheridan, Hill, Madison — all levy 3% in the Sept 2026 DOR table, but their individual ballot dates/percentages were not verified to official canvass in this task and must not be invented. Local-option tax amounts collected: DOR publishes county sales tables, not a single published local-option collections total on the pages opened. Unavailable as a single official total in this search; county-by-county local-option revenue can only be derived as 3% × the relevant channel’s sales in that county, and DOR expressly warns local-option distributions are not derived from estimated sales reports. Do not present a derived statewide local-option total as an official figure.

2.5 Licence and related fees (state level; full licence types are Part 4)

Licence and registered-cardholder fees are deposited into the marijuana state special revenue account (§16-12-111(2)(c): the account consists of, among other things, “license and registered cardholder fees deposited into the account pursuant to this chapter” and “taxes collected pursuant to Title 15, chapter 64, part 1” and “taxes deposited… pursuant to 16-12-310” [local-option]). Fees confirmed on DOR licence pages / MCA (secondary cross-check where noted): Dispensary: $5,000 for each location (first location and each additional location — MCA §16-12-224(6)(b); DOR Dispensary Licenses page: “The dispensary license fee is $5,000 for each location. Applications require a processing fee of 20% of the license fees. Application processing fees are non-refundable regardless of the outcome.”) Source: DOR Dispensary Licenses. Manufacturer: tiered by monthly concentrate production — $5,000 (<1 lb up to 10 lbs/month), $10,000 (10–15 lbs), $20,000 (15 lbs+) — MCA §16-12-221(6)(a) (Justia mirror). Cultivator: tiered by canopy, $1,000 to $37,000/year depending on square footage (MPP fee breakdown, secondary — Verify against current MCA §16-12-223 / DOR cultivator page before quoting a tier table; the full tier table is given in Part 4.3 from MCA §16-12-223 as opened). Combined-use (tribal) licence: $7,500/year (DOR Combined-Use Licenses page). Source: DOR Combined-Use Licenses. Transporter / testing laboratory: $10,000 biannually (transporter) / $5,000 annually (lab) per MPP secondary breakdown — Verify against DOR pages; not confirmed on an official page opened in this task. Application processing fee: 20% of the applicable licence fee(s), non-refundable, due with the application (DOR dispensary and combined-use pages). Local licence fees: cities/counties may charge their own business-licence / zoning fees on top of state fees (for example Billings’ historic medical-dispensary business-licence regime; Bozeman city canopy/manufacturing/dispensary licences under Ordinance 2084, §3.4). No statewide schedule of local fees exists; each jurisdiction’s fee is Verify in its own code.

2.6 Revenue figures — official DOR totals, with calendar/fiscal conflicts flagged

Official DOR cumulative totals (the anchor figures)

DOR Cannabis Sales Reports page, headed “January 2022 to September 2026 Estimated Sales” / “Estimated Taxes”: Sales: Medical $262,969,775; Adult-Use $1,260,192,343; Total $1,523,162,119. Estimated state taxes: Medical $10,518,791; Adult-Use $252,038,469; Total $262,557,260. Source: DOR Sales Reports. Arithmetic check: 20% × $1,260,192,343 = $252,038,469 (exact); 4% × $262,969,775 = $10,518,791 (exact). The DOR “estimated taxes” are therefore the straight application of the headline rates to estimated sales, excluding local-option tax. Older DOR snapshot on the Legal-is-Legit page: “January 2022 to January 2025 Total Estimated Taxes: $161,832,814 (adult-use and medical sales).” Consistent with growth to $262.6m by Sept 2026.

Calendar-year sales and tax (DOR monthly tables, summed; secondary cross-checks)

  • 2022: ≈$304m total sales, roughly one-third medical (Montana Free Press via Marijuana Moment, 17 Jan 2026, citing DOR).
  • 2025: $326.9m total (adult-use $289.4m, medical $37.6m), up 0.8% on 2024; adult-use +4.2%, medical –19.5% (CannBus Q3 2026 report summing DOR monthly tables, Jan–Dec 2025). Montana Free Press / MJBizDaily (Jan 2026) give the same shape: $327m in 2025, 90% adult-use, “nearly $60m” in tax revenue; annual tax revenue up 44% between 2022 and 2025; over Jan 2022–Dec 2025, $217m in cannabis tax revenue, ≈$207m adult-use, <$10m medical (Free Press via Marijuana Moment, Marijuana Moment, 17 Jan 2026; MJBizDaily, 15 Jan 2026). Cross-check: 20% × $289.4m + 4% × $37.6m = $57.9m + $1.5m = $59.4m, matching “nearly $60m.”
  • Jan–Sept 2026: combined sales $249,241,584 (adult-use $225,104,336; medical $24,137,248), +2% vs Jan–Sept 2025 ($244.39m); adult-use +4.3%, medical –15.7% (Marijuana Herald, 12 Oct 2026, citing updated DOR figures, Marijuana Herald).
  • September 2026 (latest month in DOR tables): total $28,055,429 (adult-use $25,577,747; medical $2,477,682), –3.9% vs August 2026 ($29,200,209); recreational ≈91% of the month. July 2026 total was $29,992,180 (adult $27,283,061; medical $2,709,119).
  • June 2026 (for county context, §3.5): state total $27,860,848 (May $27,706,021) — Northern Plains Independent, 23 July 2026, citing DOR.
CONFLICT — MPP 2025 figure does not reconcile. The Marijuana Policy Project’s state tax-revenue table (accessed via search) lists Montana adult-use state tax revenue as 2024: $55,521,165; 2025: $74,570,927; total $223,717,664, and states “In its fourth full year of sales, 2025, Montana generated over $57 million in cannabis excise taxes, the highest yet” in its own text — the table’s $74.6m for 2025 contradicts both its own text and the DOR-derived ≈$59m. Source: MPP tax revenue table. Treatment: prefer the DOR-derived figure (≈$59–60m for 2025). The MPP $74.6m figure is flagged as conflicting / Unverified and must not be quoted without that flag. Possible explanations (fiscal-year vs calendar-year, inclusion of local-option or licence revenue, or a data error) were not resolved in this search. The DOR cumulative $262.6m to Sept 2026 is also arithmetically consistent with the Free Press $217m to Dec 2025 + ≈$46m in Jan–Sept 2026, which further supports the DOR series over the MPP table.

Fiscal-year figures (secondary, for completeness). CCD November 2025 briefing to the Legislature, as reported secondarily: FY2024 adult-use tax ≈$49.7m; medical tax ≈$2.1m (falling as cardholders shifted to adult-use). Fiscal-year and calendar-year figures are not interchangeable; do not mix them in one table without labelling. Source via search: Herring Bank, Montana Cannabis Banking (secondary, citing CCD briefing — Verify against the briefing itself).

2.7 Hemp product tax — none found

No Montana excise tax on hemp or hemp-derived products was located. Hemp in Montana is charged through licence fees, not a product tax: grower licence (MDA State Hemp Program): $850 for Category A/B-only growers; $1,100 for Category C growers (includes initial inspection/testing of first Category C sample); additional samples/inspections $250 each (MDA 2026 application / ARM §4.19.104: “The fee for a Montana State Hemp License is $1,100” with the category split in current application instructions). Sources: MDA Licensing Stage 1; ARM 4.19.104 (Cornell mirror). Processor licence: Part A (floral/root extracts) $2,500/calendar year (also allows grain/seed/oil/fibre); Part B (grain/seed/seed oil/fibre only) $1,000/calendar year. Source: MDA Hemp Processors. Tax consequence: a hemp-derived THC product that is sold through a CCD marijuana licensee is taxed as marijuana (20%/4% + any local option) because it is sold as marijuana/marijuana product. A product sold outside the CCD system has no Montana hemp excise tax located in this search — Verify whether any 2025 hemp-cannabinoid bill (for example HB 49, reported secondarily as barring non-FDA-approved hemp THC products) created a tax or fee; no tax provision was confirmed. Full hemp law is Part 1 / Part 7 territory and is not decided here. Montana’s lack of a general sales tax also means there is no general sales tax to apply to hemp retail sales.

2.8 IRS §280E and Montana decoupling — state relief since 2017; federal relief now medical-only

Federal baseline (given, not re-researched): split scheduling — state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Application to Montana: many Montana licensees serve both markets (since 1 July 2025, dispensaries in green counties may sell both medical and adult-use). Such businesses must split medical vs adult-use activity, revenue and expenses: the medical slice is outside federal §280E after 28 April 2026; the adult-use slice remains inside §280E (only cost of goods sold deductible federally). No federal cannabis banking law exists. Tribal medical programmes: whether a tribal programme counts as “state-licensed” for Schedule III purposes is marked Verify in the parent brief and is not decided here.

Montana state decoupling — YES, since 2017, by DOR interpretation (secondary sources; exact statutory citation Verify): “Montana’s tax code [generally] conforms with the Internal Revenue Code. However, the Montana Department of Revenue departed from the IRC in 2017 and allowed normal business deductions for licensed (legal) cannabis corporations. The Montana Department of Revenue also interpreted the law for pass-through entities and individuals with licensed cannabis operations to allow deductions of ordinary and necessary business expenses.” Source: Cannabis Industry Journal (citing DOR guidance; article undated in snippet, pre-2022 material republished). CannBus Q3 2026 legal summary repeats the point and adds the caution that DOR’s own page on the point “was not found” in its check: “Tax advisers report DOR guidance (December 2017) lets licensed cannabis businesses deduct ordinary business expenses on Montana returns… Confirm treatment with a tax adviser.” Source: CannBus Legal Summary Q3 2026. Treatment: Montana is treated here as decoupled for state income-tax purposes since 2017 (corporations and, by DOR interpretation, pass-throughs/individuals with licensed operations). That means Montana state relief did not depend on federal rescheduling: a Montana licensee already deducted ordinary/necessary expenses on its Montana return while still barred federally (for Schedule I activity). The April 2026 federal change therefore changes the federal return for the medical slice, not the Montana state position, and it does not change the federal position for adult-use. The precise MCA provision or DOR guidance document (December 2017) was not opened as an official page in this task — Verify the citation (commonly discussed in connection with Montana’s conformity statute and DOR’s cannabis guidance) before citing it in a filing or in Part 1. No Montana cannabis-specific state tax credit, and no statement that Montana “couples” to §280E, was found. Do not present Montana as a coupled state.

2.9 Pending / recent tax and licensing legislation (2025 session; Montana meets biennially)

Montana’s Legislature meets biennially in odd-numbered years. The next regular session is January 2027 (70th Legislature). Status below is as found in this search; final leg.mt.gov status for each bill should be re-checked before Run 2 is finalised.

  • HB 932 (2025) — ENACTED, effective 1 July 2025. Revises marijuana state special revenue distribution; creates Habitat Legacy Account. See §2.3. Sponsor Rep. Ken Walsh.
  • SB 27 (2025) — ENACTED, effective 1 July 2025. “Freeze the footprint”: moratorium on new licensed premises; CCD accepted applications to add licensed locations only if complete by 30 June 2025 (CCD recommended submission by 20 June 2025); incomplete applications denied effective 1 July 2025; new applications for previously unlicensed applicants/premises resume 1 July 2027 (DOR FAQ: “On or after July 1, 2027, the department shall begin accepting applications for and issuing licenses to cultivate, manufacture, or sell marijuana… to applicants who were not previously licensed and for premises not previously licensed.”). Sources: DOR SB 27 deadline; DOR FAQ.
  • SB 307 (2025, Sen. T. McGillvray) — FAILED / DIED IN PROCESS 23 May 2025. Would have restructured distribution (HEART to 40%, new Marijuana Prevention Account, Marijuana Law Enforcement Operations Account, Marijuana Tax Revenue Accountability Council, eliminating wildlife/recreation shares). Senate 2nd Reading passed as amended 30–20 on 20 Mar 2025; Senate Finance and Claims failed to pass 11–11 on 1 Apr 2025; died in process 23 May 2025. Not law. Do not present SB 307 percentages as current distribution. Source: LegiScan — SB 307.
  • SB 537 (2025, Sen. Daniel Zolnikov) — vetoed, NOT law (see Part 0.3 / Part 1.9). The Parts 2–3 track recorded SB 537 status as “Verify” on its own searches (it had passed its final legislative vote the same day as HB 932 but, as of 16–19 May 2025 reporting, had not been transmitted to the Governor; a secondary 2026 brief asserted a gubernatorial veto, not verified against leg.mt.gov in that track). The Parts 0–1 track, with LegiScan and Marijuana Herald (16 July 2025) sources, records: vetoed 6 June 2025; veto override failed 11 July 2025. The veto/override dates are used here; confirm on leg.mt.gov before Run 2 if a single citation is required. HB 932 is the enacted vehicle in any event.
  • HB 698 (2025) and HB 659 (2025) — status Verify. Both amend §16-12-301 (local-government marijuana authority / election requirements). Only amendment/bill-text PDFs were returned in search; neither is relied on here as enacted law.
  • SB 11 (2025) — Verify enactment. Enrolled-bill text returned in search amends §§16-12-301 and 16-12-311 as part of a general local-government ballot-issue revision (immediate effective date / applicability date in the bill). Confirm chapter / effective date on leg.mt.gov before citing its §16-12-301/311 language as current.

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 The county opt-in system — how “green” and “red” actually work

The default rule is the 2020 vote, not a county-by-county application. Under HB 701 (2021), codified principally at MCA §16-12-301, “Local government authority to regulate — opt-in requirement in certain counties — exemption for existing licensees”:

  • Green counties (default-permitted): a county in which the majority of voters voted to approve Initiative Measure No. 190 in the 3 November 2020 general election. In such a county, marijuana businesses are not subject to the local-approval process in §16-12-301(3)–(6). Adult-use sales began in green counties in January 2022. DOR FAQ: “In ‘green’ counties, where the majority of voters supported Initiative 190 in November 2020, adult-use sales began in January 2022.” DOR FAQ.
  • Red counties (default-prohibited for new businesses): a county in which the majority voted against I-190. “A marijuana business may not operate in a county in which the majority of voters voted against approval of Initiative Measure No. 190 in the November 3, 2020, general election until: (i) the category or categories of license that the marijuana business seeks has or have been approved by the local jurisdiction where the marijuana business intends to operate as provided in subsection (3) or (4); and (ii) the business is licensed by the department pursuant to this chapter.” (§16-12-301(1)(a), as quoted in 2025 bill texts amending the section.)
  • Categories that need local approval in a red county (§16-12-301(1)(d) as quoted in 2025 texts): (i) cultivator; (ii) manufacturer; (iii) medical marijuana dispensary (except as provided in (1)(b)); (iv) adult-use dispensary; (v) combined-use licensee; (vi) testing laboratory; (vii) marijuana transporter facility.
  • Former medical licensee exemption (§16-12-301(1)(b)–(c)): a former medical marijuana licensee that does not apply for adult-use licensure may continue to operate in its existing premises notwithstanding a local jurisdiction’s failure to act under (3)–(6); a former licensee that does apply for cultivator/manufacturer/adult-use dispensary/testing-lab licensure may likewise operate if it has remained in good standing. This is why medical sales can continue in red counties even where new adult-use businesses cannot open, and why a red county can show medical sales (for example Fergus, Sweet Grass, Beaverhead) without being “green.”
  • How a red county opts in: by local election held under Title 13, Chapter 1, Part 4, called by the county governing body or by elector petition. “If the qualified electors of a county vote to approve a type of marijuana business to be located in the jurisdiction, the governing body shall enter the approval into the records of the local government and notify the department of the election results.” (§16-12-301(5) as quoted in 2025 texts.) DOR FAQ: “In a county where the majority of voters opposed Initiative 190, adult-use sales will be allowed if that county holds an election and a majority of the voters choose to allow marijuana businesses to operate in that jurisdiction.”
  • How a green county (or city) opts out: by the same election mechanism, but in reverse. DOR FAQ: “In a county where the majority of voters supported Initiative 190, certain marijuana businesses could be prohibited if that county holds a local election and a majority of the voters choose to prohibit that type of business from operating.” A vote overturning approval / prohibiting a previously permitted operation is effective on the 90th day after the local election (§16-12-301(7) as quoted in 2025 SB 11 text) — hence Granite County’s June 2022 opt-out ending adult-use sales in September 2022 (see §3.2).
  • Cities within counties — the crucial distinction: the county vote sets the county default, but a city may vote separately on whether categories of businesses may operate within city limits, and a city may also be the jurisdiction that approves a category in a red county. Examples that prove the point: Billings (Yellowstone County, green county): Billings voters rejected adult-use dispensaries in city limits in Nov 2021 (55–45), while Yellowstone County as a whole stayed green and adult-use sales continued outside Billings city limits. Great Falls (Cascade County, green county): the city could not ban dispensaries by ordinance alone; it needed an election (district court, Oct 2022, §3.4). Voters then allowed sales in city limits in Nov 2022 (52–48). Town of Cascade (Cascade County): voters banned adult-use sales in town limits in Nov 2022, while Cascade County and Great Falls allowed them. Richey (Dawson County, green after Dec 2021): town voters banned sales 64–32 in Nov 2022, while Dawson County stayed green. Manhattan and West Yellowstone (Gallatin County, green): ban measures failed in Nov 2022 (Manhattan: 69% rejected the ban). Deer Lodge (Powell County, green): Nov 2022 voters gave a split, category-by-category result (testing labs, manufacturers and shops allowed; cultivation/transport not — details Verify, §3.4).
  • What local governments can do WITHOUT an election (green and red alike): under §16-12-301(2), to protect public health, safety or welfare, a city council or county commission may pass ordinances — for example zoning regulations for types of businesses, distances from churches and schools, and smoking in public places. Legislative Services Division summary (Nov 2021): “city councils and county commissions may pass ordinances, for example zoning regulations for types of businesses, distances from churches and schools, and smoking in public places (16-12-301(2)). They may also ask whether voters want to prohibit specific categories of businesses, like dispensaries, cultivators, or manufacturers (16-12-301(7)).” Source: Legislative Services Division, Nov 2021.
  • What local governments CANNOT do: (a) in a green county, prohibit a category of business by ordinance alone — that requires a voter election (Great Falls case, §3.4). (b) In any county, prohibit transportation of marijuana within or through the jurisdiction on public roads by a licensed transporter or as otherwise allowed by Chapter 12 (§16-12-301(8) as quoted in 2025 texts).
  • State baseline that local rules sit on top of: MCA §16-12-207(3)(a)(iii) — DOR shall deny a cultivator, manufacturer, adult-use or medical dispensary licence if the premises are “within 500 feet of and on the same street as a building used exclusively as a church, synagogue, or other place of worship or as a school or postsecondary school other than a commercially operated school, unless the locality requires a greater distance,” measured in a straight line from centre of nearest entrance to centre of nearest entrance; renewal exception if the premises predated the church/school on the same street. ARM §42.39.124 adds a three-part test (same street address + same-street entrances + ≤500 ft straight-line); if any part fails, §16-12-207 does not apply; and “in the event that a county or city government should enact an ordinance or resolution supplanting the provisions of 16-12-207, MCA, the restriction shall not apply” (that is, the local rule replaces, not adds to, the state same-street test where it supplants it — Verify application to any specific site). Official MCA text opened: MCA §16-12-207 (2022). ARM text via Cornell mirror: ARM 42.39.124. Dispensary hours (state): adult-use and medical dispensaries “may not operate between the hours of 8 p.m. and 9 a.m. daily” (§16-12-207(11)); single secured patron entrance (§16-12-207(9)(a)).
  • Terminology — “blue” is not a third sales status: CCD uses “blue” only for tax overlay: red = adult-use sales prohibited; green = adult-use sales permitted; blue (CCD map key) = county voters opted to include a local-option tax on cannabis sales; red with blue stripes = approved a local-option tax but prohibits adult-use sales (in practice, a medical-only tax: Beaverhead, Granite); green with blue stripes = applies a local-option tax only to adult-use sales (in practice: Flathead, Missoula, Ravalli, Sanders). Source: DOR FAQ (colour key quoted in §3.3).

3.2 Every county’s current green/red status, with every opt-in/opt-out election that could be verified

Reading the table. “Current” = as evidenced by DOR county sales for September 2026, cross-checked against December 2025 and earlier county tables and election reporting. A county is GREEN if adult-use sales are lawful there (whether or not a dispensary happened to report sales in September 2026 — Deer Lodge County is the one such case, explained below). A county is RED if adult-use sales are prohibited there (medical sales may still occur via grandfathered licensees). “Sept 2026 adult sales” is the DOR estimated figure for that month; “$0” means DOR reported zero; “(blank)” means DOR suppressed the figure for confidentiality (fewer licensees — DOR footnote: “Not disclosed due to confidentiality concerns”), not zero.

County-by-county election history that is dated in sources

  1. Dawson County — RED (2020) → GREEN (Dec 2021). Rejected I-190 in 2020 by 53.6% (Marijuana Moment / MJBizDaily: “originally rejected I-190 in 2020, with 54% voting no”). Opted in by special election in December 2021, 55% approving recreational sales (MJBizDaily, May 2022; Marijuana Moment, July 2022: “Dawson County became ‘green’ after a special election in December, with 55 percent of voters approving”). As of July 2022 reporting it was described as “the only county to opt into adult-use cannabis regulation after turning it down.” Sept 2026: adult $591,906; tax 3%/3%.
  2. Granite County — GREEN (2020) → RED (June 2022), opt-back-in FAILED (Nov 2022). Approved I-190 in 2020 by nearly 55% (≈10-point margin). 7 June 2022 primary: voters opted out (53% for the ban, per Nov 2022 preview reporting; “first county to reverse from green to red”). Adult-use sales ended 4 September 2022 (90 days after the election, consistent with §16-12-301). The county’s sole dispensary (Top Shelf Botanicals, Philipsburg) then petitioned for a re-vote; 8 Nov 2022: voters narrowly rejected re-legalisation, 52–48, so Granite stayed red. Granite’s Nov 2022 tax votes are flagged in §2.4. Sept 2026: adult $0; tax medical 3% only. Sources: MTPR, 11 Oct 2022; Valley Press, 14 Nov 2022.
  3. Yellowstone County — GREEN throughout; county opt-out FAILED (7 June 2022). Yellowstone narrowly approved I-190 in 2020 (reported as 50–49). County commissioners unanimously put a county-wide opt-out on the June 2022 ballot after Billings’ city vote (below). Result: opt-out failed — voters kept recreational sales. Two percentages appear in sources: “55 percent voting in favor of keeping recreational sales” (Marijuana Moment, July 2022) vs “58% of voters opposed [the ban]” (MJBizDaily, Nov 2022). Verify exact canvass percentage; the outcome (stay green) is not in doubt. Sept 2026: adult $4,099,733 (largest county total that month); tax 3%/3%.
  4. Billings (city, Yellowstone County) — adult-use dispensaries banned in city limits (Nov 2021), county unaffected. Nov 2021: Billings voters rejected a measure to allow recreational stores in city limits 55–45. The 2021 Billings zoning ordinance that followed allowed only medical dispensaries in city limits (Heavy Commercial CX, Light Industrial I1, Heavy Industrial I2), with adult-use/combined-use prohibited, plus a 1,000-ft separation (see §3.4). Verify whether any later Billings city vote has reversed the 2021 city ban; no reversal was found in this search, and Billings’ city status does not change Yellowstone County’s green status.
  5. Custer County — RED → GREEN (5 Nov 2024). Feb 2024 DOR table: Custer adult $0, medical $131,891, tax None/None. Nov 2024 ballot included both an adult-use-sales question and a 3% tax question (see §2.4). Result: 58% of Custer County voters in favour of opening the county to the recreational market (Miles City Star, Jan/Feb 2025). Adult-use sales began at the start of 2025; Miles City Ordinance 1388 (special council meeting, 30 Dec 2024) added recreational dispensaries to the existing medical-dispensary regulations (Highway Commercial, 1,000-ft setback) as an interim measure. Sept 2026: adult $277,402; tax 3%/3%.
  6. Great Falls (city, Cascade County) — ban attempt FAILED by court, then voters ALLOWED sales (8 Nov 2022, 52–48). See §3.4 for the court case. Valley Press: “In Great Falls, residents voted by a 52-48 margin to allow cannabis sales within city limits.” Cascade County itself never opted out.
  7. Town of Cascade (Cascade County) — BANNED in town limits (8 Nov 2022). “The small town of Cascade, on the other hand, voted overwhelmingly to ban adult-use sales” (Valley Press, Nov 2022), while Great Falls and Cascade County allowed them. Verify the town’s exact percentage.
  8. Richey (town, Dawson County) — BANNED in town limits (Nov 2022, 64–32). Dawson County Clerk and Recorder confirmation via Valley Press: “Sixty-four residents supported the ban, and 32 opposed it.”
  9. Manhattan (Gallatin County) and West Yellowstone (Gallatin County) — ban measures FAILED (Nov 2022). Manhattan: 69% rejected the ban. West Yellowstone: ban failed (percentage Verify).
  10. Deer Lodge (city, Powell County) — SPLIT category vote (Nov 2022). Valley Press: “Deer Lodge gave testing laboratories, manufacturers and shops (including combined-use licenses…) a green light, but decided against allowing any other cultivation or transport businesses within city limits,” with the cultivator measure “separated by only two votes” and possibly headed for recount at the time of reporting. Verify the final canvass category by category; Powell County itself is green and Deer Lodge County (Anaconda) is separately green (see table).
  11. Other city/county votes reported as attempted but not as flips: opt-out efforts were reported as “under way” in 2022 in Cascade, Carbon, Ravalli and Flathead counties (Cannabis Industry Journal, 2022) — none resulted in a county flip evidenced in the Sept 2026 DOR table (all four remain green: Cascade, Carbon, Ravalli, Flathead all show adult-use sales in Sept 2026). No other county flip (green→red or red→green) between 2020 and Sept 2026 was evidenced beyond Dawson (in), Granite (out), and Custer (in). That sentence must be read with the caveat in the next paragraph.
CONFLICT on the original 2020 count — flagged, not smoothed over. MTPR (Jan 2022) stated adult-use sales were allowed in “the 29 counties that voted in favor of the ballot initiative.” A secondary early-2022 list (Sterling CRE Advisors) names 26 green counties (Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Cascade, Deer Lodge, Flathead, Gallatin, Glacier, Granite, Hill, Jefferson, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Lincoln, Madison, Mineral, Missoula, Park, Powell, Ravalli, Richland, Roosevelt, Rosebud, Sanders, Sheridan, Silver Bow) and lists Yellowstone, Custer, Dawson, Valley and others as red — a list that is demonstrably incomplete/confused on Yellowstone (county green, city ban) and predates the Dawson/Custer flips. Arithmetic from the current table (29 green today = original green − Granite + Dawson + Custer) implies 28 original green counties, not 29. Verify the official Secretary of State county-by-county I-190 canvass (3 Nov 2020) before stating an original-green count or an original-green list as fact. The current 29/27 split below does not depend on resolving that conflict, because it rests on DOR sales + the three dated flips.

What “$0 / $0” does and does not mean: in a red county, $0 adult sales is expected (prohibited). $0 medical sales in the same month does not prove medical sales are prohibited — it may mean no grandfathered medical licensee operates there that month, or sales were suppressed. Conversely, a blank (Beaverhead, Deer Lodge, Fergus, Sweet Grass, Wheatland) does not mean zero; DOR suppresses it for confidentiality. For Fergus and Sweet Grass, earlier DOR tables (Jan 2024) prove medical sales existed; for Beaverhead, Jan 2024 proves medical sales ($36,826) with adult $0; for Wheatland, no earlier-month figure was opened in this task — Verify.

Cities vs counties — a standing warning for Part 3 users: a county’s green status does not guarantee a dispensary may open in every city inside it, and a city ban does not change the county’s colour. Any site decision must check (1) county colour, (2) city vote/ordinance for that city, and (3) the state 500-ft same-street rule / any greater local distance.

3.3 The official CCD list of green and red counties — what exists and what does not

Official CCD source tried: CCD FAQ page: revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/faqs — section “Local and County Cannabis Regulation FAQ” states: “Please refer to the attached Montana map. The map reflects the current status of each county, as determined by the results of Initiative 190 or other local election held after HB-701 was signed into law by the Montana legislature.” Colour key (quoted in §3.1) follows. Full-size map link from that page: CCD Local Option Map (PNG).

Result: PARTIALLY RETRIEVABLE, NOT USABLE AS A TEXT LIST. The FAQ page text was opened successfully on 1 Nov 2026, and the map image URL is exposed on that page, but the map itself is a PNG image with no extractable text table in a text fetch. The CCD does not, on that page, publish a companion CSV, XLSX, PDF table, or HTML list of counties by colour that was located in this search. Therefore: no verbatim CCD text list can be quoted here, and the §3.2 table must be described as reconstructed from DOR county sales + election reporting, checked against the CCD map’s colour key — not as a transcription of a CCD list. Anyone needing a citable CCD list should (a) view the map image at the URL above, (b) request the underlying county-status dataset from CCD/DOR (phone (406) 444-6900, as printed on DOR cannabis pages), or (c) use the DOR county sales tables as the auditable proxy, as this report does.

Exact URLs tried for a CCD list (all accessed 1 Nov 2026): FAQ — SUCCESS (text, map key, no county table). Map PNG — image link exposed on the FAQ page; text extraction not possible. Sales Reports — SUCCESS (state totals + local-option explanation). September 2026 county sales — SUCCESS (county sales + local-option columns; used as the proxy list). No separate “green counties list” page, dashboard, or download was located on revenue.mt.gov in this search. If CCD publishes one under a different path, it was not found — Verify by site search on revenue.mt.gov for “green counties” before Run 2 is finalised.

Secondary map/list sources (not official, for cross-check only): Glacier Country tourism (“all eight of Glacier Country's counties (Flathead, Glacier, Lake, Lincoln, Mineral, Missoula, Ravalli and Sanders)” are green — Glacier Country) matches the §3.2 table for all eight. MTPR Jan 2022 (“29 counties that voted in favor”) conflicts on the original count (see §3.2) and must not be used to override the current table.

3.4 Local zoning, buffer rules and licence fees — required jurisdictions

State floor (applies everywhere unless a locality lawfully requires more): 500 ft, same street, church/synagogue/place of worship or school/postsecondary school (non-commercial), straight-line entrance-to-entrance, §16-12-207(3)(a)(iii) + ARM §42.39.124 three-part test; locality may require a greater distance. Dispensary hours 9 a.m.–8 p.m. Single secured patron entrance. A local ordinance filed with DOR that conflicts with a proposed premises’ zone is itself a ground for DOR denial (§16-12-207(3)(a)(i)).

Yellowstone County / Billings (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. City of Billings: adult-use dispensaries prohibited in city limits following the Nov 2021 city vote (55–45 against allowing them); medical dispensaries allowed. 2021 Billings zoning ordinance (amending Billings City Code §§27-1000, 27-1005, 27-1006, 27-1400, 27-1800; adopted on second reading 8 Nov 2021, as amended by striking adult-use/combined-use language): medical dispensaries designated “PR” (permitted with restrictions) in Heavy Commercial (CX), Light Industrial (I1) and Heavy Industrial (I2) only; adult-use and combined-use dispensaries prohibited. Standards (§27-1005.R): 1,000-ft separation from religious institutions, primary/secondary educational facilities, public parks containing a children’s playground or playfield, day-care centres, youth centres, and neighbourhood residential zones (plus behavioural-health facilities as added on first reading 25 Oct 2021), measured from exterior of occupied dispensary building space to property line of the protected use; no reduction, adjustment or variance permitted. Zoning Commission had recommended 600 ft (religious/schools/parks) and 350 ft (residential) plus CMU1/CMU2 districts; Council did not adopt those reductions. Sources: Billings ordinance PDF via Billings ordinance (PDF); Legislative Services summary (Nov 2021) via Legislative Services, Nov 2021 (“Billings: Cultivators are capped at 7,500 square feet, or Tier 4 operation. Approved marijuana businesses allowed only in industrial and heavy commercial zones, and sit at least 1,000 feet from neighborhoods, schools, churches, parks, addiction recovery centers and youth centers. Medical marijuana dispensaries limited to eight in city limits with applicants entering a lottery for licenses.” — the 8-dispensary lottery cap is the 2021 summary; Verify whether the cap/lottery survives in current Billings code, and whether the adult-use prohibition survives any later city vote.) Laurel (Yellowstone County city): Laurel Municipal Code §17.08.761 prohibits medical marijuana dispensaries within 1,000 ft of schools, childcare, parks, places of worship and correctional facilities (as cited in a Feb 2026 Laurel community letter). Verify in current Laurel code; cited here only as a secondary reference to the section. Yellowstone County (unincorporated): no county-wide marijuana-specific zoning ordinance was located in this search beyond the state floor and the county’s I-190 green status. Verify in Yellowstone County zoning regulations before siting outside city limits.

Missoula County / City of Missoula. County: GREEN, adult-only tax 3%. City: the most elaborately documented local regime in the state. 2021 baseline (Legislative Services, Nov 2021): “Missoula: Updated ordinance includes a 500-foot buffer between dispensaries, ban on nontransparent glass storefronts; ban on home cultivation or manufacturing as a legally allowed home occupation.” City Title 20 Zoning Ordinance (2021 white paper, City of Missoula Planning): marijuana businesses in B, C and M (business/commercial/industrial) districts; 500-ft buffer between dispensaries (measured straight-line, centre of customer entrance to centre of customer entrance; pre-existing dispensaries within 500 ft treated as legal non-conforming under Title 20.80.040); 500-ft buffer to churches/schools as recommended; single customer entrance; 8 p.m. close per state law. Design standards (window transparency) in Title 20, §20.40. Source: Missoula Title 20 white paper. 2024 local moratorium: Missoula imposed a moratorium on new dispensaries opening within city limits in 2024, also barring existing dispensaries from relocating within the city; reported sunset August 2026 (secondary: Cholewa Substack, 2025, and CannBus Q3 2026). Verify current status as of 1 Nov 2026 — the sunset date has passed, but whether the moratorium was extended, lifted, or replaced by the 1,000-ft proposal below was not confirmed on an official City of Missoula page in this search. Jan 2026 proposal (pending as reported): City Council approved on 21 Jan (year reported as 2026 in secondary coverage; Kaimin article) an amendment to require dispensaries to be more than 1,000 ft from each other, churches, schools and various government facilities, up from 500 ft; existing dispensaries grandfathered. The wider zoning-code reform package was still pending final adoption as of the secondary reports. Verify in current Missoula Title 20 before relying on 500 ft vs 1,000 ft for a new site. Sources: Cholewa Substack; Montana Kaimin. Missoula County (unincorporated): no separate county marijuana zoning ordinance was located; the city regime above is the operative local layer for most sites. Verify for unincorporated sites.

Gallatin County / Bozeman (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. Gallatin County (unincorporated): No marijuana-specific restriction as of 12 Jan 2026. Gallatin County Planning memo to DOR (Sean O’Callaghan, Chief Planning Officer, 12 Jan 2026): “At the present time, Gallatin County has not adopted any regulations, ordinances, or resolutions that specifically restrict the cultivation, processing, or selling of marijuana or marijuana products in unincorporated Gallatin County. When such uses are proposed, they are reviewed on a site-specific basis for compliance with any applicable zoning rules in the exact same manner that any other land use is reviewed.” The county also states it will no longer provide individual compliance letters to DOR applicants. Source: Gallatin County marijuana memo, 12 Jan 2026 (PDF). City of Bozeman — Ordinance 2084 (generally revising marijuana laws; Bozeman Municipal Code Ch. 16, Art. 8, replaced in full; zoning provisions in Ch. 38, incl. §38.360.180): city licences required on top of state licences: canopy licence (cultivator), manufacturing licence, dispensary licence. Only indoor cultivation permitted in the city; plants not visible by normal unaided vision from a public place; plants in a secured, locked space. No on-premises consumption/smoking at a dispensary in the city. Buffer (Legislative Services, Nov 2021 summary of the then-current ordinance): “Bozeman: Updated ordinance to implement 500 feet between dispensaries and schools and places of worship (current ordinance is 400 feet). Existing dispensaries are grandfathered in. New ordinance requires cultivation, manufacturing, and testing facilities to have an air vent system to control discharge of particulates and odors.” Historic cap: Bozeman formerly capped medical dispensaries at 20, first-come/first-served (Billings staff report, Sept 2021, summarising Bozeman practice); the same report notes Bozeman would “likely eliminate all caps and amend their ordinances to treat all marijuana sales equally” and that businesses were “restricted to only certain zones and prohibited in the downtown area.” Verify whether the 20-cap and the downtown prohibition survive in current Bozeman code — do not quote them as current without that check. Sources: Bozeman Ordinance 2084; Legislative Services, Nov 2021. Other Gallatin County cities: Manhattan and West Yellowstone ban votes failed Nov 2022 (§3.2); Belgrade’s historic interim marijuana ordinances (2010s) are not relied on here. Verify current Belgrade code if siting there.

Flathead County / Kalispell (city). County: GREEN, adult-only tax 3%. City of Kalispell zoning (Kalispell City Code, Ch. 27): marijuana dispensaries by Administrative Conditional Use Permit (CUP) in B-1 Neighbourhood Business, B-2 General Business, B-3 Core Area Business, B-4 Central Business, B-5 Industrial-Business, I-1 Light Industrial and I-2 Heavy Industrial districts. Marijuana cultivation and manufacturing by Administrative CUP in I-1 and I-2 only (cultivation/manufacturing not listed as CUP uses in the B districts in the code table returned). Planning Board recommendation (Sept 2021, reported by Hagadorn News Network): dispensaries in business and industrial zones (like liquor stores, Administrative CUP); cultivation/manufacturing in all industrial zones; 300-ft buffer from churches, schools, parks, and city residential (R or RA) zones — this is a recommendation figure; the adopted code’s buffer (if any beyond the state 500-ft same-street rule) was not confirmed in the code excerpts returned. Verify the adopted Kalispell buffer in current Ch. 27 before siting. Sources: Kalispell code excerpts; Hagadorn News Network, Sept 2021. Flathead County (unincorporated) — draft, not adopted, as of the documents returned: draft Text Amendment (Flathead County Planning & Zoning) would allow “marijuana, integrated facility” (dispensary + cultivation canopy ≤2,500 sq ft in B-1/B-3/B-5/B-6/BS/BM-2 as conditional use; ≤10,000 sq ft in B-2/B-4 as permitted use) and “marijuana operations” as permitted in I-1, all indoors in a fully enclosed permanent structure, no outdoor storage, with negative-air-pressure and lighting/byproduct standards. A Planning Board workshop was held (2026 reporting: “business owners… may soon see a shift… Currently, county zoning regulations prohibit cannabis cultivation in business-designated areas, restricting those zones to retail sales only. Currently… cultivation is only permitted in agricultural and certain residential zones, including AG, SAG, R-2.5, and R-1”). A Board of Commissioners public workshop on the proposed Marijuana Zoning Text Amendment was noticed for 4 Nov 2026, 9:00 a.m., South Campus Building, Kalispell — that is, after the 1 Nov 2026 access date for this report. Sources: Flathead County draft text (PDF); Flathead County draft documents. Treatment: do not present the Flathead County draft as current law. Current unincorporated-County cultivation zoning (AG/SAG/R-2.5/R-1 only, retail only in business zones) is as reported secondarily in 2026; Verify in the adopted Flathead County Zoning Regulations. Columbia Falls, Whitefish, Bigfork (Flathead County cities): no marijuana-specific ordinance was located in this search. Verify in each city’s code; do not assume Kalispell’s code applies outside Kalispell.

Cascade County / Great Falls (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. Cascade County Commission (Feb 2021, unanimous) limited dispensary zoning to two I-2 heavy industrial zones outside Great Falls city limits — a restriction that was itself litigated (see Yatsko below). City of Great Falls — the leading case on green-county bans: the city refused to process a Safety Inspection Certificate for Green Creek Dispensary (Janelle and Dale Yatsko) to open inside city limits, relying on a 2010 ordinance banning businesses selling federally illegal products and on federal illegality. District Court Judge David J. Grubich ruled 11 Oct 2022 (reported 17 Oct 2022) that the city’s ordinance was not a valid regulation and the city must process the paperwork, because in a green county a ban requires a voter election under §16-12-301. The city then put a ban referendum on the 8 Nov 2022 ballot; voters allowed sales in city limits, 52–48 (see §3.2). Sources: MTPR, 17 Oct 2022; MTPR, 29 June 2022. Current Great Falls zoning/buffer/fee schedule after the Nov 2022 vote was not located in this search. Verify in current Great Falls code (Title 17 / business-licence provisions) before siting. The Feb 2021 county I-2 limitation and any later county amendment should likewise be checked in current Cascade County regulations.

Lewis and Clark County / Helena (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. Helena Valley (unincorporated Lewis and Clark County) Zoning Regulations (adopted 19 Nov 2020, Res. 2020-97; amended 12 July 2022, Res. 2022-51) list “Marijuana Dispensary” (§704.27), “Marijuana Grow Operation” (§704.28) and “Marijuana Provider” (§704.29) as land-use categories in the use tables. The buffer/district permissions for those uses in the Helena Valley regulations were not extracted in this search — Verify in the full Helena Valley regulations for the specific district. Source: Helena Valley Zoning Regulations, 12 July 2022 (PDF). City of Helena: City Commission, 8 May 2023, gave first passage (5–0) to an ordinance amending Helena City Code Title 11, Chapter 2, §§11-2-3 (Table 1), 11-2-4 (land-use definitions) and 11-2-5 (supplemental requirements) “as they relate to marijuana sales and operations,” with discussion of grandfathering, proximity to city parks/facilities, security for cultivation, and existing locations near schools. The final-passage text and any buffer distance in the adopted Helena ordinance were not located in this search. Verify in current Helena City Code Title 11 before siting in Helena. Do not quote a Helena buffer distance from this report. Source: Helena Commission minutes, 8 May 2023 (PDF).

Butte–Silver Bow (consolidated city-county). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. No enacted comprehensive marijuana zoning/licensing ordinance was located as current law in this search. What exists is a drafting history: July 2025 (Judiciary Committee, 23 July, reported): County Attorney’s draft ordinance to regulate sale/distribution, cap retail outlets at 10, add a 500-ft radius buffer from schools/churches/parks (with grandfather clause), was held for further zoning-language work; no ordinance enacted at that session. Nov 2025 (Judiciary Committee, 13 Nov, reported): County Attorney Matt Enruth requested authorisation to draft a single “clean” ordinance; committee voted to note/place Communication 2025-310 on file; staff to resubmit for three readings and a public hearing. No adoption recorded in the material returned. A federal case illustrates the state rule in operation: Broughton, Inc. v. City and County of Butte–Silver Bow, No. 2:25-cv-00065-BMM (D. Mont., Order on Motion for Summary Judgment, 12 Mar 2026) — a proposed dispensary at 301 South Arizona Ave., Butte, ~475 ft from a Butte High School welding-classroom entrance, was held not to have a vested property interest where BSB had not issued the business licence and state/local requirements (including §16-12-207 proximity concerns) were not met. The case turns on its facts and on BSB’s existing ordinances; it does not enact a 500-ft radius rule. Sources (secondary summaries of committee action + court order): Citizen Portal, July 2025; Citizen Portal, Nov 2025; Court order (PDF). Treatment: Butte–Silver Bow’s cap (10) and 500-ft radius are proposed, not adopted, on the evidence found. Site decisions in Butte must check current Butte–Silver Bow Municipal Code and the state 500-ft same-street rule, not the draft.

Ravalli County / Hamilton (city). County: GREEN, adult-only tax 3%. Ravalli County itself does not license businesses in unincorporated areas: Ravalli County Commissioners’ letter to DOR (7 Mar 2023, Chairman Dan Huls): “Ravalli County, Montana… does not license businesses within our jurisdiction. Please waive the requirement for all licensees within Ravalli County, outside of any municipal jurisdiction (Darby, Hamilton, Stevensville and Pinesdale).” Source: Ravalli County letter, 7 Mar 2023. Hamilton (and Darby, Stevensville, Pinesdale): historic interim marijuana zoning ordinances for Hamilton (2010–2011 medical era) were returned in search but are not relied on as current adult-use law. Verify current Hamilton / Ravalli County zoning for adult-use siting; no current Hamilton buffer or fee was verified in this search. Ravalli County was among counties where a 2022 opt-out effort was reported as under way; no flip occurred (county remains green, Sept 2026 adult sales $707,878).

Lake County / Polson (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. City of Polson Ordinance No. 2021-003 (defining/establishing standards for marijuana use/sale in Polson under I-190, effective 1 Jan 2022) sets, for marijuana businesses in Polson city limits, straight-line distances (property line of the protected use to nearest point of the marijuana business, disregarding intervening structures): 300 linear ft from property zoned residential; 500 linear ft from a church/synagogue/place of worship; 500 linear ft from any building/facility/park owned or operated by the City of Polson; 500 linear ft from another marijuana business; 1,000 linear ft from all public/private K–12 schools (excluding home schools, per §20-5-402 / Lake County Superintendent listing) and all private day-care facilities registered with the State. The ordinance recites §16-12-207’s rule that “a locality may require marijuana businesses be a greater distance from schools and places of worship then required by state law” and directs the City Manager / City-County Planning Board to forward development-code text amendments identifying zones for each marijuana-business type. Source: Polson Ordinance 2021-003. Verify whether Ordinance 2021-003’s distances survive in the current Polson Development Code as amended, and which zones now permit each business type; the 2021 ordinance is the last full text located in this search.

Park County / Livingston (city). County: GREEN, tax 3%/3%. Park County was in the first local-tax wave (Nov 2021, both channels). No Park County marijuana-specific zoning ordinance was located in this search; Livingston’s current marijuana zoning/buffer was not located. Custer County / Miles City (city). County: GREEN since Nov 2024, tax 3%/3% (see §§2.4, 3.2). City of Miles City — interim regulations (Ordinance 1388, special council meeting 30 Dec 2024): recreational dispensaries added to the existing (medical) regulations in Chapter 24 of the Miles City Municipal Code; businesses restricted to Highway Commercial Districts; ≥1,000 ft linear distance, building-to-building, from public/private schools, parks, playgrounds or religious institutions. Proposed permanent ordinance (Ordinance 1391, Zoning Commission recommendation, first reading passed Feb 2025, 5–1 on amended first reading): would have limited dispensaries to Highway Commercial, Highway Commercial/Light Industrial and Highway Industrial districts (later amended proposal: Highway Industrial only, 1,000 ft from schools/childcare/churches/parks, 300 ft from residential areas/parks). Ordinance 1391 FAILED on second reading 11 Mar 2025 (unanimous vote against), sending the matter back to the drawing board. Interim Ordinance 1388 was stated to expire 1 July 2025 unless extended (City Attorney Dan Rice, Mar 2025 meeting). Verify what, if anything, replaced interim Ordinance 1388 after 1 July 2025 — the Miles City regime after that date is Unavailable in this search and must not be assumed to be the 1,000-ft Highway Commercial rule.

Phillips (Malta) — RED, no tax, $0/$0. Valley (Glasgow) — GREEN, 3%/3%. Daniels (Scobey) — RED, no tax, $0/$0. Sheridan (Plentywood) — GREEN, 3%/3%.
