Other required counties — zoning status summary (where no specific ordinance was located): Cascade (Great Falls) — see Great Falls case above; current code Verify. Lake (Polson) — see Polson ordinance above. Park, Hill, Custer, Dawson, Richland — see entries above; Miles City is the only one of this group with a located, dated, specific buffer (interim, expired/Verify). For every jurisdiction in this section where the entry ends “Verify,” the correct reading is: the state floor (500-ft same-street rule, 9 a.m.–8 p.m., single entrance) plus any filed local ordinance that DOR holds on record for that premises is what DOR will apply; the absence of a located local ordinance in this search is not proof that no local ordinance exists.

Local licence fees — general finding: Montana has no statewide schedule of city/county marijuana licence fees. Examples of local fees that do exist in the material returned are city business-licence fees generally (for example Bozeman city canopy/manufacturing/dispensary licences under Ordinance 2084 — fee amounts not stated in the ordinance excerpts returned; Verify in Bozeman’s fee schedule) and Billings’ historic medical-dispensary business-licence regime. Any Part 3 user who needs a fee for a specific city must pull that city’s current fee schedule; do not apply another city’s fee by analogy.

3.5 Business concentration — ONLY as official sources state it

What DOR publishes: monthly estimated sales by county (adult-use, medical, total) and the local-option tax columns. DOR does not, on the pages opened, publish dispensaries-per-capita by county, licensees-per-county counts in the sales tables, or a concentration ranking. Dispensaries-per-capita by county is therefore Unavailable from official sources in this search and is not estimated or ranked here, as instructed.

What can be stated from the official September 2026 county sales table alone (no ranking computed by this report beyond quoting the table’s figures): Source: September 2026 county sales. State total, Sept 2026: adult-use and medical combined are given in secondary reporting as $28,055,429 for the month (see §2.6); the county table’s disclosed counties sum to $27,751,064; the $304,365 gap to the $28,055,429 state total is the five confidentiality-suppressed counties (Beaverhead, Deer Lodge, Fergus, Sweet Grass, Wheatland). Largest county totals in the table that month (quoted, not ranked by this report as a per-capita finding): Yellowstone $4,537,037 (adult $4,099,733; medical $437,304); Gallatin $4,315,115 (adult $4,032,155; medical $282,960); Missoula $3,207,762 (adult $2,888,370; medical $319,392); Flathead $2,947,449 (adult $2,690,352; medical $257,097); Cascade $1,848,506; Lewis and Clark $1,763,056; Silver Bow $1,085,501. Smallest reported non-zero county totals that month include: Sheridan $92,020; Powell $97,213; Mineral $110,729; Valley $158,073; Blaine $159,518; Sanders $159,850 — all green counties with small populations and correspondingly small absolute sales. Counties reporting $0/$0 in Sept 2026 (23 counties listed in §3.2, excluding blanks) are, with one exception (Granite, which has medical licensees but reported $0 that month — possibly a reporting/confidentiality artefact, Verify), red counties with no operating dispensary reporting that month. $0 must not be read as “no licensee ever” — for example Custer reported $0 adult in Feb 2024 while medical licensees operated, before its Nov 2024 flip. June 2026 county picture (Northern Plains Independent, citing DOR, July 2026): “Counties with the highest total of sales during June included Yellowstone at $4,558,783, Gallatin at $4,192,801, Missoula at $3,252,607 and Flathead at $2,804,575.” Roosevelt County June: $708,657 adult-use, $759,592 total; Richland County June: $765,973 adult-use, $792,940 total. Source: Northern Plains Independent, 23 July 2026.

Secondary statements about concentration (labelled as secondary, not official): Herring Bank (Oct 2026, citing CCD’s Nov 2025 legislative briefing and DOR county reports): “County sales remain concentrated in the larger population centers: in July 2026, Yellowstone County dispensaries reported about $4.76 million in sales and Gallatin County about $4.56 million, according to the Department of Revenue’s county sales report.” And: “since July 1, 2025, all dispensaries in ‘green’ counties… may sell both medical and adult-use marijuana. Dispensaries in ‘red’ counties may make medical sales only.” Source: Herring Bank, Oct 2026. MJBizDaily (May 2022, secondary): “Yellowstone County accounted for 16% of the total recreational sales in the first quarter [of 2022].” That is a Q1 2022 share, not a current share, and must not be updated by arithmetic in this report. No official DOR statement of the form “X dispensaries per 10,000 residents in County Y” was located. Any per-capita or “most saturated county” claim in other secondary material (including pre-legalisation Missoula per-capita claims from the medical era) is not carried forward as a current official finding.

Population context (for the reader, not a concentration calculation): the green counties contain the large majority of Montana’s population (Yellowstone, Gallatin, Missoula, Flathead, Cascade, Lewis and Clark, Ravalli, Silver Bow, etc.), while most red counties are small rural counties — which is why a map that is roughly half red by county count is not half red by population or by sales. No population figure is computed here because the task forbids self-estimated rankings; the point is made qualitatively only, consistent with 2022 reporting that “more than 90% of Montana’s population has access to recreational marijuana sales in their home counties despite a half red map” (secondary, 2022 — dated, do not update).

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

Regulator naming note, used throughout: the licensing regulator is the Cannabis Control Division (CCD) within the Cannabis and Alcohol Regulation Division (CARD) of the Montana Department of Revenue (DOR). Older pages say “Cannabis Control Division”; current DOR pages say CARD. They are the same DOR function for this report. Statute is MCA Title 16, Chapter 12, the Montana Marijuana Regulation and Taxation Act. Rules are ARM Title 42, Chapter 39.

4.1 The licence list in statute

MCA 16-12-104(2)(a), as amended by SB 27 (2025), lists the licence types the department shall issue: (i) cultivator licence; (ii) manufacturer licence; (iii) adult-use dispensary licence or a medical marijuana dispensary licence; (iv) testing laboratory licence; (v) marijuana transporter licence; (vi) combined-use marijuana licence. Source: SB 27 enrolled bill, 69th Legislature 2025, SB 27 enrolled PDF; CCD FAQ, DOR FAQ. MCA 16-12-104(2)(b) adds that the department “may establish other license types, subtypes, endorsements, and restrictions it considers necessary.” That is the legal basis for endorsements such as the storage-facility endorsement below. It is not a free-standing wholesaler, delivery, consumption-lounge or “microbusiness” licence — see §4.9.

One licence, medical + adult-use: Montana does not issue a separate medical cultivator licence and adult-use cultivator licence as two different licence classes. A cultivator, manufacturer or dispensary licence covers marijuana for medical and/or adult-use sale, and the tax channel is settled per sale by whether the buyer presents a registry card. MCA 16-12-201(2) states: “A licensee may sell marijuana and marijuana products to registered cardholders at the medical tax rate set forth in 15-64-102 and to consumers at the adult-use marijuana tax rate set forth in 15-64-102.” Source: MCA 16-12-201. The dispensary distinction that does exist is medical-only vs adult-use at the premises level (see §4.4). A single business may hold any combination of cultivator, manufacturer, dispensary and transporter licences; there is no requirement to hold multiple types. Sources: CCD Cultivator Licenses, Cultivator Licenses; CCD Manufacturing Licenses, Manufacturing Licenses; CCD Dispensary Licenses, Dispensary Licenses.

4.2 Common rules for every business licence

Application route. All business applications are submitted through the DOR TransAction Portal (TAP). Source: CCD General License Information, General License Information. Processing fee — the 20% rule. “All applications require a processing fee of 20% of the fee for the license(s) applied for. This processing fee is non-refundable regardless of the outcome of the application. Before approval, the remaining 80% of license fees must be submitted.” That wording is repeated on every CCD licence page. Practical consequence: the cash due at filing is 20% of the annual fee in the table below; the other 80% is due before approval, so the first-year state cost is the full annual fee, not 20% of it. Renewal fees are the same annual amounts stated in statute for cultivators, manufacturers, dispensaries and combined-use licences.

Term and renewal timing. Licences are annual, except the transporter licence which is valid for 2 years (see §4.6). MCA 16-12-104(10) as cited in SB 27 materials states licences “must be renewed annually” (transporter excepted by its own section). CCD General License Information states: “Renewal applications are available 90 days before expiration. Renewals must be submitted 60 days in advance of expiration to allow for necessary inspection and processing. Extensions will not be granted.” Each proposed location must undergo its own approval process, and every location must be applied for, inspected and approved before beginning operation.

Decision deadlines in statute. MCA 16-12-203(7)(a), as amended by SB 74 (2025), requires the department to approve or deny: (i) within 60 days of receiving a complete application/renewal from a former medical marijuana licensee or an existing licensee; and (ii) within 120 days from a new applicant. Source: SB 74 enrolled bill, SB 74 enrolled PDF — Verify the current MCA 16-12-203(7) numbering against the live MCA page before Run 1 is compiled, because SB 74 renumbered subsections.

Residency — YES, and this is a point on which secondary summaries conflict, so the statute controls. MCA 16-12-203(1)(a)(ii) requires “proof that the natural person having day-to-day operational control over the business is a Montana resident.” MCA 16-12-203(2)(g) provides the department may not license a person if the person or an owner, including a person with a financial interest, “has resided in Montana for less than 1 year.” Source: MCA 16-12-203, opened 1 Nov 2026. CCD General License Information states it more broadly: “Individuals applying for a business license must be a resident of Montana at application submission and for 1 year prior to requesting licensure.” [AUDIT: the CannBus “no residency requirement” claim is removed as wrong; MCA 16-12-203(1)(a)(ii) and (2)(g) control.] The precise scope — every owner vs only the day-to-day control person — is the live question: the statute ties the 1-year bar in (2)(g) to “the person or an owner, including a person with a financial interest,” while (1)(a)(ii) ties proof of residency to the day-to-day control person. Do not collapse those two tests into one sentence in the final dossier without that distinction. 2026 residency rule tightening. MAR Notice No. 2026-155.2, effective 26 September 2026, amended ARM 42.39.102 and 42.39.105: a licensee must submit, with an initial application, with each renewal, and within 30 days (changed from 10 days) of any change, the name of each natural person who has day-to-day operational control and documentation demonstrating each such person is a Montana resident. Source: Adoption Notice MAR No. 2026-155.2, MAR 2026-155-2. (Reconcile with Part 1.9, where the Parts 0–1 track recorded MAR 2026-155.1 as proposed with final adoption Verify — the Parts 4–5 track reports the adoption notice as 2026-155.2 effective 26 September 2026; check the Montana Administrative Register for the final numbering before Run 2.)

Age, disqualification, fingerprints, funding, property. MCA 16-12-203(1)–(2) requires: name, date of birth and street address; the no-diversion / no-pending-citations statement; the street address of each location; and proof of a source of funding from a suitable source (a lender/source may be found unsuitable if its record or activities pose a threat to the public interest, to effective regulation, or create a danger of illegal practices). If the applicant consists of more than one individual, names of all owners plus fingerprints and date of birth of each owner with at least a 5% controlling beneficial ownership interest. Non-individual applicants must disclose a complete organisational chart with identity and ownership percentages of controlling beneficial owners, bankruptcy history, lawsuit history, financial interests in marijuana businesses in any state, and specified information for publicly traded vs non-publicly-traded controlling beneficial owners. The department may not license a person if the person/owner/financial-interest holder: has a felony or drug-offence conviction within the past 5 years and, after investigation, is found not sufficiently rehabilitated; is in custody/supervision of the Department of Corrections or a youth court; has been convicted under MCA 16-12-302 or of fraudulent representation under the former DPHHS medical programme; is under 21; has failed to pay taxes/interest/penalties/judgments, comply with Title 15 or 16 (including filing returns/reports), stay out of default on a government student loan, or pay/remedy child support; has had a Chapter 12 or former medical licence revoked within 3 years; or has resided in Montana for less than 1 year. Property rule, MCA 16-12-203(5): a licensee may cultivate/manufacture only at (i) property owned by the licensee, or (ii) property rented/leased by the licensee with written permission of the property owner filed with the department when applying (CCD General License Information adds: for each location; acceptable ownership proof includes deed, title, mortgage paperwork or property-tax information; rented premises require the owner-permission document; locations must first pass a fire inspection by the authority with jurisdiction). No portion of property used for cultivation/manufacture may be shared with or rented/leased to another licensee. Marijuana may not be consumed on any licensed premises.

Local-jurisdiction condition. Every CCD licence page repeats: “All marijuana business applicants must demonstrate they are located within a jurisdiction that allows their business to operate in accordance with 16-12-301, MCA.” That is the green/red county rule, covered in Part 3; it is a licence condition, not a separate local licence. No hemp on a marijuana premises. MCA 16-12-104(3): “A licensee may not cultivate hemp or engage in hemp manufacturing at a licensed premises.” In-state production. MCA 16-12-203(3): “Marijuana for use pursuant to this chapter must be cultivated and manufactured in Montana unless federal law otherwise allows for the interstate distribution of marijuana.”

Worker permits are not a business licence, but every employee needs one. A marijuana worker permit is required for any employee who participates in any aspect of a marijuana business, must be held before starting work, is issued for one year and renewed annually, cannot be sold/given/transferred, costs $50 per year plus a background-check fee for owners and laboratory workers only, with a $10 replacement-card fee. Applications are via TAP. Minimum age to apply is 18 (secondary summaries; CCD page confirms the permit system but the age-18 statement should be cross-checked to MCA 16-12-226 before finalising). Source: CCD Worker Permits, Worker Permits. Verify: whether workers (as opposed to owners/day-to-day control persons) must be Montana residents — secondary summaries say workers do not need to be residents, but no CCD worker-permit page text retrieved on 1 Nov 2026 stated that, so do not assert it as official in the final dossier without opening MCA 16-12-226. No ownership cap in the sections retrieved. No statute or CCD page retrieved on 1 Nov 2026 stated a numerical cap on how many licences or locations one person may hold, other than the combined-use cap of one per tribe (see §4.7). Do not invent a cap. The constraint that does the work in Montana is the premises moratorium (§4.8) plus the per-location approval/inspection requirement.

4.3 Cultivator licence — 13 tiers (Micro + Tiers 1–12)

Statute: MCA 16-12-223. CCD page: Cultivator Licenses. What it allows. To plant, cultivate, grow, harvest and dry marijuana, and to package/relabel marijuana produced at the location in a natural or naturally dried form that has not been converted, concentrated or compounded, for sale through a licensed dispensary. Cultivators may sell what they cultivate to other licensed businesses or at their own licensed dispensary. Cultivators may transport their own product between licensed facilities without a separate transporter licence. Cultivators may not sell directly to consumers for medical or adult-use; consumer sales occur only at licensed dispensaries. All licensed facilities are subject to CARD inspection. Indoor-only rule, with one grandfather exception. MCA 16-12-223(1)(a): “Except as provided in subsection (6), all cultivation that is licensed under this chapter may only occur at an indoor cultivation facility.” Subsection (6): “A licensee who engaged in outdoor cultivation before November 3, 2020, may continue to engage in outdoor cultivation.” CCD states: “All new cultivation sites are required to be indoor only.”

TierMax canopyMax indoor facilitiesAnnual fee (initial + each renewal)20% filing payment
Micro250 sq ft1$1,000$200
Tier 11,000 sq ft1$2,500$500
Tier 22,500 sq ft2$5,000$1,000
Tier 35,000 sq ft3$7,500$1,500
Tier 47,500 sq ft4$10,000$2,000
Tier 510,000 sq ft5$13,000$2,600
Tier 613,000 sq ft5$15,000$3,000
Tier 715,000 sq ft5$17,500$3,500
Tier 817,500 sq ft5$20,000$4,000
Tier 920,000 sq ft6$23,000$4,600
Tier 1030,000 sq ft7$27,000$5,400
Tier 1140,000 sq ft8$32,000$6,400
Tier 1250,000 sq ft9$37,000$7,400

Source: MCA 16-12-223, opened 1 Nov 2026. The fee is imposed on the tier of licensure only, not separately per facility (MCA 16-12-223(5)). History line on the MCA page: En. Sec. 4, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 4, Ch. 743, L. 2023; Sec. 10, Ch. 743, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 4, Ch. 350, L. 2025 (Ch. 350 is SB 27). Entry tier and tier increases. CCD: “New cultivations may start only as Micro, Tier 1, or Tier 2. Tier increases are available at the time of annual renewal.” MCA 16-12-223(1)(f): “A marijuana business that has not been issued a license before July 1, 2025, must be initially licensed at a tier 2 canopy license or lower.” MCA 16-12-223(1)(e): a cultivator at capacity may apply to advance one tier at a time in conjunction with a regular renewal by demonstrating (A) full use of currently authorised canopy, (B) the tracking system shows sale of at least 80% of marijuana produced from the existing square footage over the 2 previous quarters, or the cultivator can otherwise demonstrate a market, and (C) the proposed additional/expanded facility is in a jurisdiction satisfying MCA 16-12-301 or in a county that voted to approve I-190. The department must inspect the registered and proposed premises within 30 days of receiving the application and before approving. Combined-use cultivators are excepted: they may increase by more than one tier at a time, up to Tier 5, without meeting (A) and (B) (MCA 16-12-223(1)(e)(iii)). The department is authorised to create additional tiers as necessary (MCA 16-12-223(2)). “Microbusiness” clarification. Montana has no separate “microbusiness” business licence. “Micro” is only the smallest cultivator canopy tier (250 sq ft, 1 facility, $1,000). Any source that lists a Montana “microbusiness licence” as a distinct licence class is conflating the micro tier with a licence type. There is likewise no microbusiness exception to the moratorium in SB 27 or MCA 16-12-201 — see §4.8.

4.4 Dispensary licence — medical-only and adult-use

Statute: MCA 16-12-224. CCD page: Dispensary Licenses. What it allows. Dispensaries are locations where marijuana can be sold to registered cardholders and/or adult-use consumers. CCD: “Medical-only dispensaries may only sell marijuana and marijuana products to registered cardholders. Adult-use dispensaries may sell marijuana and marijuana products to either registered cardholders or adult-use consumers.” Dispensaries may purchase marijuana/marijuana products from other licensed businesses. All sales to cardholders/consumers must be entered in the seed-to-sale tracking system. All employees must hold a valid Worker Permit before starting work. Statute detail: an adult-use dispensary licensee may operate at a shared location with a medical marijuana dispensary if both are owned by the same person; an adult-use dispensary is authorised to sell marijuana, marijuana products and live marijuana plants to consumers or registered cardholders (MCA 16-12-224, per HB 636/SB 74 amendment summaries — Verify the live-plant sentence against the current MCA 16-12-224 page before finalising, as it was not in the CCD page text retrieved). Fee — per location. MCA 16-12-224(6): “The department shall charge a dispensary license fee for an initial application and at each renewal. The dispensary license fee is $5,000 for the first location that a licensee operates as an adult-use dispensary or a medical marijuana dispensary. The dispensary license fee increases cumulatively by $5,000 for each additional location under the same license.” CCD simplifies this to: “The dispensary license fee is $5,000 for each location.” Arithmetic is identical ($5,000 × number of locations); the statute’s “cumulative” wording matters only if a future amendment changes the increment. History line: En. Sec. 5, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 2, Ch. 714, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 743, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 5, Ch. 350, L. 2025; amd. Sec. 1, Ch. 500, L. 2025; amd. Sec. 7, Ch. 550, L. 2025. Source: MCA 16-12-224. Application rule specific to dispensaries. Each dispensary location must be applied for individually (CCD). Local-approval demonstration under MCA 16-12-301 is required where the proposed dispensary would be in a county whose majority voted against I-190 on 3 November 2020 (MCA 16-12-224(1), subject to the MCA 16-12-201(2) moratorium exception noted in amendment drafts — Verify whether the current MCA 16-12-224(1) still carries the “Except as provided in 16-12-201(2)” lead-in, as amendment drafts show it and the MCA 2025 summary page retrieved shows the sentence without it). Hours (context, not a licence fee): CCD dispensary-visitor page states all licensed Montana dispensaries may operate only 9:00 a.m. to 8:00 p.m. daily. Source: Visiting a licensed dispensary.

4.5 Manufacturer licence — 3 tiers by monthly concentrate production

Statute: MCA 16-12-221. CCD page: Manufacturing Licenses (note CCD calls the page “Manufacturing Licenses”; the statute calls the licence “manufacturer”). What it allows. To convert or compound marijuana into marijuana products, marijuana concentrates or marijuana extracts, and to package, repackage, label or relabel marijuana products as allowed by statute and rule. Manufacturers must prepare products at a licensed premises exclusively and use equipment used exclusively for manufacture/preparation (MCA 16-12-221(1)). Manufacturing premises must meet applicable local board-of-health standards for a retail food establishment as defined in MCA 50-50-102, although marijuana products are not considered a food or drug for Title 50, Chapter 31 purposes. Manufacturers may purchase marijuana from licensed businesses and sell manufactured products to other licensed businesses; they may transport their own product between licensed facilities; they may not sell directly to medical or adult-use consumers — consumer sales occur only at licensed dispensaries.

TierFee (annual)Production allowed (CCD wording)
Tier 1$5,000Up to 10 pounds of concentrate per month
Tier 2$10,000Between 10 and 15 pounds per month
Tier 3$20,00015 pounds or more per month

CCD adds: “Manufacturing licenses are tiered according to how much concentrate is manufactured monthly; they are not determined by each premises” and “Manufacturing businesses that do not produce concentrate but perform other allowable activities that fall under the definition of manufacturing are licensed as a Tier 1” and “New manufacturers may start at any tier of their choosing but may only change at renewal” and “Concentrate production will be evaluated as part of the renewal process, and required fees will be assessed with the final invoice, if applicable.” MCA 16-12-221(6)(a) as retrieved via the MCA 2025 summary states the same three dollar amounts but phrases them per manufacturing facility (“$5,000 for each manufacturing facility that produces, on a monthly basis, less than 1 pound of concentrate and up to 10 pounds…”) and the boundary wording differs (“less than 1 pound … and up to 10 pounds” vs CCD “up to 10 pounds”). SB 74 (2025, Ch. 550) is titled in part “REVISING MARIJUANA MANUFACTURER LICENSING FEES” and amended MCA 16-12-221 — the apparent purpose was to move from per-facility to per-licence fees. Flag for the final dossier: do not present the manufacturer fee basis as settled until the live MCA 16-12-221(6) text is re-opened and compared word-for-word with the CCD page. The safe formulation is: annual fee $5,000 / $10,000 / $20,000 by monthly concentrate tier (≤10 lb / 10–15 lb / ≥15 lb); CCD administers the tier per licence, not per premises; the MCA summary text retrieved still reads per facility. Source for MCA text: MCA 16-12-221. History line on MCA 16-12-221: En. Sec. 10, I.M. No. 190, approved Nov. 3, 2020; amd. Sec. 52, Ch. 576, L. 2021; redes. 16-12-221 by Code Commissioner, 2021; amd. Sec. 6, Ch. 550, L. 2025 (SB 74). 20% filing payments on manufacturer fees: Tier 1 $1,000; Tier 2 $2,000; Tier 3 $4,000; balance before approval.

4.6 Marijuana transporter licence — 2-year licence, third-party only

Statute: MCA 16-12-222. Rule: ARM 42.39.413 (transportation; transporter licences). CCD Transporter Licenses page (audit correction): revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/cannabis-licenses/transporter-licenses — transporter licences are $10,000 for a two-year period, storage-facility endorsement $1,000 annually, 20% processing fee (non-refundable), applications via TAP, valid 2 years, third-party only, no delivery to adult-use consumers. That absence is itself a finding: do not cite a CCD transporter page URL in the final dossier unless it is located and opened. What it allows. MCA 16-12-222(1)(a): “A marijuana transporter license may be issued to a person to provide logistics, distribution, delivery, and storage of marijuana and marijuana products. A marijuana transporter license is valid for 2 years. A licensed marijuana transporter is responsible for the marijuana and marijuana products after it takes control of the marijuana or marijuana product.” A transporter may contract with multiple licensed businesses. MCA 16-12-222(7): a transporter may deliver to licensed premises or registered cardholders only and may not make deliveries to individual [adult-use] consumers. MCA 16-12-222(8): a person delivering for a transporter must hold a valid worker permit under MCA 16-12-226 and be a current employee of the transporter licensee. Transporters must use the seed-to-sale system to create shipping manifests (MCA 16-12-222(6)). Source: MCA 16-12-222 (archive). Who does NOT need one. MCA 16-12-222(4): a person holding a cultivator, manufacturer, adult-use dispensary, medical dispensary or testing-laboratory licence, or an employee of such a licensee, may transport between licensed premises without a transporter licence so long as the transportation complies with the seed-to-sale and related conditions. A registered cardholder or consumer buying at a dispensary does not need a transporter licence (MCA 16-12-222(3)). In short, the transporter licence is for third-party logistics; own-product transport is covered by the underlying business licence. ARM 42.39.413 adds: applicants must submit proof of a valid Montana driver’s licence; transport must occur in a motor vehicle as defined in ARM 42.39.102, depart from a licensed premises, be delivered to a licensed premises or a cardholder’s address (card presented before completion), be accomplished within 7 days of departure, and must not leave Montana while product is in the vehicle; a transporter/employee must not sell product or transport directly to consumers. Source: ARM 42.39.413 (Cornell mirror). Fee — rule-based, not stated in MCA 16-12-222. MCA 16-12-222(1)(d) leaves the fee to rule: “The department shall establish by rule the requirements for licensure and the applicable fee.” The fee consistently reported from ARM 42.39.102 (2021 rulemaking) and secondary compilations is $10,000 for the 2-year period (that is, $5,000/year equivalent, but charged as a 2-year licence fee — do not annualise it in a fee table without that note), with a 20% processing fee on filing ($2,000) on the same CCD pattern. Sources: 2021 DOR rulemaking notice (New Rule I: “Marijuana transporter license: $10,000”), MAR Notice No. 42-1033; MPP fee compilation, MPP fees; CannDelta Montana page, CannDelta. Verify the current ARM 42.39.102 fee line on rules.mt.gov before finalising, because no current CCD transporter page was retrieved to confirm it. Storage-facility endorsement (transporter + lab). ARM 42.39.420: a transporter or testing laboratory shall obtain a marijuana storage-facility endorsement for any overnight storage; the endorsement allows a separate off-site storage facility; storage may not exceed 48 hours; the facility may not be used to grow, process, test, manufacture, consume or sell; it may not be shared with another marijuana business; it must be in a jurisdiction allowing marijuana business operation under MCA 16-12-301. Fee reported as $1,000 annually (CCD Testing Laboratory Licenses page for labs; MPP/CannDelta for transporters). Sources: ARM 42.39.420 (Cornell mirror); CCD Testing Laboratory Licenses, Testing Lab Licenses.

4.7 Testing laboratory licence

Statute: MCA 16-12-206 (laboratory requirements; scientific director). Rules: ARM 42.39.6 (testing laboratories), including ARM 42.39.603 (documentation), ARM 42.39.614 (required testing). CCD page: Testing Laboratory Licenses. What it allows. To provide testing of representative samples of marijuana and marijuana products and to provide information about chemical composition and potency and the presence of microbiological, pesticide or other contaminants. All marijuana/marijuana products must pass testing before sale in a dispensary to a cardholder or adult-use consumer; testing requirements are the same for medical and adult-use. Required testing listed by CCD: cannabinoid profile/potency, pesticides, mycotoxins, residual solvents, microbiological, percent moisture, filth and foreign matter. Requirements (CCD). Consideration for licensure follows successful submission of all ARM 42.39.603 documentation plus a successful on-site inspection by the Cannabis Laboratory Program (inspection not scheduled until documentation is received): certificates of insurance and bonding; business licence; property owner permission form for laboratories if applicable; CARD-issued worker permit; laboratory security/storage plan; quality manual; standard operating procedures; validation studies with data/results; proficiency data/results; scientific director résumé, transcripts and references; ISO/IEC 17025:2017 accreditation and associated audit reports. Labs must employ a Scientific Director meeting MCA 16-12-206 education/work-experience requirements. Lab employees must hold a valid Worker Permit before starting work; an applicant to work in a testing laboratory must submit fingerprints and undergo a criminal background check under ARM 42.39.115 and MCA 16-12-206. The Cannabis Laboratory Program performs all initial, renewal and complaint-based inspections. All documents are submitted through TAP. Fee. CCD: Testing Laboratory Licence $5,000 annually; Storage Facility Licence $1,000 annually; 20% processing fee ($1,000 on the lab licence; $200 on storage) non-refundable. The 2021 rulemaking states the lab fee as “$5,000 per licensed premises.” SB 74 (2025) removed probationary licensing options for testing laboratories (per its title) — do not describe a probationary lab licence as current. Moratorium status for labs — Verify. Neither MCA 16-12-201 (which by its terms covers cultivators, manufacturers and dispensaries) nor the CCD testing-lab page retrieved states a 1 July 2027 opening date for labs. Do not assume labs are frozen or open; the final dossier should state the position as Verify and cite the absence.

4.8 Combined-use (tribal) licence

Statute: MCA 16-12-225. Rule: ARM 42.39.415. CCD page: Combined-Use Licenses. What it is. “A combined-use license is a special license created specifically for Montana’s eight federally recognized tribes and/or entities majority owned by one of those tribes. This license consists of both a cultivation license and a dispensary license.” MCA 16-12-225(1): the department may issue a total of eight combined-use licences to entities that are (a) a federally recognised tribe located in the state, or (b) a business entity majority-owned by such a tribe. Each tribe is allowed only one; after a tribe/majority-owned business is licensed, that tribe/another of its majority-owned businesses may not obtain another combined-use licence until the prior licence is relinquished, lapses or is revoked. Fee. MCA 16-12-225(3) and CCD: $7,500 initial licensure and annual fee (20% filing payment $1,500). The applicant must satisfy all licensing requirements under Chapter 12 and is subject to all fees and taxes associated with cultivation and sale (MCA 16-12-225(5)). Canopy and location — with two flagged discrepancies. (1) Tier. The pre-2023 statute described the licence as one Tier 1 canopy licence + one dispensary licence at the same premises. HB 903 (2023) allowed a combined-use licence to increase canopy tiers, and MCA 16-12-223(1)(e)(iii) now allows a combined-use cultivator to increase by more than one tier at a time, up to Tier 5, without meeting the full-use / 80%-sold tests. The current MCA 16-12-225(2) retrieved reads: “A combined-use marijuana license consists of one cultivator license and one dispensary license allowing for the operation of a dispensary” — without the words “tier 1” or “same premises.” CCD’s combined-use page likewise says “a Cultivation License and one Dispensary License” with no tier-1 or same-premises limitation, and repeats the up-to-Tier-5 multi-tier increase. (2) Geography. CCD’s FAQ states combined-use licensees “must operate in a ‘green county’ and within 150 air miles of the exterior boundaries of the reservation, or, for the Little Shell Chippewa tribe, within 150 air miles of the tribal services area,” citing MCA 16-12-225 and ARM 42.39.415. The current MCA 16-12-225(4) summary retrieved states only the county condition (operate on land in a county satisfying MCA 16-12-301 if the county majority voted against I-190). Flag: do not drop the 150-air-mile rule (it appears in the 2023 HB 903 version of MCA 16-12-225 and in the live CCD FAQ), but re-open the current MCA 16-12-225(4) in full before finalising, because the retrieved summary and the FAQ do not match word-for-word. Source for MCA: MCA 16-12-225. Moratorium exception — the only express new-licence exception found. CCD combined-use page, verbatim: “Combined-use licenses are not subject to the moratorium on other license types. Applications are available now.” That is the single licence class a genuinely new applicant could pursue on 1 November 2026 without waiting for 2027 — and only if the applicant is a tribe or a tribal-majority-owned entity. Uptake. August 2024 biennial-report figures reported secondarily put combined-use licences at 0 licensed. No combined-use licensee is named on any CCD list retrieved on 1 Nov 2026. Verify current uptake with CCD before asserting “none issued” in the final dossier; the correct statement on current evidence is “0 in the August 2024 biennial figures; no licensee identified in lists retrieved 1 Nov 2026.”

4.9 Licence types Montana does NOT have (do not invent)

  • No standalone “microbusiness” licence. Micro = cultivator tier only (§4.3).
  • No wholesaler/distributor licence. Cultivators and manufacturers sell to dispensaries (and to each other as licensees); transporters move product. Secondary CannBus Q3 2026 confirms: “No separate license class — Montana has no wholesaler license.”
  • No delivery licence, no on-site consumption licence, no separate retail-delivery endorsement. Delivery is limited: dispensaries/transporters may deliver to registered cardholders only; delivery of adult-use marijuana is prohibited (CCD intrastate-transportation guidance, Intrastate Transportation guidance (PDF)).
  • No separate medical vs adult-use cultivator/manufacturer licence classes (§4.1); the medical/adult-use split is at the dispensary premises level and at the point of sale for tax.
  • Chemical-manufacturing endorsement — historical, Verify as current. The former medical programme (Title 50, Chapter 46) used a chemical-manufacturing endorsement. No current CCD page retrieved on 1 Nov 2026 lists a separate chemical-manufacturing endorsement under Chapter 12. Do not list it as a current licence/endorsement without opening ARM 42.39.401–42.39.402.

4.10 Ownership, transfer, relocation, surrender — what the retrieved law actually says

Licences are non-transferable, with a business-sale route. MCA 16-12-203(13) (numbering per SB 74 drafts; Verify current numbering): “(a) Except as provided in subsection (b), licenses issued by the department under this chapter are nontransferable. (b) A licensee may sell its marijuana business, including live plants, inventory, and material assets, to a person who is licensed by the department under the provisions of this chapter. The department may, in its discretion, issue a temporary license to the acquiring party to facilitate the transfer of the licensee's marijuana business.” CCD FAQ gives the short answer: “No. The marijuana licenses may not be transferred.” Both statements are true at different levels: the licence itself does not transfer; the business/assets may be sold to a person who is (or becomes) licensed, with a discretionary temporary licence to facilitate the transfer. The final dossier must carry both halves, not just the FAQ one-liner. Becoming a controlling beneficial owner requires prior department qualification. MCA 16-12-203(14) (SB 74 numbering; Verify): “A person who is not a controlling beneficial owner in a licensee may not receive or otherwise obtain an ownership interest in a licensee that results in the person becoming a controlling beneficial owner unless the licensee notifies, in writing, the department of the proposed transaction and the department determines that the person qualifies for ownership under the provisions of this chapter.” “Controlling beneficial owner” (MCA 16-12-102(7)) includes, in outline, a person who alone or in concert owns/acquires beneficial ownership of 5% or more of the owner’s interest, an affiliate that controls the business (including any manager), a person otherwise in a position to control the business, and a qualified institutional investor owning more than 15%. “Financial interest” (MCA 16-12-102(13)(a)) means an interest entitling the holder, directly or indirectly, to 5% or more of net profits or net worth. Third-party contracts/relationships must be disclosed to the department before a third party begins work for a marijuana business, and every individual performing work for any aspect of a marijuana business must hold a worker permit (MCA 16-12-203(7)). Relocation is permitted during the moratorium; new premises are not. MCA 16-12-201(1)(a)(ii) expressly allows the department to “allow a licensee to move an existing licensed premises” during 1 July 2025–30 June 2027. CCD General License Information confirms: “After 7/1/2025, no new business locations will be issued. Current locations may move to other locations, if permitted by local ordinances and regulations.” A move is still a per-location approval/inspection process, not an automatic right. Renewal, suspension, revocation (for Part 11 cross-reference, stated here only as it bears on licence tenure). MCA 16-12-109(1): if the department has reasonable cause to believe a licensee violated Chapter 12 or a department rule, it may reprimand, revoke, suspend for up to 1 year, refuse renewal after expiration, or impose a civil penalty not to exceed $3,000. MCA 16-12-109(5) requires revocation (no reissue) for, among others, a controlling beneficial owner convicted of a felony drug offence, allowing an unauthorised person to possess the licence, or transporting marijuana outside Montana unless allowed by federal law. A revoked licensee may not reapply for 3 years (MCA 16-12-109(6)). Source: MCA 16-12-109.

4.11 The moratorium — full chronology and exact current scope

This is the single most mis-stated Montana fact in secondary sources, which variously say “paused until 7/2025,” “until 2025,” “until 2027,” or “frozen.” The statute chain is: (1) I-190 / HB 701 original window (1 Jan 2022–30 June 2023). Between 1 January 2022 and 30 June 2023 the department could only accept applications from and issue licences to former medical marijuana licensees (see §4.12). Adult-use sales began 1 January 2022 to this closed cohort. (2) HB 128 (2023) extension to 30 June 2025. HB 128 extended the moratorium for new applications to 30 June 2025, with the department to begin accepting new applications on or after 1 July 2025. Source: DOR 2023 legislative-changes summary, DOR 2023 legislative changes. (3) SB 27 (2025, Ch. 350, L. 2025; effective 1 July 2025) — the current premises freeze to 30 June 2027. MCA 16-12-201(1) as amended reads, verbatim in substance: “(1)(a) Between July 1, 2025, and June 30, 2027, the department: (i) may accept applications from and issue licenses to persons qualified for licensure under this chapter only if the application is related to an existing licensed premises; (ii) may allow a licensee to move an existing licensed premises; and (iii) may not allow for new licensed premises. (b) On or after July 1, 2027, the department shall begin accepting applications for and issuing licenses to cultivate, manufacture, or sell marijuana or marijuana products to applicants who were not previously licensed and for premises not previously licensed.” Source: MCA 16-12-201; SB 27 enrolled bill (above).

What SB 27 changed, precisely — and what it did not do. SB 27’s preamble states the 2021 Act’s moratorium “allows only certain people to be licensed” (former medical licensees); SB 27 “seeks to remove the moratorium as it relates to license applicants, allowing for new people to apply and participate,” while requiring “new licenses to be related to an existing licensed premises” and prohibiting “new location licenses for dispensaries until 2027.” In other words, 2025–2027 is a freeze on new premises, not a freeze on new persons: a new person may be licensed during the window only on an existing licensed premises (for example by acquiring/operating an existing business/premises through the §4.10 route), and an existing licensee may move an existing premises (subject to local ordinances). It is not correct to say “no new licences at all until 2027,” and it is not correct to say “the market opened 1 July 2025” (the pre-SB 27 date found in stale 2025 guides). Which licence types the freeze covers. MCA 16-12-201 is titled “Licensing of cultivators, manufacturers, and dispensaries” and its operative text covers licences “to cultivate, manufacture, or sell.” CCD’s three business pages confirm the same date for those three classes: cultivator — “Applications for new businesses to cultivate will be available starting 7/1/2027”; manufacturing — “Applications for new licenses to manufacture will be available starting 7/1/2027”; dispensary — “Applications for new dispensary licenses will be available starting 7/1/2027.” Exceptions within the freeze: (a) applications related to an existing licensed premises; (b) moves of existing premises; (c) combined-use licences, which CCD states are “not subject to the moratorium on other license types” (§4.8). No micro-tier or small-operator exception was found. Transporter and testing-laboratory status under the freeze is Verify (§§4.6–4.7), because MCA 16-12-201 does not name them and no CCD page retrieved states a 2027 date for them. Is there a closing date or cap for the 2027 opening? No closing date, application cap or lottery was stated on any CCD page retrieved. A secondary guide (CannDelta) asserts rolling first-come, first-served review with no announced closing date and no limit on the number of licences — treat that as secondary/Verify, not official. The only official statement retrieved is the opening date: on or after 1 July 2027.

LicenceNew applications open 1 Nov 2026?
Cultivator (new premises)No — from 1 July 2027
Manufacturer (new premises)No — from 1 July 2027
Dispensary — medical-only or adult-use (new premises)No — from 1 July 2027
Combined-use (tribal / tribal-majority-owned)Yes — CCD: “Applications are available now”
TransporterVerify — no CCD 2027 statement retrieved; MCA 16-12-201 does not name transporters
Testing laboratoryVerify — no CCD 2027 statement retrieved; MCA 16-12-201 does not name labs
Worker permitYes — separate permit system, via TAP (not a business licence)
Any licence tied to an existing licensed premises, or a move of an existing premisesYes, within the SB 27 terms — subject to local ordinances and per-location approval

4.12 How medical providers converted (DPHHS → DOR)

Transfer of the programme. HB 701 (2021, Ch. 576, L. 2021) transferred authority over licensing, cultivation and sale of medical marijuana to registered cardholders from the Department of Public Health and Human Services (DPHHS) to the Department of Revenue, created separate licence categories for cultivation, manufacturing, dispensing and transporting, provided for eight combined-use licences, created the marijuana worker permit, and provided for a local-option excise tax and local-government approval. Source: HB 701 enrolled bill summary/title, HB 701 enrolled (PDF). Who counted as a “former medical marijuana licensee” for the closed window. The controlling formulation in the current statute chain (SB 27 enrolled text, amending MCA 16-12-201) is: former medical marijuana licensees “that were licensed by or had an application pending with the department of public health and human services on April 27, 2021, and are in good standing with the department and in compliance with this chapter, rules adopted by the department, and any applicable local regulations or ordinances as of January 1, 2022.” CCD FAQ states the same cohort in short form: “Previously licensed means license holders in ‘green’ counties who were licensed on or before April 27, 2021, or who had a pending application on or before April 27, 2021,” and “As of January 1, 2022, previously licensed medical marijuana license holders are now allowed to sell to adult-use consumers” and “A licensed medical marijuana dispensary licensed on or before November 3, 2020, may sell adult-use marijuana in the same location as medical marijuana.” Conflict flagged: some secondary summaries state the cutoff as “licensed or pending on or before November 3, 2020” (the I-190 election date) rather than 27 April 2021. The 27 April 2021 date is the one in the SB 27 enrolled text and the CCD FAQ’s “previously licensed” definition and should be preferred, but the final dossier should check MCA 16-12-102’s definition of “former medical marijuana licensee” verbatim, because the two dates circulate in both official-adjacent and secondary material and the November 2020 sentence in the FAQ appears to describe a different (dispensary-location) rule. Conversion mechanics in practice. There was no separate “conversion application” creating a new licence class: former medical licensees in green counties were licensed under Chapter 12 by DOR and, from 1 January 2022, could sell adult-use marijuana (at the 20% adult-use rate) alongside medical sales (at the medical rate) from the same licensed dispensary location if owned by the same person (MCA 16-12-224(3) shared-location rule). Medical-only dispensaries remained possible for cardholder-only sale, including in red counties where adult-use sale is prohibited. A DOR rulemaking fiscal note from September 2021 gives the scale of the converting cohort: “As of September 2021, there were 296 providers, 434 dispensaries, 222 marijuana-infused products providers, five testing laboratory licensees, and 40,176 registered cardholders.” Source: DOR MAR Notice No. 42-1033, 29 Oct 2021, MAR 42-1033 (PDF) — Note: those are September 2021 pre-conversion counts, not current licence counts, and must not be quoted as 2026 totals.

4.14 Estimated startup costs — ALL non-official estimates, marked as such

No official DOR/CCD publication of total startup costs was found. The only official costs are the state fees in §§4.3–4.7 above, plus the $50 worker permit. Everything in this subsection is secondary, non-official, and must be labelled as an estimate in the final dossier.

  • Dispensary — Montana-specific secondary estimate: IndicaOnline, “How to Get a Dispensary License in Montana 2026” (published 19 June 2026, updated 6 July 2026): “Opening a dispensary in Montana can run from $150,000 to $500,000+, depending on scale,” covering property leasing/purchase, buildout and security, POS/compliance software, legal/consulting and inventory, beyond the licence fee. Source: IndicaOnline, June/July 2026.
  • Same publisher, wider range — conflict within one secondary source: IndicaOnline, “How to Open a Dispensary in Montana” (accessed 2026): “most entrepreneurs should expect to spend anywhere from $150,000 to more than $1 million to fully open a dispensary in Montana depending on scale, location, and business model.” Source: IndicaOnline (accessed 2026) — the $150k–$500k+ and $150k–$1m+ ranges cannot both be quoted as a single figure; present the range as $150,000 to $1,000,000+ (secondary) and note the publisher’s own inconsistency.
  • Component secondary estimates (same 2025 guide, now stale on the moratorium date — use only for component shape, not as current totals): Illinois Lawyer Now, “How to Open a Dispensary in Montana: The 2025 Guide” (Jan 2025; its “new licences July 1, 2025” premise was superseded by SB 27): licence $5,000/location; processing 20%; initial inventory $50,000–$100,000+; consulting/compliance $20,000–$100,000; security/compliance systems $20,000–$50,000+. Source: Illinois Lawyer Now, Jan 2025.
  • Generic (NOT Montana-specific) benchmarks — do not present as Montana costs: Catalyst BC via Medium (26 June 2025) gives generic facility build-out of $300,000–$1.5m+ for dispensaries, $250–$450/sq ft for indoor grows, $500,000–$2m+ for processing labs, plus 6–12 months’ working capital. Growthink gives generic dispensary build-out $100,000–$300,000, security $20,000–$50,000, equipment $50,000–$150,000, working capital $100,000–$300,000. These are included here only so the final dossier can explicitly exclude them as Montana figures if a reader cites them.
  • Official-fee floor a reader can verify (not a startup estimate): a single-location adult-use dispensary: $5,000 annual licence fee ($1,000 at filing + $4,000 before approval) + $50 per worker permit, before rent, buildout, security, inventory, insurance, legal, Metrc/POS and local fees, none of which DOR prices. A micro-tier cultivator: $1,000 annual fee ($200 at filing). A Tier 1 manufacturer: $5,000 annual fee ($1,000 at filing). A combined-use licence: $7,500 annual fee ($1,500 at filing). A transporter: $10,000 per 2-year licence ($2,000 at filing, 20% processing fee, per the CCD Transporter Licenses page).

No official estimate was found for: cultivation buildout per sq ft in Montana, extraction-lab buildout, testing-lab accreditation/equipment costs, or local licence/zoning fees (those are county/city-specific; see Part 3 work). Mark all as Unavailable from official sources.

PART 5: LICENCE DATA SOURCES

How to read this part. For each source: holder/type covered, URL, format, access/download method, update frequency as stated by the publisher, latest data date actually observed, fields available, and limitations. Where a direct download link + file name could not be captured from the publisher’s own page in a text fetch, that is stated as Verify / filename not captured — no filename is invented. A source that is an interactive web page with no download button is labelled as such. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced; business phone numbers that appear in the publisher’s own public licence lists are described by field name only, not copied here, except where the field list itself is the finding. Do NOT try to list every licence holder in this part — this is an inventory of sources.

5.1 CCD / CARD — licence hub and general pages (gateway, not a dataset)

Holder/type: all cannabis business licences (gateway to type pages). URL: revenue.mt.gov/card/; licence pages listed in §4.13. Format: HTML web pages. No bulk download, no CSV/XLSX, no export button found on the licence-type pages retrieved. Access: open web, no login. Applications themselves are in TAP (below), which is not a public search. Update frequency as stated: not stated on the licence pages; content reflects SB 27 (2025) and, on the FAQ page, the September 2026 rule change, as retrieved 1 Nov 2026. Latest data date: content current to 1 Nov 2026 retrieval; no “as of” date printed on the cultivator/manufacturing/dispensary pages. Fields: licence descriptions, fees, moratorium dates, application requirements. Not a holder list. Limitation: the hub does not give counts. Counts must come from the lists/reports below, which are location lists, not a single licensee register with status history.

5.2 CCD — Licensed Dispensary Locations (the dispensary list)

Holder/type: licensed dispensary locations (medical-only and adult-use). URL (list page): Licensed Dispensary Locations — reached via the “Licensed Dispensary List” link on the CCD visitor page Visiting a licensed dispensary. Format: interactive web list page — no download option identified in the text fetch. The text fetch of the list page on 1 Nov 2026 returned only the DOR phone footer and no list rows, which indicates the list renders in a way the text fetch does not capture (embedded table / scripted content). Do not claim a CSV/PDF download for dispensaries on current evidence. A live-browser check is needed to confirm whether an export/print control exists on the rendered page. Access: open web, no login stated. Update frequency as stated: CCD visitor page: “The department maintains a list of licensed marijuana dispensaries on our website and the department updates this list monthly.” Latest data date: a secondary source citing DOR lists the dispensary list as “September 2026” (CannBus Q3 2026 source list, item 13: “Licensed Dispensary Locations (September 2026) and related cultivator and manufacturer lists”). The DOR page itself, as text-fetched 1 Nov 2026, printed no “as of” date. Treat September 2026 as secondary-attributed, not as a date printed on the DOR list page. Fields as stated by CCD: name of dispensary, city of location, phone number, and whether the dispensary is medical-only or also permitted to sell adult-use to 21+. No street address, licence number or county field is stated on the visitor page. Street addresses appear instead on the site certificate displayed at the premises (premises name, address, licence number, issue and expiry dates), not in the published list as described. Limitations: (1) city only, not county — county must be inferred from city, which is error-prone for cities spanning counties and for the consolidated city-counties (Butte–Silver Bow, Anaconda–Deer Lodge); do not derive a county table from this list without a city→county crosswalk and a Verify flag. (2) No status history (surrendered/expired/suspended premises drop off a monthly snapshot without a changelog). (3) No licence-number field stated, so this list cannot be joined to tax or enforcement records by licence number. (4) The September 2026 list was not countable from the text fetch — no current official dispensary total is asserted in §5.9 from this list.

5.3 CCD — Licensed cultivator, manufacturer and transporter location lists

Holder/type: licensed cultivator locations; licensed manufacturer locations; licensed transporters. URL: exact list URLs not captured on 1 Nov 2026. The dispensary list sits under …/cannabis-licenses/lists/dispensary-locations. By pattern, sibling lists exist under the same /lists/ path, but no sibling URL is asserted here because none was returned verbatim in a search result or opened page. The existence of the lists is established secondarily: CannBus Q3 2026 source list item 13 cites “Licensed Dispensary Locations (September 2026) and related cultivator and manufacturer lists” and item 15 cites “Licensed Transporters (August 2026)” as DOR sources, and its §5 text states “DOR publishes location lists for dispensaries, cultivators, manufacturers, testing labs and transporters (Aug–Sep 2026).” Format: Verify — presumed web lists on the same pattern as dispensaries; no CSV/XLSX/PDF download was confirmed for cultivators, manufacturers or transporters. Update frequency as stated: not stated in retrieved material for these three lists; the dispensary monthly cadence (§5.2) should not be assumed for them. Latest data date (secondary-attributed): cultivator/manufacturer lists September 2026; transporter list August 2026 (CannBus citations). No count was extracted from these lists on 1 Nov 2026, so no 2026 total by type is asserted from them. Fields/limitations: Verify against the live lists. Do not assume they carry the same fields as the dispensary list or the lab list. In particular, whether cultivator lists state canopy tier, and whether manufacturer lists state concentrate tier, is Unavailable on current evidence and is a recommended follow-up (live-browser inspection of the /lists/ pages).

5.4 CCD — Licensed Testing Laboratory List (PDF — the one fully captured download)

Holder/type: licensed testing laboratories only. Statutory basis stated on the list itself: MCA 16-12-104(10). The list states it “does not represent locations where a registered cardholder or adult-use consumer can visit to purchase marijuana. This list is for informational purposes only.” URL (exact direct download link): Licensed-Testing-Laboratory-List.pdf. File name: Licensed-Testing-Laboratory-List.pdf. Format: PDF, 1 page in the copy retrieved. Access/download: direct open/download, no login. Update frequency as stated: not stated on the PDF. CCD testing-lab page does not state a cadence for this list. Latest data date observed: the PDF footer in the copy retrieved reads 03/03/2026 (3 March 2026). That is the latest lab-list date actually observed, not September 2026: CannBus cites a September 2026 lab list, but the PDF served at the same URL on 1 Nov 2026 (via the search cache dated 5 April 2026) still bore the 03/03/2026 footer. Flag: either the September 2026 list is served at a different URL, or the PDF at this URL had not been refreshed when cached. Re-fetch the PDF in a live browser before quoting a lab count. Fields: Licensee’s Name; City; Location Name; Phone. (Four columns; no licence number, address or accreditation field.) Entries in the copy retrieved (2): Nordic Labs LLC — Bozeman — Nordic Labs; and Fidelity Diagnostics Laboratory LLC — Missoula — Fidelity Diagnostics Laboratory. Conflict flagged: CannBus Q3 2026 reports the September 2026 labs as “New Frontier Laboratory (Billings) and Fidelity Diagnostics Laboratory (Missoula)” — that is, a different Billings/Bozeman lab name and city from the March 2026 PDF (Nordic Labs, Bozeman). Possible explanations include a rebrand, a new licence, or a secondary-source error. Do not state the current lab count as settled at 2 or name the Billings lab without re-fetching the live list. The August 2024 biennial figure was 3 labs (see §5.9), so a fall from 3 to 2 is plausible but not confirmed as a closure.

5.5 CCD — List of Controlling Beneficial Owners

Holder/type: controlling beneficial owners (≥5% / control persons, MCA 16-12-102(7)) for each licensee — an ownership list, not a premises list. Statutory basis: MCA 16-12-104(11) (list required by statute, per CCD February 2024 newsletter); MCA 16-12-110(5) requires the department to furnish the list to the Economic Affairs Interim Committee on request; HB 128 (2023) provided that controlling beneficial owner names will be published on the department’s website effective 1 October 2023. URL / file name: not captured on 1 Nov 2026. CCD February 2024 newsletter states: “A list of all controlling beneficial owners for each licensee is now available. Please note this list is required by statute (MCA 16-12-104(11)). … This list is for informational purposes only.” Source: CCD February 2024 newsletter. The newsletter page as retrieved does not expose the list’s direct URL/filename in text form. Verify via the live CCD site (likely under the cannabis-licences /lists/ path or a newsletter attachment) before citing a link or a count. Format: Verify (PDF or web list — not confirmed). Update frequency / latest date: Unavailable on retrieved evidence. Do not assume monthly. Limitation: even when located, this list identifies owners, not premises, tiers or sales; it cannot substitute for the §5.2–5.3 location lists, and its “informational purposes only” caveat means it should not be used as proof of current licensure without cross-checking the location lists.

5.6 CCD — Enforcement / disciplinary actions (no standalone public list found)

Finding: no standalone, downloadable CCD enforcement or disciplinary-actions list was located on 1 Nov 2026. Searches returned the penalty statute, not a list. What exists instead: (a) the penalty framework, MCA 16-12-109 (reprimand; revocation; suspension up to 1 year; refusal of renewal; civil penalty ≤$3,000; mandatory revocation categories; 3-year reapplication bar; contested-case review before the department’s Office of Dispute Resolution; district-court appeal within 30 days), MCA 16-12-109; (b) CCD newsletters and rule notices, which occasionally describe enforcement priorities (for example unlicensed hemp/synthetic-product cease-and-desist actions described in a 2025 legislative briefing) but are not a case register; (c) the absence of a holder from a monthly location list, which is not evidence of discipline (it may reflect surrender, expiry, relocation or non-renewal). Format/access/frequency/latest date: Unavailable as a dataset. Recommended follow-up: public-records request to DOR/CARD for contested-case outcomes, or EAIC oversight materials (MCA 16-12-110 requires periodic department reports to EAIC, which may include enforcement summaries — see §5.10). Do not present news reports of individual closures as a disciplinary dataset.

5.7 CCD / CARD — Cannabis Product Recalls (hub + PDFs; hub is NOT a complete archive)

Holder/type: recalled marijuana products by licensee/manufacturer, with Metrc source-package numbers — not a licensee list, but the only product-level enforcement publication found. Hub URL: Cannabis Product Recalls. Format: HTML hub linking to individual PDFs. No CSV, no bulk download, no export button on the hub as text-fetched. Update frequency as stated: not stated; entries are added per recall. A September 2026 CCD newsletter notes a revised recall rule (MAR Notice No. 2026-447.2, amending ARM 42.39.312, effective 11 July 2026) covering recall-plan requirements, notification procedures and timelines — future recall publication practice may change under that rule. Source: September 2026 CCD newsletter. Entries on the hub as text-fetched 1 Nov 2026 (2 only): (1) “Marijuana Product Recall – S&J Strategic Enterprises LLC – 01/28/2026 (PDF)” — exact direct link + file name captured: Marijuana-Product-Recall-SJ-Strategic-Enterprises.pdf — recall: Source Rosin, not tested; administrative hold 21 January 2026; products: Huckleberry Runtz Rosin, Huckleberry Runtz Rosin Pods, Moss Rocket Nectar Intimate Oil, Moss Rocket Shroom and Bloom Balm; no health complaints reported in the notice. (2) “Marijuana Product Recall – Broughton, Inc. – 04/07/2025 (PDF)” — date and licensee from the hub; direct filename not captured in the text fetch — Verify before citing a download link (do not guess a filename from the S&J pattern). Critical limitation — hub incompleteness proven by search: at least three further CARD/CCD recall PDFs exist in the same revenuefiles.mt.gov folder but are not listed on the hub as fetched: Hometree Co. recall, 12 February 2026 (concentrate, failed testing for Spinosad; hold 5 February 2026), Product-Recall-Hometree-Co.pdf; A Lucker, LLC voluntary recall, 11 September 2025 (infused ingestibles, testing results cannot be verified; hold 3 September 2025), A-Lucker-LLC.pdf; Selective Strains, LLC recall, 20 May 2025 (infused pre-rolls, source concentrate not tested; hold 21 April 2025), Selective-Strains-LLC-Recall-05-19-2025.pdf. The final dossier must therefore not describe the hub’s two entries as “all recalls.” The complete recall set requires a folder-level or newsletter-level search, and the April 2025 distillate recall widely reported in secondary press (inhalable cartridges, beverages, gummies, pre-rolls) could not be tied to a single captured PDF on 1 Nov 2026 — Verify before naming its licensee(s). Fields in each recall PDF: recall date, licensee name, product names, reason (untested / failed test / unverifiable testing), administrative-hold date, Metrc source-package numbers, “should not be sold or consumed / destroy” instruction, Poison Control referral. No sales-volume or exposure field.

5.8 DOR — Cannabis Sales Reports and county sales pages (tax/sales, not holder lists)

Holder/type: aggregate sales/tax by channel (medical vs adult-use) and by county — no holder-level sales. Statewide hub URL: Cannabis Sales Reports. County example URL (exact, opened via search): September 2026 county sales — pattern is /sales-reports/YYYY-MM-county-sales; the hub links monthly county pages. No CSV/XLSX download button was identified in the text fetch; tables are HTML. Whether a print/export control exists on the rendered page is Verify (live-browser check). Format: HTML tables on the hub and on each monthly county page. Update frequency as stated: monthly (the hub is headed “January 2022 to September 2026 Estimated Sales” as retrieved 1 Nov 2026, with a monthly table beneath). Latest data date observed: September 2026. Statewide estimated totals as printed on the hub: medical sales $262,969,775; adult-use $1,260,192,343; total $1,523,162,119; estimated taxes medical $10,518,791; adult-use $252,038,469; total $262,557,260. Fields (statewide): medical / adult-use / total estimated sales and estimated taxes, January 2022 to the latest month. Fields (county page, September 2026 example): county; Local Option Tax – Medical; Local Option Tax – Adult Use; Estimated Total Adult Use Sales; Estimated Total Medical Sales; Estimated Total County Sales. Counties with prohibited adult-use sales are flagged with an asterisk note (“Adult-use marijuana sales are prohibited in this county”). Limitations — stated by DOR itself on the hub: the data are estimates: “The reported monthly sales data shown in these tables may include discounted or reduced purchase prices paid by a purchaser. A discounted or reduced purchase price amount may not reflect the actual ‘Retail Price.’” And: “Local Option Tax is not derived from estimated sales reports.” Dispensaries file quarterly (returns due 15 April, 15 July, 15 October, 15 January, per secondary analysis of DOR practice), so monthly figures are revised after the quarter closes — do not treat a single month’s county figure as final tax data. County pages withhold some counties in later months in secondary analyses (5–10 of 56 counties withheld from July 2024 onward in one secondary audit — secondary, Verify against the live county pages before repeating). Related page — Local Option Tax Distribution: linked from the sales hub (“Please visit Local Option Tax Distribution for additional information”) and from the FAQ (distribution 60 days after quarter-end; quarterly distribution dates 15 June / 15 September / 15 December / 15 March). Exact URL not captured in text fetch — Verify.

5.9 Total licence counts by type and status — what official figures exist, and their conflicts

No current (2026) official total by type was extractable from a DOR list on 1 Nov 2026, because the location-list pages did not render as countable text and no DOR “licences by type” dashboard table was retrieved. The figures below are therefore dated official figures as reported secondarily, plus one directly observed list count. They must not be blended into a single “current total.”

August 2024 — DOR Biennial Report (1 July 2022–30 June 2024), as reported by CannBus Q3 2026 citing the biennial report:
