Transporters are counted as licensees.” The biennial report PDF itself was not opened on 1 Nov 2026; its direct URL is Verify. Do not cite CannBus as the official source in the final dossier — cite the biennial report once located, with CannBus as the finding aid. Conflicting biennial figures — flagged, not resolved: a 2024 secondary article (The Pulp, Missoula) describing “the Department of Revenue’s latest biennial report” gives different numbers: 402 licensed cultivators, 444 dispensaries, 217 manufacturers, and 4,745 cannabis worker permits. Source: The Pulp, Aug 2024. These cannot both describe the same August 2024 snapshot under the same counting rule. Possible causes: licensees vs locations confusion, a different biennial edition, or a different as-of date within the biennium. The final dossier must not pick one silently; it must either open the biennial PDF and quote its tables verbatim, or present both secondary reports as conflicting and mark the count Verify.

Later secondary counts — all Verify, none adopted as official: CRB Monitor licensing database, 25 June 2024: 320 cultivation, 423 active adult-use retail, 165 processing licences. Source: CRB Monitor, 25 June 2024. DOR September 2021 cohort (pre-conversion, not a 2026 count): 296 providers, 434 dispensaries, 222 marijuana-infused products providers, 5 testing labs, 40,176 cardholders (DOR rulemaking fiscal note, §4.12). CannBus Legal Summary Q3 2026: “roughly 557 licensed dispensaries” (no as-of list date given in the snippet). CannBus Market Report Q3 2026: transporter list August 2026 = 12 transporters (vs 9 in Aug 2024); testing labs September 2026 = 2 (vs 3 in Aug 2024). CannabisDataProject secondary analysis (August 2026 verification): DOR “types 550 of its 558 dispensary locations ‘Adult Use’.” All three dispensary figures (465 / 550 / 557 / 558) describe different dates and possibly different definitions (locations vs licensees vs adult-use-typed locations) and must not be averaged or presented as a trend without the underlying lists. Directly observed count (1 Nov 2026 retrieval): testing laboratories = 2 named entries in the March 2026 PDF (§5.4). That is the only holder count in this part that rests on an opened primary document rather than a secondary report of a primary document. Status breakdown (active / suspended / revoked / expired) — Unavailable. No DOR source retrieved breaks licences by status. Monthly location lists show only currently listed locations; they do not publish a suspended/revoked/expired register. Do not infer status counts from list-to-list differences.

5.10 Medical registry statistics (counts only — and a checked negative)

Finding: no DOR-published cardholder/patient count was located in the DOR cannabis section on 1 Nov 2026. A secondary audit dated 16 August 2026 (CannabisDataProject) states it enumerated every link on the CARD cannabis landing page by hand — sales reports, price studies, licence lists, recalls, rules, tax — and “none carries a registry figure,” calling this a checked negative, not an unexplored gap. Source: CannabisDataProject — Montana. Treat the audit as a lead, not as proof: the final dossier should re-enumerate the landing page in a live browser, because a registry figure could sit in a biennial/EAIC report PDF rather than an HTML page. Where a count should exist in principle: MCA 16-12-110(1) requires EAIC oversight monitoring to include “the number of registered cardholders,” and MCA 16-12-110(3) requires the department to periodically report to EAIC and the legislative clearinghouse on persons licensed/registered under MCA 16-12-203 and 16-12-503, including the number of cultivators, manufacturers and dispensaries, canopy square footage and production/sales amounts — but expressly: “The report may not provide any identifying information of registered cardholders or physicians.” Source: MCA 16-12-110 via Justia summary, Justia — §16-12-110. Historical counts that may be quoted only with their dates: 40,176 registered cardholders, September 2021 (DOR rulemaking fiscal note, §4.12). Any later patient count found in Part 6 work must be dated and sourced to the biennial/EAIC report it came from; no 2024–2026 patient total is asserted here. Format/access: Unavailable as a standalone public dataset on current evidence. Cardholder information pages (application/renewal) are not statistics pages and must not be cited as counts.

5.11 Green / red county list (official source — a map, not a dataset)

Holder/type: county adult-use status (green = adult-use sales permitted; red = prohibited; blue overlay = local-option tax adopted — see Part 3 for the full taxonomy). URL (FAQ page carrying the map): DOR FAQ — “Please refer to the attached Montana map. The map reflects the current status of each county, as determined by the results of Initiative 190 or other local election held after HB-701 was signed into law.” Map image (exact direct link, from the FAQ page’s Images footnote): CCD Local Option Map (PNG) — caption on the FAQ page: “Map of Montana showing county-level status of adult-use cannabis sales under Initiative 190 and subsequent local elections. Counties in red prohibit adult-use sales. Counties in green permit adult-use sales. Counties in blue allow adult-use sales with a local option tax. Red counties with blue stripes have approved a local option tax but prohibit adult-use sales. Green counties with blue stripes apply a local option tax only to adult-use sales.” Format: PNG image only. No CSV, XLSX, PDF table or searchable county list was found on the FAQ page. A reader cannot reliably extract a 56-county table from the map image alone without an official tabular source. Update frequency as stated: not stated on the FAQ page. The map is described as reflecting I-190 results “or other local election held after HB-701,” but no “as of” date is printed in the text fetched. Latest data date: Unavailable from the page text. The county sales pages (§5.8) provide a month-by-month corroborating signal (local-option tax flags and the “adult-use sales are prohibited” asterisk per county), but they are sales tables, not a status register, and a county with $0 sales in a month is not thereby “red.” Limitation: for Part 3, the county-by-county table cannot be sourced to this map alone. The authoritative county-status source for the final dossier should be the Secretary of State’s official election results for I-190 plus each subsequent county opt-in/opt-out election, cross-checked to the map — the map is corroboration, not the register.

5.12 Montana Department of Agriculture — hemp licence sources (no public holder list found)

Programme page: MDA Hemp — states: “A hemp license is required to cultivate hemp in Montana, applicants must comply with Section 80-18-101 through 80-18-111 of MCA and ARM 4.19.101-202. A hemp license provides authorization for the production of hemp at a particular growing area by a particular individual or entity. Montana is operating its 2026 State Hemp Program as a USDA approved Plan, effective January 27, 2023.” Also: “The state of Montana does not have authority over hemp grown within the boundaries of tribal reservations. That authority rests with the tribal nation on the reservation or the USDA, depending on the status of the resident tribe’s hemp plan.” Licensing page: MDA Hemp Licensing — states (2026): “The 2026 Indoor/Outdoor License is valid from the time of issuance until December 31, 2026. Late outdoor applications are subject to a $100 late fee. Please note there will be no online submission option in 2026, forms must be mailed or emailed. No hemp can be planted until the applicant has received a Planting Permit.” Two-stage licensing: Stage 1 Planting Permit (purchase seed/live plants, seed/transplant) → Stage 2 Production licence (on submission of a Planting Report confirming the crop planted; allows production and harvest of compliant hemp). Processor page: MDA Hemp Processors — Part A (floral/root extracts + grain/seed/oil/fibre) $2,500 per calendar year; Part B (grain/seed/oil/fibre only, voluntary) $1,000 per calendar year; each physical location licensed separately (structures within a 1-mile radius = one location); a current compliant grower may process their own production without a processor licence; licence expires 31 December each year; application period open throughout the year. Processor application (exact direct link + file name captured): Hemp-Processor-License-Application.pdf — “HEMP PROCESSOR LICENSE APPLICATION … For year ending December 31, 2025” (form eff. 12/16/2024 in the copy retrieved; the 2026 form should be confirmed on the licensing page before citing it as current). Seed-provider list (adjacent, not a grower list): MDA Licensing Stage 1 page, Licensing Stage 1 — states: “Current list of hemp seed providers licensed to distribute into Montana. This list is subject to change and will be updated periodically.” The list itself was not extracted in the search snippet; Verify whether it is a downloadable file or a web table, and do not present it as a grower/processor register. Public hemp licensee list — NOT FOUND (checked negative, to be re-checked live): no MDA page retrieved on 1 Nov 2026 published a searchable or downloadable list of licensed hemp growers or processors by name/county. Secondary material states only: “As of January 1, 2025, there were 17 licensed hemp producers, and 3 licensed hemp processors as of May 8, 2025” (HempCBD Business Plans summary of MDA programme, HempCBD Business Plans — secondary, Verify against MDA). Hemp licence counts by year and acreage belong to Part 6/Part 7; for Part 5 the finding is that MDA does not appear to publish a holder list comparable to CCD’s location lists, and holder-level hemp data for Montana must come from the USDA sources in §5.14 or from a public-records request to MDA. Do not state that MDA publishes no list at all without a live re-check of agr.mt.gov/hemp subpages, because the Stage 1 page shows MDA does publish at least one list (seed providers) on the same site structure.

5.13 Secretary of State — business entity search (not a cannabis licence list)

Holder/type: all Montana-registered business entities (LLCs, corporations, assumed business names, etc.) — not cannabis licensees. Useful only to verify that a licensee’s legal entity exists, its status, registered agent and filing history. Search URL (exact, from multiple secondary guides citing the official portal): SOS Business Search — portal home: biz.sosmt.gov. Format: interactive web search. No bulk download, no CSV export, no “all cannabis businesses” filter. Searching is free with no login; filing requires login. Results are limited in the basic search; advanced search narrows by match mode (Starts with / Contains / Word Contains) and by entity type, subtype, status, state, county, registration/expiration date, and by registered-agent PID. Separate tabs search state trademarks and commercial registered agents. Fields (results + detail panel): company name, filing number, previous name, entity type, status (for example Active–Good Standing; Involuntary Dissolution; Expired for DBAs/trademarks), registration date, registered agent (name/address in the detail panel), filing history, annual-report actions, Certificate of Existence ordering ($5; certified copy $10, per secondary fee summaries — Verify against the live SOS fee schedule before quoting). Limitations: (1) no cannabis-licence field — an entity can be Active–Good Standing with SOS while its cannabis licence is expired/suspended, and vice versa. (2) No premises, tier or county-of-operation field beyond the registered-agent/county filters, which do not prove where cannabis is grown/sold. (3) Sole proprietors/DBAs appear as assumed business names ($20 for five years, per secondary summaries), not as a separate business-licence register. (4) Montana has no general state business licence to search — SOS explicitly directs users to city/county offices for local business licences (see §5.15).

5.14 USDA hemp records for Montana

Approved-plan list (does NOT list individual growers): URL: USDA AMS — State and Tribal Plan Review. Format: HTML table. Updated 17 February 2026 (as stated on the page). Montana entries: “Montana — MT Approved Plan (pdf)” (state plan, approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021; MDA states the plan effective date as 27 January 2023 — the two dates describe plan approval vs the current plan version and should not be conflated). Tribal entries on the same table include “Assiniboine and Sioux Tribes of the Fort Peck Reservation — USDA Hemp Producer License” and “Confederated Salish & Kootenai Tribes of the Flathead Reservation — USDA Hemp Producer License” (that is, those tribes are under USDA direct licensing, not the state plan, for hemp — consistent with MDA’s reservation-boundary notice in §5.12). Limitation: this page proves plan status only. It does not list Montana growers, acreage or licence numbers. USDA public producer search (holder-level, secondary citation for the URL — Verify live): URL as cited in a 2022 USDA/Michigan bulletin: hemp.ams.usda.gov/s/PublicSearchTool — described as: “anyone can look up people or businesses licensed to cultivate hemp nationwide. … States and tribes are required to report licensee information to USDA, and that information is used to populate their database. In addition, it also includes producers USDA licenses directly.” Source: USDA/Michigan bulletin, 2022. Format: interactive web search tool (Salesforce-based public search). No bulk download stated. Limitation: coverage depends on state/tribal reporting to USDA; producers under a 2014-pilot notation were not required to register growing locations federally (per the USDA law-enforcement guide), and the public tool’s Montana completeness for MDA licensees was not verified on 1 Nov 2026. Do not present a count from this tool without running it and recording its filters (state = Montana; licence status; date run). USDA law-enforcement search tool (HeMP) — NOT public: URL: hempsearch.ams.usda.gov (login via Login.gov / USDA eAuthentication; access on request to farmbill.hemp@usda.gov). Fields (for authorised users): producer name, growing state/county, growing locations (mapped), licence number (USDA or state/tribe format), licence standing. This is the only USDA tool described as “real-time” for licence standing, but it is restricted to law-enforcement/regulatory officials and must not be listed as a public data source. Sources: USDA Hemp Law Enforcement Search Tool information sheet / user guide (AMS).

5.15 Tribal records (public) — none located as a holder list

Finding: no public tribal cannabis/hemp licensee register was located on 1 Nov 2026, and no combined-use licensee is named in any CCD list retrieved. Tribal cannabis/hemp law, ordinances and any state-tribal agreements are Part 10 work and must come from official tribal government sources and the state, not from CCD lists. What the state sources do establish for Part 5 purposes: (a) combined-use licences are capped at 8, one per tribe/majority-owned entity (§4.8); (b) August 2024 combined-use uptake was 0 in secondary reporting of the biennial report (§5.9); (c) MDA has no authority over hemp within reservation boundaries — authority rests with the resident tribe or USDA depending on that tribe’s hemp-plan status (§5.12); (d) USDA’s approved-plan table lists Fort Peck and CSKT under USDA direct hemp licensing (§5.14). Format/access/frequency/latest date: Unavailable as a public dataset. Do not list tribal operations from news or industry directories in Part 5; those belong, if at all, to Part 10 with tribal-source verification.

5.16 Local (county/city) licence and zoning records — no central inventory found

Finding: no statewide index of county/city cannabis licences or zoning approvals was located on 1 Nov 2026. Montana has no general state business licence, and SOS directs local-licence enquiries to city/county offices. Local approval under MCA 16-12-301 is a condition of the state licence, but the county/city’s own record (business licence, zoning verification, conditional-use permit) is held locally and published, if at all, on that county/city’s website or clerk’s office. Partial official proxies (not substitutes): (a) the CCD green/red map (§5.11) shows county adult-use permission, not local licences; (b) DOR county sales pages (§5.8) show where sales occurred and which counties adopted a local-option tax, not which premises are locally licensed; (c) city/county codes and council minutes would be needed for any “local licence” claim in Part 3/Part 8. Recommended follow-up for Part 3: for each county/city in the Part 3 check-list, record whether that jurisdiction publishes a business-licence register or a zoning-permit portal at all; do not assume a county that publishes council agendas thereby publishes a cannabis register.

5.17 Sources that are NOT licence-holder datasets (to prevent miscitation in the final dossier)

  • TransAction Portal (TAP) — tap.dor.mt.gov: application, renewal, payment and licence-management portal for applicants/licensees. Not a public search. Cited here only because every CCD licence page routes applications through it; it must not be listed in Part 5 as a holder-data source.
  • Metrc (seed-to-sale tracking): licensee-only operational system under MCA 16-12-105. The public cannot search Metrc for holders; recall notices use Metrc source-package numbers, but Metrc itself is not a public list. Do not cite Metrc as a Part 5 source.
  • DOR newsletters (for example September 2026 Cannabis Newsletter, September 2026 newsletter; February 2024 CCD Newsletter, February 2024 newsletter): rule-change and programme updates; they announce lists (for example controlling beneficial owners, February 2024) but are not themselves holder datasets. Useful as finding aids for list publication dates.
  • EAIC / legislative reports (MCA 16-12-110): periodic department reports to the Economic Affairs Interim Committee and the legislative clearinghouse, including licence counts, canopy and sales. These are the most authoritative source for counts by type once located, but no direct report PDF URL was captured on 1 Nov 2026 — Verify via leg.mt.gov EAIC materials / legislative clearinghouse before quoting counts as “DOR biennial report” figures.
  • Commercial licence databases (CRB Monitor, CannBus, etc.): secondary compilations. They are cited in this report only where they are the finding aid for an official figure (biennial counts, list dates) or where they expose a conflict. They must not be presented in the final dossier as official Montana sources, and their counts must be replaced by the underlying DOR lists/reports wherever the parent agent can obtain them in a live browser.

  2. Open the DOR Biennial Report (1 July 2022–30 June 2024) PDF itself and quote its licence tables verbatim, resolving the 465/343/180/9/3/0 vs 444/402/217/4,745 conflict in §5.9. Do not publish either secondary version as the official count until then.
  3.   5. CCD transporter page and fees now confirmed (see above); ARM 42.39.413/42.39.420 text on rules.mt.gov still to be re-opened (Cornell mirror only in the draft).

  7. PART 6: MARKET DATA

All Part 6 sources accessed 1 Nov 2026. DOR = Montana Department of Revenue. CCD = Cannabis Control Division, now within the Cannabis and Alcohol Regulation Division (CARD). DOR labels its sales and tax figures “Estimated Sales” / “Estimated Taxes” and the caveats in §6.1 apply to every monthly figure below. Local-option tax is NOT derived from estimated sales reports (DOR Sales Reports page). Where a figure could not be read from a DOR page directly, the intermediary is named and the figure is flagged.

6.1 Official sales and tax — what DOR publishes, and what it does not

Source: DOR Cannabis Sales Reports, revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/sales-reports/. Cumulative (DOR’s own running totals, January 2022–September 2026): total sales $1,523,162,119 (adult-use $1,260,192,343; medical $262,969,775); estimated taxes $262,557,260 (adult-use $252,038,469; medical $10,518,791). Arithmetic check: 20% × $1,260,192,343 = $252,038,469; 4% × $262,969,775 = $10,518,791 — the DOR estimated-tax series is exactly the headline rates applied to estimated sales, excluding local-option tax.

Why the monthly figures are estimates: dispensaries file quarterly (returns due 15 April, 15 July, 15 October, 15 January), so monthly figures are estimates and may include discounted prices that do not reflect “Retail Price” (the established price paid by the consumer, excluding discounts/reductions, per DOR). Local-option tax is collected separately and distributed 60 days after quarter-end; it is not back-calculated from estimated sales. No standalone monthly tax series is published — Unavailable. Do not present a monthly tax column as a DOR publication.

* Rate-derived tax = 20% × adult-use + 4% × medical, applied to the estimated sales in the same row. It is a calculation from DOR estimated sales, not a separate DOR publication. The 2022–2025 adult-use sum implied by the DOR cumulative minus the Jan–Sep 2026 derived figure is $1,035,088,013; the table above preserves the annual figures as reported and the 2022–2025 total as $1,273,920,541 — small rounding differences of a few dollars arise from the secondary summation of monthly tables and should not be “corrected” by arithmetic in the final dossier without re-summing the DOR monthly tables. Monthly grid source: MontanaStateCannabis.org transcription of DOR monthly tables, updated 6 October 2026, MontanaStateCannabis.org — Sales — flagged: DOR monthly tables did not render as text in a direct fetch on 1 Nov 2026, so the monthly grid rests on this transcription cross-checked to the DOR cumulative totals above, which it reconciles to. Do not cite the transcription as DOR itself.

Monthly pattern (from the same series): January 2022: adult $14,141,897, medical $10,143,750. Record month: August 2025, $30,046,391 total. September 2026: medical $2,477,682, adult $25,577,747, total $28,055,429 [AUDIT NOTE: $7 residual vs the set derived by subtracting annual sums from the DOR cumulative — re-read the DOR monthly table live before final publication]. Summer (July–August) is highest every year; February is lowest every year. Conflict flagged: contemporaneous weekly reporting for January 2022 gave adult-use $12,851,519.45 for 1–30 January (MJBizDaily, 2 Feb 2022) and about $13 million for 31 January–27 February (Marijuana Moment, 4 Mar 2022), versus calendar-month $14.14 million / $13.53 million in the reconciled series — a window + DOR revision difference; the calendar series is adopted because it reconciles to the DOR cumulative. Press “44% increase 2022–2025” conflicts with +29.8% rate-derived tax growth — the press figure appears to compare collections or a different base; collections vs estimates is the likely explanation and is flagged, not resolved, here. FY2024 cash collections (secondary, CannBus citing DOR Biennial Report 2022–2024): adult $49.7 million, medical $2.07 million, total $51.8 million; local-option FY2024 $6.37 million across 28 counties. Fiscal-year collections and calendar-year estimates must not be mixed in one table without labelling.

6.3 Medical patients — steep decline, no late-2026 official count found

January 2022: 40,291 (MPP compilation; note the Parts 0–1 track gives 40,522 for 1 January 2022 from a different secondary compilation — the 231-person difference is flagged in the notes and neither figure is presented as the single official count). August 2024: 14,930 (DOR Biennial Report via CannBus). 10 February 2025: 13,987 (MPP, 1.22% of the state population of 1,144,694) — a fall of about 65% from January 2022. Monthly medical sales fell in parallel: $10,143,750 (Jan 2022) → $2,609,660 (June 2026), down 74%. No 2026 official cardholder count was found — Unavailable / Verify. Historical context: peak 30,036 in June 2011, collapsed to 8,844 in mid-2012 under SB 423 (2011). See also Part 5.10 (no DOR-published registry count located in the DOR cannabis section).

6.4 Licence locations and counts (dated, conflicting — read with Part 5.9)

August 2024 (DOR Biennial Report via CannBus Q3 2026, CannBus): dispensary locations 465 (214 licensees), cultivator locations 343 (228 licensees), manufacturer locations 180 (159 licensees), transporter licensees 9, testing labs 3, combined-use tribal licences 0, worker permits 5,834. Later secondary figures: transporter 12 (August 2026 list), labs 2 on the September 2026 list per CannBus (but see the lab-name conflict in Part 5.4). SB 27 testimony (2025): about 1,000 licensed businesses, about 460 dispensary locations, about 300 distinct entities, no limit on locations per licensee. Conflict: CannBus Legal Summary “roughly 557 dispensaries” / “418 dispensaries” is unsourced — do NOT use. 2026 verified location count: Unavailable. See Part 5.9 for the conflicting biennial description (444 dispensaries / 402 cultivators / 217 manufacturers / 4,745 worker permits, The Pulp, August 2024) — do not pick silently; open the biennial PDF and quote verbatim, or present both as conflicting + Verify.

6.5 Sales by county and per-capita (official figures only; no ranking computed)

NO official per-capita ranking exists. Secondary CannBus calculations only: about 51 locations per 100,000 adults, about $703,000 sales per location (2025), about $286 per resident — secondary, not official; do not present as DOR figures. County sales examples from DOR tables: July 2026 — Yellowstone about $4.76 million, Gallatin about $4.56 million (Herring Bank citing DOR, Herring Bank); September 2026 DOR county table (September 2026 county sales): Gallatin $4,315,115, Missoula $3,207,762, Flathead $2,947,449, Cascade $1,848,506, Lewis and Clark $1,763,056. DOR county tables omit confidential counties and do not sum to the statewide total. Full 56-county September 2026 table is in Part 3.2. Dispensaries-per-capita by county: Unavailable from official sources — do not compute it from sales ÷ population.

6.6 Prices — official DOR Retail Price Studies

Source (directly read): DOR Retail Price Studies, revenue.mt.gov/card/cannabis/retail-price-studies/. 2026 study (July 2025–June 2026), average retail prices: buds $5.14 per gram, concentrate $28.81 per gram, edibles $14.06 per item, pre-rolls $6.30 per gram. Buds peaked at $5.32 per gram (September–October 2025) and fell to a low of $4.83 per gram (April 2026). Prior-year buds average $5.34 per gram (MJBizDaily, 15 January 2026, citing DOR — Verify against the prior study page). Launch era: above $7 per gram. No official wholesale price series and no official sales-by-product-type series were found — Unavailable. The falling bud price alongside flat total sales is consistent with the secondary “saturated/plateauing” description in Part 0.4, but DOR does not itself publish a saturation conclusion.

6.8 Banks and payment — no federal banking law; no DOR bank list

No DOR/CCD bank list was found — Verify. Clearwater Credit Union, Missoula (KGVO, 7 June 2022, CEO Jack Lawson: accounts for about 2 years at that date, deposit/transactional services only, no lending; 2026 terms Verify). Safe Harbor Financial — a fintech, NOT a bank, with partner institutions; Montana page Safe Harbor — Montana. Herring Bank Montana page, 18 October 2026, Herring Bank — Montana Cannabis Banking (Herring Bank is not Montana-chartered). Other Missoula credit unions were described in plural in the 2022 headline but only Clearwater was named — do not name others. Dual-market §280E: no Montana official guidance on splitting dual medical/adult-use activity was found — Verify. Herring Bank (18 Oct 2026) notes clean separation matters — a bank page, not official guidance. See also Part 2.8.

PART 7: HEMP LICENCE DETAIL

Part 7 research text. All sources accessed 1 November 2026 unless a different date is stated in the source itself. ARM citations obtained via the Cornell LII mirror are identified as such; the official ARM text is at rules.mt.gov and should be re-checked there before relying on a rule citation, because Cornell states its regulations are updated quarterly.

Scope and method. This report covers Part 7 only. It is a re-run, split from the failed combined Parts 7–8 track: the previous combined report hit an output token cap and delivered nothing, so this file is written incrementally, section by section, and the final message to the parent is a short completion note only. Run 1 was read first: the Run 1 file is ~/workspace/your_files/montana-cannabis-hemp-run1/montana-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6.html. Where a fact below repeats a Run 1 anchor it is cross-referred to Run 1, not presented as newly opened, unless this round opened the source itself and says so. ARM citations obtained via the Cornell LII mirror are identified as such; the official ARM text is at rules.mt.gov and should be re-checked there before final HTML citations, because Cornell states its regulations are updated quarterly and several of its cross-references are stale. No proposed rule, vetoed/failed bill, or draft measure is presented below as current law. Montana has biennial sessions; the 69th Legislature (2025) is the latest completed regular session; there is no regular session in 2026; the next is the 70th (2027).

Federal baseline applied as given, not re-researched: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use remains Schedule I; IRS §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, adult-use remains fully subject to §280E; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 Nov 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp delayed to 31 Dec 2026; the federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 Dec 2026; no federal cannabis banking law. Montana note: Montana has both medical and adult-use, many licensees serve both; Schedule III / 280E applies to medical only, dual businesses must split activity, adult-use remains Schedule I / fully 280E. If any tribe operates a medical programme, whether it counts as "state-licensed" is Verify (Parts 9–10/11–12 territory, not decided here). The tribal hemp plans in §6 below are USDA-approved tribal hemp plans or USDA producer licences; they are not state-licensed medical marijuana licences, and their status must not be conflated with that Schedule III question.

Headline resolutions in this round (see §11 for the full list): (1) The hemp chapter is Title 80, Chapter 18 — the Parts 9–10 "Chapter 27" citation is not corroborated by any MDA or MCA source opened here. (2) The Run 1 THCA question is resolved for official MDA testing: THCA is counted, by post-decarboxylation / the 0.877 factor (State Hemp Plan §§2, 6). (3) The USDA tribal table, opened in full this round, shows five Montana tribal entries, not two: Fort Peck and CSKT (USDA producer licences) plus Blackfeet, Chippewa Cree, and Fort Belknap (Approved Plans). (4) The SB 375 vs §16-12-117(3) tension is not resolved — no reconciliation guidance was located. (5) No Montana agency statement responding to PL 119-37/119-103 was located as of 1 Nov 2026.
