7. Interstate transport of hemp through Montana

7.1 What the State Hemp Plan actually says — §8, Transportation

State Hemp Plan §8 (p. 13, opened in full 1 Nov 2026), in full:

  • "Any intrastate transportation of raw hemp should include a copy of the producer's hemp license."
  • "Transportation of raw hemp that includes flowers must also include a copy of an official lab report showing the hemp is compliant."
  • "Non-compliant raw hemp being transported for disposal or remediation must include a copy of the Corrective Action Plan demonstrating an agreement between the license holder and the department and/or the processor designated in the plan."
  • "Transportation of processed hemp (e.g., distillate, isolate) should include a copy of the producer's hemp license and a copy of the Montana Processor's license."
  • "Transportation of hemp crude should include transportation documents provided by the department."
  • "Transportation of hemp originating outside of Montana into Montana should include a copy of the license holder's hemp license from an approved state or federally approved plan and a bill of lading showing destination of a person allowed to receive hemp."

Note the Plan's wording is "should include", not "must include", for most of these items. Quote it as "should" in final HTML; do not upgrade it to a mandatory document list.

7.2 Through-transport: no Montana rule located — stated plainly

Through-transport (origin and destination both outside Montana, passing through Montana): no Montana-specific rule was located in Plan §8 or in the MDA pages opened in this round. Plan §8 addresses intrastate transport and transport into Montana; it does not address through-transport. Do not state a Montana hemp through-transport right or prohibition from a Montana source alone.

Law-enforcement stops: no Montana-specific hemp stop/inspection procedure was located in the sources opened. The Plan's inspection/access provisions (§5.2) concern licensed premises/growing sites, not roadside stops. Mark law-enforcement stop procedure Verify/Unavailable; do not invent a stop checklist.

Sale vs transit distinction, stated plainly: SB 375 and §16-12-117 are sale rules (see §8). Their texts on their face address sale to a consumer in Montana, not transit through Montana. That textual observation is not a located Montana transit rule and must not be presented as advice that a through-shipment is lawful. The federal 2018 Farm Bill interstate-commerce framework was not re-researched per the given-baseline instruction; do not import a federal through-transport conclusion as a Montana rule.

Related, non-Montana-rule context found only in secondary sources (do not use as Montana law): secondary summaries assert that under the 2018 Farm Bill states/tribes cannot prohibit transportation of hemp through their territory, and that carriers have their own hemp policies. Those are secondary federal-law summaries, not Montana sources, and were not verified against primary federal text in this round. If the parent wants a federal transit paragraph, it must be researched separately and labelled as federal, not Montana, law.

---

8. Hemp-derived product manufacturing and retail outside dispensaries

8.1 What a processor licence covers — and what it does not say about retail

Processor licence at wholesale/ingredient level (MDA processors page, opened 1 Nov 2026): the licence "allows licensees to produce derivatives that may be included in products for food, fiber, oils, supplements or drugs (excluding THC) for the wholesale or ingredient market". Note the "(excluding THC)" qualifier exactly as printed. It sits in tension with any reading that a processor licence authorises THC product manufacturing; flag that tension in assembly rather than smoothing it over. The same page states retail approval "continue[s] to be subject to the laws and regulations of the United States Food and Drug [Administration] and the Montana Department of Public Health and Human Services (DPHHS)" (see §3.2; the page's spelling is "Association" — write the agency's correct name in final HTML).

No special retail licence for finished hemp products — secondary only, Verify. A secondary business-plan site (https://hempcbdbusinessplans.com/montana-hemp-industry/, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) states: "No special license is required to manufacture or sell finished hemp-derived products. The United States Food and Drug Association (FDA) and the Montana Department of Public Health and Human Services (DPHHS) are responsible for granting approval of manufacturing hemp-derived products at the retail level." That is a secondary summary, not an MDA/DPHHS primary source, and the same site's $50/$400 pilot-era fee figures elsewhere on the page are stale and must not be used. Do not upgrade the secondary "no retail licence" claim to a confirmed rule without an MDA/DPHHS primary source.

8.2 CBD in food and supplements — DPHHS/FDA position (secondary-reported; primary letter NOT opened)

Secondary reports agree on the direction but are not primary sources. WeedMaps summary (https://weedmaps.com/learn/cbd/is-cbd-oil-legal-in-montana, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) states DPHHS released a public letter in July 2019 declaring CBD prohibited from use as a food additive or dietary supplement, informed by the FDA position that hemp-derived CBD does not meet the legal definition of a food or dietary supplement, and that MDA does not regulate production/sale of CBD oil but defers to FDA guidelines. Other secondary summaries (Elevated MT, MontanaStateCannabis, Zebra CBD, all accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) agree that CBD is prohibited in conventional food, beverages, and dietary supplements in Montana, while hemp seed, seed oil, and hemp protein (no cannabinoids) are treated separately, and non-food forms such as tinctures, oils, topicals, salves, and balms are described as available. A DPHHS policy document titled "Industrial Hemp and Cannabidiol Policy for Foods" is referenced by title in a secondary source (Zebra CBD) with a partial MDA-hosted URL, but the DPHHS letter/policy itself was not opened in this round. Verify the DPHHS primary document before quoting any DPHHS position as confirmed, and label all food/supplement statements in the interim as secondary-reported. Also note secondary disagreement on detail (for example, whether capsules/gummies sold as non-food supplements are treated differently from conventional foods) — do not resolve that detail from secondary sources.

8.3 Hemp beverages

No Montana hemp-beverage-specific statute or rule was opened in this round. Beverages containing CBD/THC extracts fall, on the secondary analysis above, under the food/drink position (CBD extracts not allowed in conventional food/drinks) and, for THC-containing products, under §16-12-117(3) caps and SB 375 (§8.5). A secondary hemp-drink article (ithy.com, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) asserts the CBD-in-drinks prohibition is enforced through DPHHS administrative interpretation rather than explicit MCA language, mirroring FDA guidelines. That is secondary analysis, not a located DPHHS rule text. Mark a Montana hemp-beverage rule, as distinct from the general food position and the THC sale rules, as Verify.

8.4 Age rules — none located in primary sources opened

No minimum purchase age for hemp products appears in MCA Title 80 Ch. 18, in the ARM 4.19 excerpts opened, or in the MDA pages opened. Secondary sites disagree in wording: some state there is no age limit for hemp-derived CBD purchase/possession, with vendors possibly restricting under-18 or under-21 as a business policy; others state vendors may impose under-21 restrictions. Do not state 21+, 18+, or no age limit as Montana law. The 21+ rule that does exist in this dossier is for marijuana dispensary purchases under Title 16 Ch. 12, not for hemp retail. Mark hemp retail age as Verify, and keep the marijuana 21+ rule clearly separate in final HTML.

8.5 Delta-8 and converted cannabinoids

  • §80-18-101(1)(b) excludes synthetic cannabinoids from "hemp"; §80-18-101(5) gives synthetic cannabinoids the §50-32-222 meaning, including artificially produced cannabinoids whether by chemical synthesis or biosynthesis using recombinant agents including yeast and algae (see §1).
  • §50-32-222 lists delta-8 (delta-6) tetrahydrocannabinol as Schedule I: this was opened as official MCA 2025 text in the Parts 11–12 track (see part1112-report-summary.md), not re-opened in this Part 7 round. Cross-refer to that track; do not re-quote it here as newly opened.
  • HB 948 (2023, Ch. 746, signed 22 May 2023) created §16-12-117 and §16-12-305 (Run 1 anchor). HB 49 (2025, Ch. 100, signed 7 Apr 2025) expanded the synthetic ban (including "store" in the Run 1 summary) and added the §16-12-117(3) caps. DOR's synthetic-product page (Run 1 anchor, not re-opened in this round) lists delta-8, delta-10, THC-O, THCV, THCP, HHC, HHC-O, HHCP, CBN as synthetic examples. Verify the full current DOR synthetic list in assembly if the parent wants the list quoted; do not re-quote it here as newly opened.
  • Naturally occurring trace delta-8 threshold: Verify (Run 1 gap, still open). No threshold was located in this round.
  • Testing distinction: for official hemp testing, Plan §2 states "Delta-8 will be considered for this purpose to be the same as Delta-9" for Total THC (§5.3). That testing treatment does not by itself decide whether a given delta-8 product is "synthetic" for §16-12-117 purposes; keep the two questions separate.

8.6 SB 375 — enrolled text and the unresolved tension with §16-12-117

SB 375 enrolled text (69th Legislature 2025, enrolled-bill PDF, https://billtexts.s3.amazonaws.com/_data/mt/https-bearbeta-legmt-gov-docs-v1-documents-shortPdfUrl-documentId-321885-bill-id-SB-375.pdf, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026):

  • Title: "AN ACT REVISING LAWS RELATED TO THE SALE OF HEMP PRODUCTS TO CONSUMERS; PROHIBITING THE SALE OF HEMP PRODUCTS THAT CONTAIN THC TO CONSUMERS; PROVIDING DEFINITIONS; AND PROVIDING AN IMMEDIATE EFFECTIVE DATE."
  • Section 1: "(1) Unless authorized as a food or drug by the United States food and drug administration, a hemp product containing total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) may not be sold to a consumer in the state. (2) For the purposes of this section, the following definitions apply: (a) 'Consumer' means an individual who is a member of the public, takes possession of the hemp product, and does not offer the hemp product for resale. (b) 'Hemp' has the same meaning as provided in 80-18-101. (c) 'Hemp product' means a product that contains postharvest hemp and that is intended for consumption, smoking, vaping, or another method of administration."
  • Section 2 (codification): Section 1 is intended to be codified as an integral part of Title 50, chapter 31, part 2, and that part's provisions apply to Section 1.
  • Section 3 (effective date): effective on passage and approval. Run 1 dates SB 375 as enacted 2025 (chapter assigned 8 May 2025; signature date Verify on bills.legmt.gov); the enrolled PDF text opened via search shows blank signature-date lines, so the 5 May 2025 date remains a Run 1/secondary date, not re-verified from the enrolled signature page in this round — note that in §11 if the parent cites a signature date.
  • DOR summary of SB 375 (Run 1 anchor, repeated in secondary LegalClarity, https://legalclarity.org/is-delta-9-thc-legal-to-buy-in-montana/, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026): DOR summarises SB 375 as prohibiting the sale of any hemp product containing any level of total THC. LegalClarity also calls HB 49 a "companion" carve-out/exception — that characterisation is secondary interpretation, not statute text: HB 49 does not on its face say "except SB 375".

SB 375 vs §16-12-117(3) tension — UNRESOLVED, state it plainly:

  • §16-12-117(3) (HB 49, Ch. 100, 2025): hemp/hemp products sold by any person ≤0.5 mg total delta-9 THC per serving, ≤2 mg per package.
  • §16-12-117(4): does not apply to unadulterated hemp flower not further processed into extracts/infused products/concentrates.
  • SB 375 on its face bans sale to a consumer of any hemp product containing total delta-9 THC unless FDA-authorised as food/drug, and its "hemp product" definition (postharvest hemp intended for consumption, smoking, vaping, or another administration) would on its face include flower intended for smoking. That sits in tension with both the 0.5/2 mg caps (which imply some THC-containing sales are contemplated) and the flower exemption.
  • No MDA, DOR, or DPHHS reconciliation guidance was located in this round. The secondary claim (InfinityWellness, accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) that SB 375 "sits under Title 50 and falls to the Department of Public Health and Human Services" is secondary attribution only. Parts 9–10 added §16-12-305(2) inspection agencies for §16-12-117(1) violations (MDA, DOJ, DPHHS, sheriffs, municipal police, county attorney, DOR) — that section concerns §16-12-117(1) synthetic violations, not SB 375 Title 50 enforcement. SB 375's day-to-day enforcing agency remains Verify. Do not assign it to DPHHS, DOR, or MDA on secondary evidence alone, and do not present either §16-12-117(3) or SB 375 alone as "the" hemp rule.

8.7 THCA analysis (no THCA-specific statute)

No THCA-specific Montana statute was located in Run 1 or in this round. Analyse by category only:

  • Official hemp testing counts THCA via post-decarboxylation / 0.877 factor (§5.3).
  • Unadulterated hemp flower not further processed: §16-12-117(4) exemption from the 0.5/2 mg caps, but SB 375's "hemp product" definition (intended for consumption/smoking/vaping/administration) creates the tension noted above for flower intended for smoking/consumption.
  • Processed product containing total delta-9 (including THCA counted as total THC): §16-12-117(3) caps and SB 375 both potentially apply, unresolved as above.
  • Material above 0.3% total delta-9 THC dry weight is marijuana under §80-18-101 / §16-12-102(20) (Run 1 anchor), and Title 16 Ch. 12 / Title 45 Ch. 9 apply accordingly. No separate "hemp above 0.3%" criminal offence was located (Parts 11–12 finding, cross-referred).

8.8 Montana response to federal hemp dates — none located

Searches and pages opened in this round (MDA hemp, licensing, Stage 1, processors, inspections/sampling, disposal/remediation, State Hemp Plan, USDA table) contain no MDA or DOR statement responding specifically to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019, the 12 Nov 2026 date, the 11 Dec 2026 date, or a 0.4 mg per container cap. Result: Unavailable/Verify as of 1 Nov 2026; re-check after 11 Dec 2026. Note the unit difference for assembly: Montana caps are mg per serving/package (§16-12-117(3)); the federal cap in the given baseline is mg per container. No conforming Montana amendment was located. Note, as analysis only: Montana official hemp testing already uses a total-THC (THCA-inclusive) method, so the federal move to a total-THC definition does not by itself change Montana's testing method — but Montana's product sale caps/bans are separate Montana law and are not automatically conformed by the federal change.

9. Interaction with the marijuana programme

9.1 Premises prohibition — §16-12-104(3), verbatim

§16-12-104(3), official MCA page (https://mca.legmt.gov/bills/mca/title_0160/chapter_0120/part_0010/section_0040/0160-0120-0010-0040.html, accessed via search result 1 Nov 2026; also in SB 27 and SB 74 enrolled texts):

"A licensee may not cultivate hemp or engage in hemp manufacturing at a licensed premises."

This is a premises prohibition for Title 16 Ch. 12 licensees; it does not on its face prohibit the same person from holding a separate MDA hemp licence at a different, non-licensed premises — but that reading is analysis, not located guidance, and should be flagged as such if the parent states it.

9.2 Hemp product sold via a CCD licensee taxed as marijuana — Run 1 anchor, not re-opened

Run 1 anchor, not re-opened in this round. Carry it as a Run 1 anchor and flag it in §11 for verification against CCD tax guidance before final HTML; do not upgrade it to newly confirmed.

9.3 Dispensary hemp-flower/plant sales — secondary only

Secondary claim, not newly opened: LegalClarity (accessed via search 1 Nov 2026) states licensed dispensaries are explicitly prohibited from selling hemp flower, hemp plants, synthetic cannabinoids, or synthetic cannabinoid products from their premises, keeping regulated and unregulated markets separate. That claim was not verified against a DOR page in this round; label it secondary and Verify if the parent wants it in final HTML. It is consistent in direction with §16-12-104(3) and the synthetic ban, but consistency is not verification.

9.4 No hemp-specific security rule located for marijuana vs hemp premises

No hemp-specific security rule was located for marijuana premises vs hemp premises in this Part 7 round; the Parts 11–12 finding (no hemp-specific security rule located in ARM Title 4 Ch. 19 / MDA guidance opened in Run 1, to be re-checked against current ARM Title 4 Ch. 19) is cross-referred, not re-decided here.

ARM 4.19.104 conflict, to carry into §11 and into assembly: Cornell ARM 4.19.104(1) as opened states flatly: "The fee for a Montana State Hemp License is $1,100." It does not show an $850 A/B tier. MDA's 2026 Stage 1 form, Stage 1 page, and State Hemp Plan §7 all operationalise $850 for A/B-only and $1,100 for Category C. Possible explanations (rule text simplified in Cornell, category pricing set in programme/plan documents rather than the rule, or a later rule amendment not reflected in the Cornell version opened) were not resolved in this round. Assembly instruction: for 2026, use the MDA $850/$1,100 split, cite MDA/Plan sources for it, and flag the ARM 4.19.104 text check on rules.mt.gov as Verify. Do not write "$850 is the ARM fee" or "$1,100 is the only grower fee" without that flag.

Three $100 charges — do not merge them silently: (a) $100 late/incomplete documentation processing fee (ARM 4.19.104(2) / Plan §7); (b) $100 late outdoor application fee (MDA licensing page); (c) $100 late fee on invoices unpaid over 30 days (Plan §7). Whether (a) and (b) are the same charge applied in practice was not stated in the sources opened. Flag as Verify if the parent wants a single "late fee" line.

No hemp tax found: Run 1 anchor — licence fees only, no hemp-specific tax located. No hemp tax section in Title 15 Ch. 64 was located in this round; mark the no-tax statement as Run 1 anchor + Verify against current tax statutes if the parent wants it as a standalone claim.

Fees NOT found / not stated: reinstatement fee amount after a CAP-related suspension (Plan §10.1 states reinstatement fees must be paid, no amount given); any fee for a live-plant seller licence beyond the hemp + nursery licences themselves (no separate live-plant fee was located); any retail hemp licence fee (consistent with the secondary "no retail licence" claim, but not confirmed from a primary source). Mark each as Verify/Unavailable in §11; do not invent amounts.

PART 8: CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

Part 8 research text. All sources accessed 1 November 2026 unless a different date is stated in the source itself.

Scope note. This report covers Part 8 only. It does not re-deliver Parts 0–7 or 9–12. Run 1 facts are cross-referred to the Run 1 file (~/workspace/your_files/montana-cannabis-hemp-run1/montana-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6.html), not re-quoted at length. This is a re-run: the previous combined Parts 7–8 track failed because its final report hit an output token cap and was truncated, delivering nothing. This report is therefore written incrementally to this file, section by section, and the final message to the parent will be a short completion note only.

Federal baseline applied as given, not re-researched: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use remains Schedule I; IRS §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, adult-use remains fully subject to §280E; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 Nov 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp delayed to 31 Dec 2026; federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 Dec 2026; no federal cannabis banking law. No contradiction with a Montana property fact was found in this round; the federal baseline does not change any zoning conclusion below, because zoning permission is a state/local question under MCA Title 16, Chapter 12 and the city/county code.

1. Method: the strict verified standard

A property counts as a qualifying, cannabis-zoned property ONLY if ALL four conditions are verified:

(a) Montana location verified — city + county verified, with same-name/consolidated distinctions resolved (Butte-Silver Bow ≠ Anaconda-Deer Lodge; city of Deer Lodge, Powell County ≠ Deer Lodge County, Anaconda; Helena MT ≠ Helena AR; Billings MT ≠ Billings elsewhere; Bozeman, Missoula, Great Falls, Kalispell, Havre, Miles City, Livingston, Lewistown, Sidney, Glendive, Hamilton, Polson, Whitefish, Dillon, Shelby, Columbia Falls each checked as Montana places, not same-name places in other states).

(b) Zoning district actually permits the stated cannabis use under the CURRENT city/county code — code section cited — or local approval is documented. A green county, a nearby dispensary, a business/industrial zoning label, or a broker's "cannabis" marketing label is NOT, by itself, a code citation.

(c) County green/red status AND city vote status (where the city voted separately) checked against §16-12-301. Current county split used throughout: 29 green / 27 red. Original 2020 I-190 vote: 28 Yes counties per the Secretary of State canvass — 29 is the CURRENT count (Dawson in Dec 2021, Granite out Jun 2022, Custer in Nov 2024), not the 2020 result. Green: Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Cascade, Custer, Dawson, Deer Lodge, Flathead, Gallatin, Glacier, Hill, Jefferson, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Lincoln, Madison, Mineral, Missoula, Park, Powell, Ravalli, Richland, Roosevelt, Rosebud, Sanders, Sheridan, Silver Bow, Valley, Yellowstone. Red: Beaverhead, Broadwater, Carter, Chouteau, Daniels, Fallon, Fergus, Garfield, Golden Valley, Granite, Judith Basin, Liberty, McCone, Meagher, Musselshell, Petroleum, Phillips, Pondera, Powder River, Prairie, Stillwater, Sweet Grass, Teton, Toole, Treasure, Wheatland, Wibaux. A city can decide separately from its county under §16-12-301 — ARM 42.39.102 green/red definitions expressly do NOT include a municipality that has approved/disapproved categories under §16-12-301.

(d) The 500-ft same-street rule (§16-12-207) or any greater local buffer addressed for that address. State floor, §16-12-207(3)(a)(iii): deny licence if premises within 500 feet and on the same street as a building used exclusively as church/synagogue/place of worship, school/postsecondary school (other than commercially operated), or DPHHS-licensed/registered child-care facility, unless the locality requires greater distance; straight-line centre of nearest entrance to nearest entrance; renewal exception if premises pre-dated the protected use; "school" includes public/private preschools; applies to new applications on/after 1 Jan 2024. ARM 42.39.124 three-part test (same street + same-street address + ≤500 ft). Where a locality requires more (e.g. Billings 1,000 ft, Polson up to 1,000 ft, Miles City interim 1,000 ft), the greater distance governs for that address.

What does NOT count. A generic commercial/industrial listing in a green county; a listing whose broker calls it "cannabis-ready," "cannabis-zoned," or "dispensary opportunity" without a code citation; a licence or business sale (goodwill, inventory, licence, or a business as a going concern) that is not a fee-simple property sale; a property in a red county; a property inside a city that banned the relevant category (e.g. adult-use in Billings city limits); a property whose only zoning basis is a draft ordinance, a failed ordinance, or an expired interim ordinance; a listing withdrawn, expired, or in another state. Each such item, where instructive, is placed in Section 3 (fails) with its reason, not in the qualifying table.

Sources searched in this round (accessed 1 November 2026 unless stated): 420Property (Montana listings search and individual listing pages); Crexi (Montana cannabis / commercial listings search); LoopNet (Montana commercial listings search); Montana city/county code sources and code-library pages for Billings, Missoula, Bozeman, Kalispell/Flathead County, Helena/Helena Valley, Butte-Silver Bow, Polson, Miles City, Great Falls/Cascade County, Gallatin County, Ravalli County/Hamilton, and Laurel; DOR CCD/CARD pages (moratorium, combined-use, licence pages); MCA Title 16, Chapter 12 pages on leg.mt.gov / mca.legmt.gov; Secretary of State canvass material as carried in Run 1 and Part 9–10 (I-190 county results, 28 Yes counties). Where a page could not be opened, or opened only as a search excerpt, that is stated and the claim is marked Verify — search excerpts are NOT upgraded to "opened" in this report.

Run 1 §3.4 checklist used as the starting point (each item re-checked for current status where a property turns on it; stale or draft status is NOT copied as current): Billings 2021 ordinance, medical only CX/I1/I2, 1,000 ft, no variance/reduction, historic cap 8 medical lottery — verify survival; Laurel §17.08.761 1,000 ft — verify current; Missoula 2021 B/C/M districts, 500 ft between dispensaries + churches/schools recommended, no nontransparent glass, no home cultivation/manufacturing as home occupation, single entrance; 2024 city moratorium, sunset Aug 2026 — verify extension; Jan 2026 proposal >1,000 ft, grandfathered, final adoption Verify; Gallatin County unincorporated 12 Jan 2026 memo: NO marijuana-specific restrictions, site-specific zoning review same as other uses, no more individual compliance letters; Bozeman Ord 2084: city canopy/manufacturing/dispensary licences, indoor only, not visible, locked, no on-premises consumption; buffer 500 ft dispensaries-schools/worship (was 400 ft), air-vent odour/particulate for cultivation/manufacturing/testing; historic cap 20 medical Verify + downtown prohibition Verify; Kalispell Ch. 27: dispensaries Admin CUP B-1/B-2/B-3/B-4/B-5/I-1/I-2, cultivation/manufacturing Admin CUP I-1/I-2 only, Planning Board Sep 2021 recommendation 300 ft — adopted buffer Verify; Flathead County unincorporated: current retail only in business zones, cultivation only AG/SAG/R-2.5/R-1 per 2026 secondary — verify adopted regs; DRAFT text amendment, workshop 4 Nov 2026 (after access date) — NOT law; Cascade County Feb 2021: dispensaries to two I-2 heavy industrial zones outside Great Falls — verify current; Great Falls court 11 Oct 2022 Judge Grubich: city cannot ban without election, must process Safety Inspection Certificate, voters then allowed 52-48; Helena Valley Res 2020-97 amended 12 Jul 2022 Res 2022-51 §§704.27-29 — buffer/districts Verify full regs; Helena city 8 May 2023 first passage 5-0 Title 11 Ch. 2 amendments — final text/buffer Verify; Butte-Silver Bow: NO enacted comprehensive ordinance found; draft Jul 2025 cap 10 + 500 ft radius schools/churches/parks held; Nov 2025 clean draft requested, no adoption recorded — PROPOSED only; Broughton v. Butte-Silver Bow No. 2:25-cv-00065-BMM order 12 Mar 2026, 301 S Arizona Ave ~475 ft from Butte High welding entrance, no vested interest — facts only (and note the Parts 11–12 track's caution that the full order was not read end-to-end); Ravalli County does NOT licence businesses unincorporated (letter 7 Mar 2023 Chairman Dan Huls, waive requirement outside Darby/Hamilton/Stevensville/Pinesdale); Hamilton historic interim ordinances not relied on — verify current; Polson Ord 2021-003: 300 ft residential-zoned property, 500 ft worship, 500 ft city-owned building/facility/park, 500 ft another marijuana business, 1,000 ft K-12 schools + private day-care, straight-line property line to nearest point disregarding structures — verify survival in current Development Code + zones; Park/Livingston: no specific ordinance located — verify; Havre/Hill: none located — verify; Miles City interim Ord 1388 30 Dec 2024: recreational added to Ch. 24, Highway Commercial only, ≥1,000 ft building-to-building schools/parks/playgrounds/religious; proposed Ord 1391 FAILED second reading 11 Mar 2025 unanimous, interim expired 1 Jul 2025 unless extended — post-Jul 2025 regime Unavailable/Verify; Glendive/Dawson, Sidney/Fairview/Richland: none located — verify; Billings city adult-use ban Nov 2021 55-45 (county unaffected).

DOR county sales tables are NOT a zoning source. DOR county tables omit confidential counties and do not sum to statewide. $0 sales is NOT proof of red status, and sales presence is NOT proof of zoning permission for any particular parcel.

2. Qualifying properties table — HEADLINE FINDING: ZERO

ZERO properties meet the strict verified standard in Section 1.

No property found in the searches tried satisfies all four conditions at once: (a) verified Montana city + county, (b) a CURRENT code section (or documented local approval) showing the zoning district permits the stated cannabis use, (c) county green/red + city vote status checked, and (d) the §16-12-207 500-ft same-street rule or any greater local buffer addressed for that specific address.

This is NOT a finding that no cannabis business operates in Montana, and it is NOT a finding that no parcel could ever be licensed. It is a finding about the listings evidence available on 1 November 2026: the marketed inventory is overwhelmingly licence sales, business/asset sales, leased locations, confidential locations, or generic/un-zoned parcels, and none of the addressed fee-simple candidates carries both a code citation and a buffer assessment for its address. The two closest candidates are discussed first in Section 3, so a reader can see exactly what is missing for each, rather than having the standard lowered to admit them.

Why the closest candidates do not qualify — in one paragraph each (full entries in Section 3):

  • 6 89th Street S, Billings (Yellowstone County), $749,900 on MLS / $779,900 on 420Property, 4,125 sq ft, 1.45 acres. The listing's own zoning field is "Un-zoned," not a district that permits a cannabis use by code citation; the neighbouring Lipp-area listings are likewise described as "unzoned part of Yellowstone County." No Yellowstone County code section or local approval document for this parcel was located, no buffer assessment (nearest church/school/child-care facility on the same street) was located, the two prices conflict, and whether the active licence is included in the property price is ambiguous — and under SB 27 a new premises cannot in any event be licensed until 1 Jul 2027, so the only 2025–2027 route is relocation of an existing licensed premises (see Section 6).
  • 7795 Thorpe Road, Bozeman area (Gallatin County), $3,250,000, ±13,000 sq ft, 2.4 acres, "Unzoned." Currently configured/operating as a cultivation facility, in a green county whose 12 Jan 2026 memo states the county has adopted NO marijuana-specific restrictions and reviews each use site-specifically like any other use. That memo is a statement of no specific restriction, not a code section permitting cultivation at this parcel, and no site-specific approval, zoning district, or buffer assessment for 7795 Thorpe Road was located. Its licence details are blank in the listing, so no licence is shown as included.

A reader who obtains, for either parcel, (i) the parcel's zoning district or a written local approval, and (ii) a measured buffer assessment, could re-test it against the standard — on the evidence in this report, neither parcel passes today.

  • Montana-wide licence-only listings (no location, no parcel): Tier 1 cultivation $100,000 (Listing ID 974155, listed 5 Oct 2026, updated 6 Oct 2026, "can be relocated anywhere in the state capable of meeting state and local zoning laws and passing state inspections" — source: https://www.420property.com/listing/10-23-26-tier-1-cultivation-license-for-sale-all-offers-will-be-reviewed-and-considered/, via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026); Tier 2 cultivation $75,000 / $120,000 package with MIP; Tier 5 cultivation $80,000 / $100,000 combined; MIPP manufacturing licence $75,000; micro-tier + cultivation licences $250,000 (Billings, licence only); "Dispensary to be relocated $75K" (Bozeman); "Dispensary License for Relocation" (Bozeman); "Montana Cannabis Business Asset Package — Tier 2 Cultivation + MIP $120,000 / Tier 2 $75,000 / Mip $45,000." 3.4 Wrong-state, draft-only, expired-interim, and red-county traps (instructive fails)

  • Wrong state, same-name cities — EXCLUDED, do not count as Montana properties: 740 Schneider Drive, South Elgin, IL (court-ordered sealed-bid auction, appearing in 420Property Montana index results as a spotlight listing); Brooklyn, NY dispensary; Los Angeles, CA micro-business space; New Jersey, Florida, Colorado, Michigan, Oklahoma, and other-state listings that appear in 420Property Montana search results as spotlights/related listings. South Elgin, IL ≠ any Montana place. A same-name trap closer to home: any "Helena," "Billings," "Bozeman," "Missoula," "Great Falls," "Butte," "Kalispell," "Havre," "Miles City," "Livingston," "Lewistown," "Sidney," "Glendive," "Hamilton," "Polson," "Whitefish," "Dillon," "Shelby," or "Columbia Falls" listing must be checked for "MT" + Montana county before counting — none of the qualifying-table candidates in this report rests on an unchecked same-name match.
  • Butte-Silver Bow draft ordinance — PROPOSED ONLY, not a zoning basis for any Butte parcel: Run 1 §3.4 recorded a Jul 2025 draft (cap 10 + 500-ft radius schools/churches/parks, held) and a Nov 2025 clean-draft request, no adoption recorded. Re-check in this round (Citizen Portal summaries, via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026) confirms the draft was still held in abeyance at the 8 Oct 2025 Judiciary Committee meeting, with earlier draft variants showing different numbers (a 1,500-ft separation + cap 15 in a Jun 2025 draft discussion; a 500-ft radius + cap 10 in later drafts) — i.e. the draft itself changed between meetings and was never enacted in the material found. Sources: https://citizenportal.ai/articles/5917619/montana/silver-bow-county/montana/silver-bow-county/Silver-Bow-County/Montana/Butte-Silver-Bow-judiciary-committee-holds-draft-marijuana-ordinance-for-further-review ; https://citizenportal.ai/articles/5974721/Montana/Silver-Bow-County/Butte-Silver-Bow-to-draft-ordinance-capping-marijuana-retail-outlets-at-10 . No Butte parcel in this report is qualified on the draft cap or draft radius. The Broughton site, 301 S Arizona Ave, Butte (~475 ft from a Butte High School welding-classroom entrance, Order 12 Mar 2026, No. 2:25-cv-00065-BMM), is a litigation fact about vested interest, not a listed property and not a zoning permission — and the Parts 11–12 track cautions the full order was not read end-to-end.
  • Miles City interim Ordinance 1388 — EXPIRED 1 Jul 2025 unless extended; post-Jul 2025 regime Unavailable: Run 1 §3.4: interim Ord 1388 (30 Dec 2024), Highway Commercial only, ≥1,000 ft building-to-building schools/parks/playgrounds/religious; proposed Ord 1391 FAILED second reading 11 Mar 2025 unanimous. Re-check in this round (Miles City Star, via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026, and Miles City Council agenda 12 Aug 2025, https://meetings.municode.com/adaHtmlDocument/index?cc=MILESMT&ip=True&me=1adc0f8c4b78455abe1a6028a244fc49) confirms Ord 1391's failure and shows the council on 12 Aug 2025 discussing the Zoning Commission's three options for marijuana dispensaries and staff guidance — but no adopted replacement ordinance text was located. No Miles City parcel is qualified in this report, and no Miles City listing was found that could be tested against a current code section. Do NOT cite the interim 1,000-ft Highway Commercial rule as current Miles City law.
  • Billings city adult-use ban — a Billings-city adult-use parcel fails on city vote even though Yellowstone County is green: Billings voters rejected adult-use dispensaries in city limits Nov 2021, 55–45 (Run 1 §3.4). Any parcel inside Billings city limits therefore fails for adult-use dispensary use, whatever its CX/I1/I2 status, unless a later city vote reversing the ban is located — none was located in this round (Verify: re-check Billings city election record before relying on the ban for a filing). Medical dispensary use in CX/I1/I2 remains the only city-limits route in the 2021 ordinance as carried, subject to the 1,000-ft rule and the Verify items on the historic 8-dispensary lottery cap.
  • Red counties — no parcel in a red county can qualify for adult-use use, and no red-county parcel was found in the listings tried: Beaverhead, Broadwater, Carter, Chouteau, Daniels, Fallon, Fergus, Garfield, Golden Valley, Granite, Judith Basin, Liberty, McCone, Meagher, Musselshell, Petroleum, Phillips, Pondera, Powder River, Prairie, Stillwater, Sweet Grass, Teton, Toole, Treasure, Wheatland, Wibaux. A medical-only use in a red county (e.g. Beaverhead medical-only 3% tax) would still need a code section and buffer for its address — none was located for any red-county parcel in this round.
  • Flathead County draft text amendment — NOT law (workshop 4 Nov 2026, after the 1 Nov 2026 access date): Run 1 §3.4 already treated the Flathead County draft (integrated facility, B-district conditional uses, I-1 permitted operations, enclosed structure, negative-air-pressure standards) as draft only. No Flathead County unincorporated parcel is qualified on the draft in this report. Current unincorporated-County position as reported secondarily in 2026 (retail only in business zones, cultivation only AG/SAG/R-2.5/R-1) is itself Verify against the adopted Flathead County Zoning Regulations — it was not confirmed from the adopted regulations text in this round, so it cannot qualify a parcel either.
  • Missoula Jan 2026 1,000-ft proposal — status Verify as a zoning amendment, superseded for licensing purposes by Ordinance 3786 (see Section 4): the Kaimin/Montana Free Press reporting (via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026: https://www.montanakaimin.com/in_print/in-local-news/article_336fea4b-e5d7-44e2-a9f9-02ed3ed82eaf.html) that Council approved on 21 Jan an amendment requiring >1,000 ft from each other, churches, schools, and various government facilities (up from 500 ft, existing dispensaries grandfathered) was already flagged Verify in Run 1 for final adoption. It is NOT used as a qualifying buffer in this report, because the licensing prohibition in Ordinance 3786 now governs new adult-use dispensary licences in Missoula city (Section 4), and the final Title 22 text was not opened.

4. Jurisdiction re-checks for Part 8 (what changed, or did not change, since Run 1)

This section records only re-checks that affect a siting decision. It does not repeat Run 1 §3.4 at length; Run 1 remains the checklist, and any item not mentioned here stands as Run 1 left it (Verify where Run 1 flagged Verify).

  • Missoula city — RESOLVED IN PART, and tighter than Run 1's open question: Run 1 left the 2024 moratorium's status after its reported Aug 2026 sunset as Verify (extended, lifted, or replaced by the 1,000-ft proposal — not confirmed on an official City page). Re-check in this round finds the City of Missoula Cannabis page (via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026: https://www.ci.missoula.mt.us/2881/Cannabis) stating: "Ordinance 3786 was finally adopted 6 July 2026 (first reading 15 June 2026) and is effective 30 days after passage and adoption. Missoula Municipal Code §5.08.121(D) sunsets two years after its effective date; the City Cannabis page states the prohibition runs through 5 August 2028, which is consistent with that sunset, not with the state moratorium ending 30 June 2027. The prohibition bars a new business licence and any additional location, but allows an existing dispensary to vacate one location and relocate to a new location that complies with all applicable regulations, and allows renewal for operations in existence when the section took effect. However, an existing licensed dispensary may relocate to a new location if the existing location closes as a dispensary and the new location complies with all applicable regulations." Citizen Portal coverage of the 6–7 Jul 2026 Council action (via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026) is consistent: Council unanimously adopted an ordinance 6 Jul 2026 extending the temporary prohibition on new adult-use retail dispensary business licences, allowing existing dispensaries to relocate if the new location complies with Title 22 and building codes, with staff stating the change aligns the city with the state moratorium through 1 Jul 2027 while giving time to finish local zoning updates. The ordinance text itself (Ordinance 3786, CivicPlus, via search, accessed 1 Nov 2026: https://mt-missoula4.civicplus.com/DocumentCenter/View/81890/Ordinance-3786) was located only as a search excerpt in this round — full text Verify before quoting its operative language verbatim — and there is a tension to flag, not smooth over: the City page's end date (5 Aug 2028) runs beyond the state moratorium's end (30 Jun 2027) and beyond the Council-coverage framing ("aligns… through July 1, 2027"). For Part 8 the safe statement is: no NEW adult-use dispensary site in Missoula city limits is currently available on the city layer; only relocation of an existing dispensary (old location closes, new location compliant) is available, and the end date of the city prohibition must be confirmed from Ordinance 3786 itself before a filing relies on either 2027 or 2028. The Jan 2026 >1,000-ft proposal's final adoption status remains Verify (see Section 3.4).
  • Butte-Silver Bow — NO ADOPTION FOUND: draft held in abeyance Oct 2025 (Section 3.4); no enacted comprehensive ordinance located. A Butte parcel therefore rests on the state 500-ft same-street rule plus whatever existing Butte-Silver Bow code provisions apply — not on the draft cap 10 or draft 500-ft radius. Verify in the current Butte-Silver Bow Municipal Code before siting.
  • Miles City — NO REPLACEMENT ORDINANCE TEXT LOCATED: Ord 1391 failed 11 Mar 2025; council discussed three Zoning Commission options 12 Aug 2025; no adopted replacement text located. Post-1 Jul 2025 regime remains Unavailable/Verify. Do not cite interim Ord 1388 as current.
  • Gallatin County unincorporated — CONFIRMED AS CARRIED (12 Jan 2026 memo): no marijuana-specific restrictions; site-specific review like any other use; no more individual compliance letters to DOR (Section 3, F-2). This helps explain why Gallatin unincorporated parcels appear "un-zoned" or "no restrictions" in listings, but it does NOT by itself qualify a parcel under the strict standard, which requires a district/approval + buffer for that address.
  • Belgrade — PERMIT REGIME CONFIRMED, EXCLUSIVITY NOT CONFIRMED: §10-3-20 requires a city marijuana business permit per location and activity, annual renewal, site/building plans, state licence copy, and zoning/land-use permits (Section 3, F-6). No downtown exclusivity rule was located in the excerpt opened.
  • Billings / Yellowstone County unincorporated — NO CHANGE FOUND: no county-wide marijuana-specific zoning ordinance located for unincorporated Yellowstone County; Billings city 2021 medical-only CX/I1/I2 ordinance + adult-use city ban carried as Run 1, with the 8-dispensary lottery cap's survival still Verify and any later city vote reversing the ban still Verify (none located).
  • Kalispell / Flathead County, Helena / Helena Valley, Bozeman, Polson, Great Falls / Cascade County, Ravalli County / Hamilton, Laurel, Park / Livingston, Havre / Hill, Glendive / Dawson, Sidney / Fairview / Richland — NO NEW ADOPTION LOCATED IN THIS ROUND beyond Run 1 §3.4. Each item Run 1 flagged Verify (Kalispell adopted buffer; Helena Valley §§704.27-29 districts/buffers; Helena Title 11 final text/buffer; Bozeman historic cap 20 medical + downtown prohibition; Polson Ord 2021-003 survival in the current Development Code + zones; Great Falls current code after the Nov 2022 vote; Cascade County I-2 limitation current status; Ravalli/Hamilton current adult-use zoning; Laurel §17.08.761 current text; Park/Livingston, Havre/Hill, Glendive/Dawson, Sidney/Fairview/Richland ordinances) remains Verify. No parcel in any of those jurisdictions is qualified in this report, because no parcel in them was found with both a code citation and a buffer assessment in any event.
  • State floor — unchanged and applied throughout: §16-12-207(3)(a)(iii) 500 ft same-street + ARM 42.39.124 three-part test (Section 1). A local ordinance filed with DOR that conflicts with a proposed premises' zone is itself a ground for DOR denial (§16-12-207(3)(a)(i), Run 1 §3.4) — i.e. even a green-county parcel fails at the state layer if the locality's filed ordinance does not allow the use there.

5. General commercial/industrial siting context (NOT qualifying properties)

The following kinds of generic listings exist in green-county business/industrial areas, and a reader will encounter them in ordinary commercial searches. They are listed here only to explain why they do NOT count, not as alternatives to the qualifying table.

  • Un-zoned / "no restrictions" industrial and flex space in unincorporated Yellowstone County (Lipp area, Billings postal) and unincorporated Gallatin County (Belgrade–Four Corners corridor): e.g. 8 S 89th St W shops for lease ("NO RESTRICTIONS, NO HOA… in an unzoned part of Yellowstone County"), 5 S 89th St W Lot 1 ("Unrestricted (only restriction is for feedlots)"), and the Thorpe Road facility itself. "Un-zoned" or "no restrictions" in a broker listing means the broker asserts no zoning constraint; it is not a code section, not a local approval, and not a buffer assessment. In a county with no marijuana-specific ordinance (Gallatin unincorporated, and — on Run 1's finding — unincorporated Yellowstone County), the absence of a specific restriction is relevant context, but the strict standard still requires a site-specific determination + buffer for the address. A buyer must obtain that determination from the county planning office and from DOR/CCD before treating an un-zoned parcel as licensable.
  • CX / heavy-commercial lots in Billings city (e.g. Kuhlman Drive, Section 3 F-4): CX is a real Billings district, but in city limits it maps to the medical-only dispensary rule + 1,000-ft separation + possible lottery cap, not to a general adult-use permission. A CX label without a street number, a medical-vs-adult-use distinction, and a 1,000-ft measurement is not a qualifying property.
  • Downtown / Main Street retail in green-county cities (Thompson Falls, Belgrade, Missoula, Bozeman, Kalispell): retail frontage and an existing dispensary tenant do not prove the district permits a (new) dispensary, do not prove the buffer, and — in Missoula city — do not overcome Ordinance 3786's prohibition on new adult-use licences through 5 Aug 2028 (city page) / the relocation-only route.
  • Agricultural / ranch land in green counties (e.g. Realmo agricultural inventory, 143 Montana agricultural properties, and individual acreage listings in Missoula, Flathead, Gallatin, and Yellowstone Counties): agricultural zoning, even with a building, is not a cannabis-use permission. Cultivation in Montana is indoor-only under §16-12-223(1)(a) except for licensees who engaged in outdoor cultivation before 3 Nov 2020 (§16-12-223(6), Run 1 Part 4) — so an outdoor/greenhouse acreage listing, however large, does not by itself support a cultivation use, and no acreage listing in this round carried a code citation for cultivation.
  • Licence-portfolio and multi-site business sales: the market's dominant form (Section 3.3) is the sale of licences, businesses, and leases, with real estate (if any) secondary, leased, confidential, or optional. That structure is itself informative: under SB 27's premises freeze, the scarce asset being traded is usually an existing licensed premises / licence position that can be moved, not a newly zoned parcel. A buyer who mistakes a licence price for a property price will overstate what is being bought.

6. Lease vs buy, and relocation under SB 27 — the only 2025–2027 route to a new site

Lease vs buy under §16-12-203 (Run 1 Part 4 anchors, carried — re-open the section before quoting verbatim in final HTML): a licensee may cultivate/manufacture only at owned property or at leased property with the owner's written permission filed with the Department; a fire inspection is required; no portion of a licensed premises may be shared with or rented to another licensee; no consumption on licensed premises. Consequences for Part 8:

  • Buying (fee-simple) is the cleanest property position, but it does not by itself create a licence. A buyer of 6 89th St S or 7795 Thorpe Road acquires land/building; the state licence, if any, is a separate question governed by §16-12-203 (non-transferability, below) and by SB 27 (premises freeze, below).
  • Leasing is expressly contemplated (owner's written permission filed with the Dept.), and most of the marketed inventory is in fact leased (Gallatin confidential dispensary: land leased, subject to landlord approval and assignment terms; Great Falls and Flathead Valley businesses: leased, business can be moved; Belgrade 23 W Main: 10-year commercial lease, building purchase only as a separate option). A lease does not fail the strict standard merely because it is a lease — but a leased location with no address, no zoning district, and no buffer still fails, and a lease whose landlord has not given (or will not give) written permission filed with the Dept. cannot support a licence at all. Landlord approval / assignment terms are therefore a Verify item for every leased candidate, not a formality.
  • Licences are non-transferable, with a business-sale exception — carry BOTH halves: MCA §16-12-203 (Run 1: §16-12-203(13), SB 74 numbering — Verify numbering in final assembly) provides licences are non-transferable EXCEPT a licensee may sell its business including live plants/inventory/material assets to a person licensed under Chapter 12, and the Dept. may in its discretion issue a temporary licence to the acquirer to facilitate transfer. CCD FAQ's short answer "No. The marijuana licenses may not be transferred" is incomplete without the exception. Broker claims of "100% transferable" licences (Belgrade, Missoula 425 N 5th St) must not be adopted as legal conclusions; the acquirer must itself be (or become) a Chapter 12 licensee, and the premises must still satisfy §16-12-207, ARM 42.39.124, and local zoning.
  • SB 27 premises freeze (Ch. 350, L. 2025) — the decisive timing rule for every Part 8 buyer: 1 Jul 2025 to 30 Jun 2027, the Dept. (i) may accept applications / issue licences only if related to an existing licensed premises, (ii) may allow a licensee to move an existing licensed premises, and (iii) may NOT allow new licensed premises. On/after 1 Jul 2027, the Dept. shall begin accepting applications from previously unlicensed applicants for premises not previously licensed. Combined-use licences are expressly not subject to the moratorium (CCD combined-use page: "Combined-use licenses are not subject to the moratorium on other license types. Applications are available now."). Consequences:
  • A buyer who is NOT already a licensee with an existing licensed premises cannot open a NEW premises at a newly bought parcel until 1 Jul 2027 (and then only on the Dept.'s then-current application process — no closing date, cap, or lottery is stated officially; "rolling first-come first-served, no cap" is secondary/Verify, Run 1 Part 4).
  • A buyer who IS an existing licensee may move an existing licensed premises to a new parcel, subject to per-location approval/inspection and to local ordinances/regulations (CCD: "After 7/1/2025, no new business locations will be issued. Current locations may move to other locations, if permitted by local ordinances and regulations"). That is why many listings are framed as "relocatable" licences / "business can be moved" — the value is the moveable existing-premises position, not a new zoning permission.
  • A combined-use (tribal) buyer is the only express new-licence exception, subject to §16-12-225 (max 8, one per tribe, $7,500, §16-12-301 county condition in red counties — current text contains no tier-1, same-premises, or 150-mile wording; CCD FAQ wording on those points is stale, Part 9–10 resolution).
  • Dispensary hours (9 a.m.–8 p.m.) and single secured entrance (ARM 42.39.121(2)) apply to any relocated premises as to any other dispensary.
  • Practical sequence for a buyer of any Section 3 parcel, stated as steps not legal advice: (1) confirm the parcel's jurisdiction (city limits vs unincorporated county) from a boundary map, not a postal address; (2) obtain the parcel's zoning district + the code section permitting (or prohibiting) the intended use, or a written local approval; (3) measure the §16-12-207 / local buffer from the parcel's entrance to every protected use on the same street (and, where the locality uses a radius or building-to-building measure — Billings 1,000 ft, Polson up to 1,000 ft, Miles City interim 1,000 ft building-to-building — measure on that locality's measure, not the state's); (4) confirm SB 27 status (existing-premises move vs new premises vs combined-use) and, for a lease, the owner's written permission filed with the Dept.; (5) confirm fire inspection and, for cultivation/manufacturing, indoor-only compliance. Until steps (1)–(3) are documented for a specific address, the parcel cannot enter the qualifying table.

Addendum — Realmo index snippets whose detail pages could not be opened (not qualified)

  • 5701 US Hwy 93 N, Florence, MT 59833 — Ravalli County (Realmo index snippet only, accessed 1 Nov 2026: https://realmo.com/agricultural-properties/for-sale/mt/): "Cannabis Facility with Highway Frontage… 2.32 AC lot… Commercial-zoned facility configured for cultivation, extraction, and specialized kitchen operations."   • 2690 US Hwy 93 N, Kalispell, MT 59901 — Flathead County (Realmo index snippet only, accessed 1 Nov 2026, same index page): "Cannabis Property Portfolio… 864 sq ft… 1.85 AC lot… Seven owned or controlled real estate assets across Montana… Five operating dispensary locations… Tier 6 Cultivation License and Manufacturing License… Cultivation facilities, greenhouses, on-site laboratory, and manufacturing infrastructure." No price and no single-parcel terms in the snippet; a multi-asset portfolio is not a single fee-simple parcel sale. Kalispell city Ch. 27 (dispensaries Admin CUP in B-1–B-5/I-1/I-2; cultivation/manufacturing Admin CUP I-1/I-2 only, Run 1) would in any event need a district + CUP + buffer per site. Verdict: FAIL on evidence.

Montana Department of Agriculture (MDA)Hemp programme: MCA Title 80, Chapter 18, §§80-18-101–111 (see Part 7 for the Chapter 18 resolution; the Part 9–10 track's Chapter 27 citation was not corroborated anywhere opened and must not be used as the hemp chapter). Current State Hemp Plan document dated 3 January 2023; MDA states Montana is operating its 2026 State Hemp Program as a USDA-approved plan, effective 27 January 2023. USDA approval under the federal final rule effective 22 March 2021 is a different event from the 27 January 2023 plan version — do not conflate them. MDA states it has no authority over hemp grown within reservation boundaries; that authority rests with the tribal nation or USDA, depending on the resident tribe's hemp-plan status.agr.mt.gov/hemp; MT State Hemp Plan (PDF), accessed 1 Nov 2026.
Department of Public Health and Human Services (DPHHS) — historicRan the medical registry before HB 701 transferred the programme to DOR. The exact transfer effective date is still Verify (V-1). DPHHS retains child-care licensing (relevant to the 500-foot rule from 1 January 2024) and is a §16-12-305(2) inspection agency. DPHHS does not currently run the registry or cards.HB 701 (2021), Ch. 576, L. 2021; transfer date Verify.
Department of Justice / Division of Criminal Investigation (DCI)Criminal investigation, including dangerous drugs, Narcotics Investigation Bureau, drug task forces, Montana Law Enforcement Academy, fire-safety inspections. DCI was created by HB 353, 42nd Legislature (Ch. 219), effective 4 March 1971, under DOJ from the 9 March 1973 reorganisation. DCI does not license marijuana businesses. A DOJ "Marijuana in Montana" page was described secondarily but not opened — Verify (V-3); no DOJ enforcement policy or caseload figure is stated in this file.Secondary description only for the DOJ page; Verify.
Economic Affairs Interim Committee (EAIC) — §16-12-110Oversight, including (a) licensee numbers, (b) canopy square footage and percentage in production, (c) cultivation/manufacture/sale/testing/use issues, (d) seed-to-sale under §16-12-105, (e) cardholder numbers, (f) cardholder violations and penalties, (g) lab testing procedures; EAIC identifies issues for future legislation. The Department reports periodically to EAIC and the legislative clearinghouse (§5-11-210) on licensees/registrants (including cultivator/manufacturer/dispensary numbers, licensee violations, revocations, amounts cultivated/sold, registry applications and cardholders approved, conditions, cards revoked, physicians and certifications each) — with no identifying cardholder or physician information. §16-12-110(4) inspection report (announced/unannounced by canopy tier) full text was not extracted; (5) controlling-beneficial-owner list to EAIC on request; (6) Board of Medical Examiners annual report (§37-3-203) on physician-certification complaints. History: En. Sec. 25, I.M. 190 (2020); amd. Sec. 45, Ch. 576, L. 2021, plus a further amendment not fully extracted — Verify the complete history line (V-2).MCA §16-12-110 via 2025 mirror — official page not opened, Verify (V-2).
Board of Pharmacy; county attorneys, sheriffs, municipal police; USDA/DEA (federal); tribal governmentsBoard of Pharmacy: Drug Registry, §37-7-1514. County attorneys/sheriffs/municipal police: criminal enforcement and §16-12-305(2) inspection (see §9.2). USDA/DEA: federal hemp and scheduling matters under the given federal baseline. Tribal governments: see Part 10.As cited in §9.2 and Part 10, accessed 1 Nov 2026.

The required-reports archive (archive.legmt.gov/committees/interim/eaic/required-reports/) lists 2023–2024 items, including the Synthetic Marijuana Task Force Report (one-time, due 15 September 2024, HB 948), marijuana regulation reports due 1 September 2024 (§16-12-110), Drug Registry 2024 (Board of Pharmacy, §37-7-1514), and physician-certification complaints 2023 and 2024 (Board of Medical Examiners, annual). The latest EAIC marijuana report is not established — no 2025 or 2026 report was located or opened; do not claim one exists (V-5).

Local building, health, fire and zoning codes and contacts are per-jurisdiction; no statewide code or contact list was located — Verify per jurisdiction (V-4). §16-12-207(9)(c) preserves state and local entry/exit and fire requirements.

9.2 Inspection and local-approval statutes

§16-12-305(2), verbatim (Justia mirror of MCA 2025; official page not opened — V-2):

"The department of agriculture, the department of justice, the department of public health and human services, local sheriff departments, municipal police departments, a county attorney's office, and the department of revenue may inspect any business to investigate unlawful activity under 16-12-117(1)".

History: En. Sec. 2, Ch. 746, L. 2023 (HB 948). §16-12-305(1): a local government may by ordinance regulate products under §16-12-117(1) and (3).

§16-12-301 (FindLaw mirror; official page not opened — V-2): a business in a red county may not operate until the relevant categories are approved by the local jurisdiction (§16-12-301(3)/(4)) and the Department has licensed it (§16-12-301(1)(a)). Categories include "combined-use marijuana licensee" (1)(d)(v) and "marijuana transporter facility" (1)(d)(vii). A local government "may not prohibit the transportation of marijuana within or through its jurisdiction on public roads by any person". The full current §16-12-301 verbatim text remains Verify (V-2).

9.3 Timeline

  • I-148, 2 November 2004: medical marijuana legalised; DPHHS registry created. "Nearly 62%" approval is secondary — exact Secretary of State canvass percentage is Verify (V-6).
  • HB 161 (2011): repeal bill vetoed by Governor Schweitzer (secondary) — not law.
  • SB 423 (2011): repealed the voter Act effective 1 July 2011 and replaced it with a restrictive regime (no more than 3 patients per provider, no compensation for providers or marijuana-infused-products providers, medical-board audit of doctors recommending for more than 25 patients per year — MPP secondary). Litigation: temporary restraining order 13 May 2011; Montana Cannabis Industry Association v. State, DA 11-0460 (2012); several portions enjoined June 2011–August 2016 (Legislature SB 423 monitoring archive, archive.legmt.gov). The Montana Supreme Court on 25 February 2016 upheld all challenged portions except the compensation ban; rehearing delay declined; effective date 31 August 2016; United States Supreme Court appeal denied. Post-ruling, more than 93% of 12,400 patients were said to have lost provider access by August 2016 (MPP secondary, unverified — V-7). Full opinions were not opened (V-7).
  • I-182, 8 November 2016: approved, 57% secondary — exact Secretary of State percentage is Verify (V-6); secondary accounts say it removed the patient limit, created the business regulatory structure, and added chronic pain and PTSD — verify against the I-182 text before treating that as verbatim law (V-6).
  • I-190, 3 November 2020 — official Secretary of State canvass (see §9.4): Yes 341,037 (56.90%), No 258,337 (43.10%). Sponsor New Approach Montana and MPP; qualified 13 August 2020 (secondary).
  • CI-118 (2020): approved, about 58% (preliminary 5 November 2020 and CannBus) — exact official canvass votes are Verify (V-8; the CI-118 section of the canvass PDF was not located by text search). CI-118 text (Secretary of State ballot language, sosmt.gov/elections/ballot_issues/2020-2/): amends Montana Constitution Article II, §14 so the legislature or the people by initiative may establish the legal age for purchasing, consuming or possessing alcohol and marijuana.
  • HB 701 (2021), Ch. 576: signed by Governor Gianforte (Run 1 date 18 May 2021 — a Tuesday, consistent with secondary "signed Tuesday" articles published 5 June 2021; enrolled-bill date still Verify, V-9); sponsor Representative Mike Hopkins (R-Missoula) (secondary). Created CARD/CCD, transferred DPHHS to DOR (date Verify, V-1), set the 20% adult-use tax (early secondary articles wrongly say medical 5% — the current rate is 4%; do not repeat 5% as current), restricted the first 18 months of adult-use retail to former medical dispensaries, set adult home grow at 4+4 then 2+2 per person (maximum 4+4 per household), flower at 35%, edibles at 100 mg per package (10 mg per serving originally). Multiple effective dates: 18 May 2021, 1 July 2021, 1 January 2022. The enrolled effective-date section was not opened (V-9).
  • HB 655 (2021): lawful-products and employment — see Part 12 for the now-verified §39-2-313 text; full HB 655 text still Verify (V-9).
  • MAR 42-1033 hearing, 16 November 2021: first Chapter 12 rules, including New Rule VI on combined-use, under authorising statute §16-12-225.
  • Adult-use retail begins 1 January 2022 (DOR FAQ). Possession and use legal from 1 January 2021 per CannBus secondary — not repeated as a statutory date without an I-190 text check (V-9).
  • Local flips: Dawson County in, December 2021; Granite County out, June 2022; Custer County in, November 2024 — the local election records themselves were not opened (V-10). Current green count = 28 − 1 + 1 + 1 = 29; red = 27.
  • HB 128 (2023): moratorium extended to 30 June 2025; combined-use clarified — licences do not need to share a location and are not limited by proximity to tribal land or a service area; adult-use smoking/vaping concentrates up to 800 mg (DOR 2023 summary, revenue.mt.gov/news/2023/cannabis-control-division-legislative-changes-summary-for-2023). Chapter mapping Ch. 712 is inferred from the §16-12-225 history "Sec. 21, Ch. 712, L. 2023" — Verify against the 2023 House bill-to-chapter table (V-11).
  • HB 903 (2023): former medical licensee = licensed by or application pending with DPHHS by 27 April 2021; cultivators may increase one tier unless using the combined-use pathway (combined-use may jump more than one tier up to Tier 5 without §16-12-223(1)(e)(i)(A)–(B)); dispensary fees $5,000 plus $5,000 per additional location; combined-use $7,500 per year; from 1 January 2024 a licence is denied if the premises are within 500 feet of a DPHHS-licensed child-care facility (new applications only); preschools added to the distance rule; Board of Medical Examiners report requirement (bill title via LegiScan). Sixteen businesses licensed or pending before 27 April 2021 that were medical-only were enabled for adult-use; 29 post-cutoff applicants remained medical-only for the time being (Northern Plains Independent, May 2023, secondary). Chapter mapping Ch. 743 is inferred from "Sec. 6, Ch. 743, L. 2023" — Verify (V-11).
  • HB 229 (2023): testing laboratories, effective 1 October 2023 (DOR summary); chapter Verify (V-11).
  • HB 948 (2023), Ch. 746: created §16-12-117 and §16-12-305; Synthetic Marijuana Task Force Report due 15 September 2024.
  • Other 2023 items (Ch. 714 subjects, the tabled advertising bill number, HB 265 on 500 feet to 1,000 feet, not enacted) — carried as Run 1 Verify, not confirmed (V-11).
  • Residency challenge: D. Mont. No. 6:23-cv-00051-BMM-KLD, complaint filed 24 July 2023 (Commerce Clause challenge to §16-12-203(2)(g)); complaint PDF located; outcome not located — do not state the residency rule was struck down or upheld (V-12); §16-12-203(1)(a)(ii) still appears in the published text.
  • 2025 bills — chapters confirmed from the official Senate bill-to-chapter table (static.legmt.gov/Laws/Tables/TABLE-Senate-Bill-to-Chapter-Number-2025.pdf): SB 27 = Ch. 350 (sign date 5 May 2025 is secondary — Verify, V-13); SB 74 = Ch. 550 (subject and effective date not extracted — Verify, V-13). HB 49 Ch. 100 is carried from Run 1 (secondary: signed 7 April 2025, immediate effect; enrolled text not opened, V-13). SB 375 chapter and enrolled text Verify (V-13). HB 636 Ch. 500 (Run 1; became law 8 May 2025, chapter assigned 13 May 2025 per FastDemocracy/LegiScan secondary; effective 1 July 2026, edible serving 10 mg to 5 mg, confirmed by the DOR September 2026 newsletter and CannBus). HB 792 chapter Verify (signed 13 May 2025, chapter assigned 19 May 2025 per LegiScan secondary; substance: expanded warnings, addiction-resource QR code on exit packaging, point-of-sale posters under §16-12-215, effective 1 July 2026). HB 932 chapter Verify (signed 13 May 2025, chapter assigned 19 May 2025 per LegiScan secondary; distribution 11% HEART plus Habitat Legacy from Run 1).
  • SB 537 vetoed 6 June 2025, veto override failed 11 July 2025 — NOT law. Do not conflate it with SB 442 (2023) — a different bill, whose veto override also failed (24 senators and 41 representatives, two-thirds required); the governor's revenue quotation ("addiction and recovery services, law enforcement, veterans, wildlife habitat and state parks and trails, just as Montanans intended when they voted… in 2020") belongs to SB 442 (2023), not SB 537.
  • HB 952 vetoed 19 June 2025 — NOT law. Veto letter dated 20 June 2025 (secondary: Marijuana Moment, Filter, Ganjapreneur, Marijuana Herald, 24 June 2025): "unnecessary and duplicative", authority already exists under the State-Tribal Cooperative Agreements Act, the bill might "constrain the scope and flexibility of negotiations", would impose "unnecessary bureaucratic hurdles" and "impose State priorities on tribal nations", with sovereignty and self-determination concerns. "Over 400 agreements with Montana's eight tribal nations" (Ganjapreneur) — Verify against the veto letter itself before quoting it as a cannabis-specific fact (V-14). Sponsor Representative Frank Smith (D, Montana American Indian Caucus) (secondary). No compact statute exists.
  • SB 307 died in process 23 May 2025 — NOT law.
  • 1 July 2026: the HB 636 edible change and the HB 792 labelling and education requirements take effect.
  • MAR 2026-447.2 (recalls, ARM 42.39.312) effective 11 July 2026 (DOR newsletter; notice not opened — V-15). MAR 2026-93.2 (packaging and labelling, ARM 42.39.122, 42.39.314, 42.39.319) effective 22 August 2026: proposed as MAR 2026-93.1 (published 5 June 2026, MAR Issue No. 11), amending ARM 42.39.122, 42.39.314 and 42.39.319 to implement HB 792; authorising §16-12-112, implementing §§16-12-101, 112, 203, 207; adoption notice at revenue.mt.gov/news/rules-hearing/MAR-Notice-2026-93-2 and proposal PDF opened. MAR 2026-445.2 (seed-to-sale, ARM 42.39.203) effective 22 August 2026 (DOR newsletter; notice not opened — V-15). MAR 2026-155.2 (operational control and residency, ARM 42.39.102/105) effective 26 September 2026 (CannBus Q3 2026 secondary citing official sources: name each day-to-day control person and prove Montana residency; change-report deadline 10 days to 30 days). The adoption notice itself was not opened in this track — Verify (V-15); Part 11 opened the MAR 2026-155.2 adoption notice, and both findings are stated in the combined notes rather than overclaiming a single source.
  • Federal split scheduling effective 28 April 2026 (given baseline); no Montana DOR or DOJ guidance applying it to Montana licensees was located — Verify (V-16).
  • Cut-off 1 November 2026: the federal hemp dates 12 November and 11 December 2026 fall after the cut-off; no Montana response was located.
  • Cases cross-referred from Run 1 Part 3 (full opinions not opened — V-7): Yatsko / Great Falls (Judge Grubich, 11 October 2022); Broughton v. Butte-Silver Bow, No. 2:25-cv-00065-BMM (order 12 March 2026) — see Part 11 for the Broughton summary, with its limits stated.

9.4 I-190 official canvass and the original green count

Official Secretary of State canvass (sosmt.gov/wp-content/uploads/State_Canvass_Report.pdf): Yes 341,037 (56.90%), No 258,337 (43.10%), valid votes 599,374, invalid/blank 12,701, total votes 612,075; 663 precincts, 752,538 registered voters, 81.33% turnout.

Original green count: 28 counties voted Yes, 28 voted No. The 28 Yes counties are: Big Horn, Blaine, Carbon, Cascade, Deer Lodge, Flathead, Gallatin, Glacier, Granite, Hill, Jefferson, Lake, Lewis and Clark, Lincoln, Madison, Mineral, Missoula, Park, Powell, Ravalli, Richland, Roosevelt, Rosebud, Sanders, Sheridan, Silver Bow, Valley, Yellowstone. The 28 No counties are the remaining counties: Beaverhead, Broadwater, Carter, Chouteau, Custer, Daniels, Dawson, Fallon, Fergus, Garfield, Golden Valley, Judith Basin, Liberty, McCone, Meagher, Musselshell, Petroleum, Phillips, Pondera, Powder River, Prairie, Stillwater, Sweet Grass, Teton, Toole, Treasure, Wheatland, Wibaux. "Deer Lodge" in the canvass is Deer Lodge County (Anaconda), not the city of Deer Lodge (Powell County, itself Yes 1,632 to 1,521).

Note on the No list: the No classification follows from the 28 Yes counties above against the 56-county list in Run 1 Part 3.

9.6 Political positions, 2026 ballot, 2027 pre-files

Governor Gianforte: HB 952 veto (see §9.3); HB 701 signing statement (secondary, Marijuana Moment, 5 June 2021): "Since January, we've been focused on implementing the will of Montana voters in a safe, responsible, and appropriately regulated manner. House Bill 701 accomplishes this." The SB 442 (2023) revenue statement must not be misattributed to SB 537 (see §9.3). Legislative leadership: no position statement was located in official records — do not infer one from sponsorship, votes or veto outcomes. EAIC: no position statement was located; its statutory role is oversight and legislation development only.

Fort Belknap Indian Community position on HB 952 (tribal source): President Jeffrey Stiffarm's letter of 5 May 2025 urging a veto — the bill was drafted and advanced without meaningful consultation, conditioned tribal authority on state approval, mandated state control over licensing and revenue sharing, treated tribes as subordinate, and lacked free, prior and informed consent; the Community is not opposed to collaboration and favours voluntary government-to-government compacts with sovereignty respected (billtexts S3, documentId-325402, HB-952). Attribute this as a tribal position.

2026 ballot (Secretary of State, sosmt.gov/elections/ballot_issues/proposed-2026-ballot-issues/): Ballot Issue #12 did not qualify — a constitutional initiative that would have allowed adult use of medical marijuana while prohibiting marijuana for non-medical purposes, Submission #1 on 27 March 2026, Secretary of State status "Forwarded to LSD on 27 Mar 2026", submitter Tyler Newcombe. Thresholds: a statutory initiative or referendum requires 5% of qualified voters (based on votes for governor at the last general election), including 5% in each of 34 house districts; a constitutional initiative requires 10%, including 10% in each of 40 house districts. Qualified for the 2026 general election (three, none cannabis): CI-132 (Issue #5, judicial elections remain nonpartisan, qualified 17 August 2026, submitters Monica Paoli and Megan Simpson), CI-133, and I-194 (per the 2026 Voter Information Pamphlet table of contents). Election Day 3 November 2026 (pamphlet excerpts give "November 3" without the year in some lines — confirm on the pamphlet cover, V-18).

2027 pre-files: no 2027 marijuana or hemp bill draft was confirmed — state "none located" (the bills.legmt.gov 2027-session search was not completed — V-29). The only adjacent draft found was LC0661.1, 70th Legislature 2027, by request of EAIC — kratom alkaloids (mitragynine and hydroxymitragynine) as controlled substances, amending §50-32-222 — NOT a marijuana or hemp bill (relevant only because §50-32-222 also lists tetrahydrocannabinols). HB 2 (2025 General Appropriations) language conditioning CCD funding ($514,870 in FY2026, $503,434 in FY2027, 4.00 PB) on a bill extending the new-licensee date to 1 July 2027 or later under §16-12-201 is 2025 appropriations intent language, not a 2027 pre-file.

PART 10: TRIBAL, FEDERAL LAND, TRANSPORT, BORDER

Part 10 sources accessed 1 November 2026. Tribal hemp entries are updated by Part 7 §6, which opened the full USDA table: the two-entry excerpt in the original Part 9–10 track (Fort Peck and CSKT only) is superseded — see §10.3. Nothing in Part 10 states a rule for North Dakota, South Dakota or Canada, which were not researched (V-28).

10.1 Tribes

Eight nations, 12 distinct tribes, 7 reservations plus the landless Little Shell Tribe: (1) Assiniboine and Sioux Tribes of the Fort Peck Indian Reservation; (2) Blackfeet Tribe (Blackfeet Reservation, Browning); (3) Chippewa-Cree Indians of the Rocky Boy's Reservation; (4) Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes of the Flathead Reservation (CSKT, Pablo; Salish, Pend d'Oreille, Kootenai); (5) Crow Tribe of Montana (Apsáalooke); (6) Fort Belknap Indian Community (Gros Ventre / A'aninin and Assiniboine); (7) Little Shell Tribe of Chippewa Indians — federally recognised under the Little Shell Restoration Act enacted 20 December 2019, landless; headquarters in Great Falls, Montana (tribal-government press release); older "state recognised only" pages are superseded and must not be used; (8) Northern Cheyenne Tribe. Sources: BLM Pompeys Pillar page; Montana Office of Public Instruction tribal-governments document; Montana PBS tribal territories page; Montana State Hemp Plan. A national federally recognised total (574 or 575, secondary) was not used without a Federal Register check (V-19).

10.2 Combined-use licences — current law

Current §16-12-225, verbatim (official MCA 2025, mca.legmt.gov — §16-12-225):

"(1) The department may issue a total of eight combined-use marijuana licenses to entities that are: (a) a federally recognized tribe located in the state; or (b) a business entity that is majority-owned by a federally recognized tribe located in the state. (2) A combined-use marijuana license consists of one cultivator license and one dispensary license allowing for the operation of a dispensary. (3) Initial licensure and annual fees for a combined-use license is $7,500. (4) A combined-use marijuana licensee shall operate its cultivation and dispensary facilities on land that is located in a county that has satisfied the local government approval provisions in 16-12-301 if the majority of voters in the county voted against approval of Initiative Measure No. 190 in the November 3, 2020, general election."

History: En. Sec. 6, Ch. 576, L. 2021; amd. Sec. 21, Ch. 712, L. 2023; amd. Sec. 6, Ch. 743, L. 2023 (last amended 2023). Subsections (5) and (7) via LawServer mirror only: (5) an applicant must satisfy all Chapter 12 requirements and is subject to all fees and taxes; (7) after a tribe or majority-owned business is licensed, that tribe or another of its majority-owned businesses may not obtain another licence until the prior licence is relinquished, lapses or is revoked. §16-12-225(1)–(7) are resolved on the official MCA 2025 page: (5) applicant must satisfy all Chapter 12 requirements and is subject to all fees and taxes; (6) licence must be operated in compliance with Chapter 12; (7) one-licence-per-tribe bar until the prior licence is relinquished, lapses or is revoked. ARM 42.39.415 full text remains Verify (V-20, partly resolved).

Correction to Run 1 and to the CCD FAQ: current §16-12-225(2) reads "one cultivator license and one dispensary license" without "tier 1" and without "same premises"; §16-12-225(4) requires only the §16-12-301 county condition; there is no 150-air-mile rule in the current text; the CCD FAQ formulation (tier-1 plus same premises plus green county plus within 150 air miles) is stale — do not quote it as current law. The DOR 2023 summary states HB 128 removed the share-location and proximity limits. Secondary HB 952 coverage saying "at least 150 miles outside" is a wording error in the opposite direction.

The CCD combined-use page (current, consistent with the statute) states: maximum 8, one per tribe, cultivation plus dispensary, 20% processing fee, $7,500, not subject to the moratorium, applications available now via TAP, must demonstrate a §16-12-301 jurisdiction; a combined-use cultivator may increase more than one tier at a time up to Tier 5 without §16-12-223(1)(e)(i)(A)–(B).

Uptake: CannBus Q3 2026 reports combined-use licensed 0 in August 2024. No licensee was named in the Run 1 lists or the Part 9–10 searches. Safe statement: "0 in August 2024 biennial figures; no licensee identified in lists retrieved 1 November 2026." A live CCD list check is required before stating a 2026 figure (V-21). Do not state "0 in 2026" as confirmed.

10.3 Tribal ordinances, compacts and hemp
